Der Naturwissenschaftlichen Fakultät

der Friedrich-Alexander-Universität

Erlangen-Nürnberg

zur

Erlangung des Doktorgrades Dr. rer. nat.

vorgelegt von

Jonas Schober

Als Dissertation genehmigt von der Naturwissenschaftlichen Fakultät der Friedrich-Alexander-Universität Erlangen-Nürnberg.

Tag der mündlichen Prüfung: 2. August 2023

Gutachter: Prof. Dr. Karl-Hermann Neeb

Prof. Dr. Gandalf Lechner

Prof. Dr. Yoh Tanimoto

Zusammenfassung

Standard-Unterräume sind viel studierte Objekte in der algebraischen Quantenfeldtheorie (AQFT). Gegeben einen Standard-Unterraum
{\tt V}
in einem Hilbertraum
\mathcal{H}
, ist man interessiert an unitären Einparameter-Gruppen auf
\mathcal{H}
für welche
U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}
für alle
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
gilt. Falls
({\tt V},U)
ein nicht-degeneriertes Standard-Paar auf
\mathcal{H}
ist, d.h. der selbstadjungierte infinitesimale Erzeuger von
U
ist ein positiver Operator mit trivialem Kern, haben wir zwei klassische Resultate mit dem Satz von Borchers, welcher nicht-degenerierte Standard-Paare mit Darstellungen der affinen Gruppe
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
mit positiver Energie in Verbindung setzt und dem Satz von Longo–Witten, laut welchem die Halbgruppe der unitären Endomorphismen von
{\tt V}
mit der Halbgruppe der symmetrischen operatorwertigen inneren Funktionen auf der oberen Halbebene identifiziert werden kann.

In dieser Arbeit beweisen wir Sätze ähnlich denen von Borchers und Longo–Witten auch für allgemeinere Fälle von unitären Einparameter-Gruppen ohne die Annahme, dass deren infinitesimaler Erzeuger positiv ist. Wir wollen diese Annahme durch die schwächere Annahme ersetzen, dass das Tripel
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
eine sogenannte reelle reguläre Einparameter-Gruppe ist.

Nach Bereitstellung der grundlegenden Theorie von regulären Einparameter-Gruppen, werden wir reguläre Einparameter-Gruppen untersuchen, die infinitesimal erzeugt werden von operatorwertigen Pick-Funktionen. Wir werden Kriterien bereitstellen, um zu prüfen, wann eine Pick-Funktion eine reguläre Einparameter-Gruppe generiert und in diesem Fall werden wir Formeln für den Multiplizitätenraum der regulären Einparameter-Gruppe bereitstellen und zeigen, dass die Dimension des Multiplizitätenraumes mit der Komposition von Pick-Funktionen verträglich ist.

Wir werden auch reflexionspositive reguläre Einparameter-Gruppen untersuchen und eine Normalform für diese angeben. Wir tun dies, indem wir sie in Verbindung setzen mit reflexionspositiven Hilberträumen der Form
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\theta_{h})
mit einem komplexen Hilbertraum
\mathcal{K}
und einer Funktion
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))
, wobei die Involution
\theta_{h}
auf
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
durch
{(\theta_{h}f)(x)=h(x)\cdot f(-x)}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, gegeben ist. In dem multiplizitätsfreien Fall
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
geben wir eine vollständige Klassifikation aller Funktionen
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})}
, für welche das Tripel
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
ein maximaler reflexionspositiver Hilbertraum ist. Außerdem geben wir eine explizite Beschreibung der Osterwalder–Schrader–Transformation dieser reflexionspositiven Hilberträume.

Wir werden diese Resultate anwenden, um zu beweisen, dass jeder positive kontraktive Hankel-Operator auf
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
zu einer Involution
\theta_{h}
auf
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
erweitert werden kann, für welche das Tripel
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
ein maximaler reflexionspositiver Hilbertraum ist.

Abschließend wenden wir unsere Resultate über (reflexionspositive) reguläre Einparameter-Gruppen auf den Kontext von Standard-Unterräumen an und benutzen sie, um Analoga für den Satz von Borchers und den Satz von Longo–Witten auch für solche Paare
({\tt V},U)
zu beweisen, für welche das Tripel
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
eine reelle reguläre Einparameter-Gruppe ist bzw. für welche das Quadrupel
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
eine reelle reflexionspositive reguläre Einparameter-Gruppe ist.

Abstract

Standard subspaces are a well-studied object in algebraic quantum field theory (AQFT). Given a standard subspace
{\tt V}
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, one is interested in unitary one-parameter groups on
\mathcal{H}
with
U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}
for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. If
({\tt V},U)
is a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
, i.e. the self-adjoint infinitesimal generator of
U
is a positive operator with trivial kernel, two classical results are given by Borchers’ Theorem, relating non-degenerate standard pairs to positive energy representations of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
and the Longo–Witten Theorem, stating that the semigroup of unitary endomorphisms of
{\tt V}
can be identified with the semigroup of symmetric operator-valued inner functions on the upper half-plane.

In this thesis, we prove results similar to the theorems of Borchers and of Longo–Witten for a more general framework of unitary one-parameter groups without the assumption that their infinitesimal generator is positive. We replace this assumption by the weaker assumption that the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is a so-called real regular one-parameter group.

After providing some basic theory for regular one-parameter groups, we investigate regular one-parameter groups that are infinitesimally generated by operator-valued Pick functions. We provide criteria for a Pick function to generate a regular one-parameter group, and in this case, we provide a formula for the multiplicity space of the regular one-parameter group and show that the dimension of the multiplicity space is compatible with composition of Pick functions.

We also investigate reflection positive regular one-parameter groups and provide a normal form for them. We do this by linking them to reflection positive Hilbert spaces of the form
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\theta_{h})
with some complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and some function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))
, where the involution
\theta_{h}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
is given by
{(\theta_{h}f)(x)=h(x)\cdot f(-x)}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In the multiplicity free case
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
we give a full classification of all functions
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})}
for which the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space. Also, we give an explicit description of the Osterwalder–Schrader transform of these reflection positive Hilbert spaces.

We apply these results to prove that every positive contractive Hankel operator on
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
can be extended to an involution
\theta_{h}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
for which the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space.

Finally, we apply our results about (reflection positive) regular one-parameter groups in the context of standard subspaces and use them to provide analogs of Borchers’ Theorem and the Longo–Witten Theorem to pairs
({\tt V},U)
for which the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is a real regular one-parameter group or for which the quadruple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
is a real reflection positive regular one-parameter group respectively.

Notation

Sets

•

We set
??\coloneqq\left(0,\infty\right)
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\coloneqq\left[0,\infty\right)
and define
{\mathbb{R}}_{-}\coloneqq-{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\coloneqq-{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.

•

We set
{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\coloneqq{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\coloneqq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
.

•

We define the complex half-planes

??\coloneqq\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Im}\left(z\right)>0\right\},\quad{\mathbb{C}}_{r}\coloneqq\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Re}\left(z\right)>0\right\}

and

{\mathbb{C}}_{-}\coloneqq-{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\quad{\mathbb{C}}_{l}\coloneqq-{\mathbb{C}}_{r}.

•

We set

??\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|=1\}\quad\text{and}\quad??\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|<1\}.

•

By
??
we denote the Riemann sphere.

•

For any open subset
U\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}
we denote by
??\left(U\right)
the set of holomorphic functions on
U
.

•

For a set
X
and an involution
\theta:X\to X
, we define

X^{\theta}\coloneqq\{x\in X:\theta x=x\}.

•

For an algebra
\mathcal{A}
and a subset
E\subseteq\mathcal{A}
we define the
commutant

??
by

E^{\prime}\coloneqq\{a\in\mathcal{A}:(\forall b\in E)\,ab=ba\}.

Hilbert spaces

•

We denote scalar products in a Hilbert space by
??
, being linear in the second argument.

•

If not mentioned otherwise, we assume that all occuring Hilbert spaces are separable.

•

For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
we denote by
??
the set of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
, by
??
the set of self-adjoint linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
and by
S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
the set of positive linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
.

•

For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a closed subspace
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
we denote by
??\in B(\mathcal{H})
the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{K}
and by
??:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{K}
the partial isometry with

p_{\mathcal{K}}\big|_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{K}}\quad\text{and}\quad p_{\mathcal{K}}\big|_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}=0.

•

For a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a one-parameter (semi)group
U:{\mathbb{R}}_{(\geq 0)}\to B(\mathcal{H})
, we write
??
for the unbounded operator und
\mathcal{H}
defined on

\mathcal{D}(\partial U)\coloneqq\left\{v\in\mathcal{H}:\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{it}(U_{t}-\textbf{1})v\text{\,\,exists}\right\}

by

\partial Uv=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{it}(U_{t}-\textbf{1})v

(cf.
[
Ne17
, Def. 5.8]
).

??
-spaces

•

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
by
\mathcal{L}^{p}\left(\Omega,\mathbb{K},\mu\right)
we denote the vector space of
p
-integrable
functions with values in the field
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
on the measurable space
\Omega
with respect to the measure
\mu
. Further, we write
L^{p}(\Omega,{\mathbb{K}},\mu)
for the corresponding Banach space of equivalence classes of such functions with respect to the equivalence relation given by

f\sim g\qquad:\Leftrightarrow\qquad\left(\exists E\subseteq\Omega\right):\left(\mu(E)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\forall x\in\Omega\setminus E:f(x)=g(x)\right).

•

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote the Lebesgue-measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
by
??
.

•

For
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
we set

L^{p}(\Omega,{\mathbb{K}})\coloneqq L^{p}\left(\Omega,{\mathbb{K}},\lambda_{n}\big|_{\Omega}\right).

•

For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
over a field
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
, we define the Hilbert space

L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{H},\mu)\coloneqq L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{K},\mu)\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H}.

Interpreting
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{H},\mu)
as
\mathcal{H}
-valued, almost everywhere defined functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
with

(f\otimes v)(x)=f(x)\cdot v,\quad f\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{K},\mu),v\in\mathcal{H},x\in{\mathbb{R}},

the scalar product on
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{H},\mu)
is given by

\left\langle f\middle|g\right\rangle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\left\langle f(x)\middle|g(x)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,dx.

•

For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
over a field
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
and
g\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{K},\mu)
, we define the multiplication operator
??\in B(L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{H},\mu))
by

(M_{g}f)(x)=g(x)\cdot f(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

•

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space over a field
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
and let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. We call a function
f:\Omega\to B(\mathcal{H})

weak-
*
-measurable
if, for every trace-class operator
T\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, the function

f_{T}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{K}},\quad x\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(Tf(x))

is measurable. We write
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\Omega,B(\mathcal{H}),\mu)
for the set of bounded weak-
*
-measurable functions
f:\Omega\to B(\mathcal{H})
. Further, we write
L^{\infty}(\Omega,B(\mathcal{H}),\mu)
for the corresponding Banach space of equivalence classes of such functions with respect to the equivalence relation given by

f\sim g\qquad:\Leftrightarrow\qquad\left(\exists E\subseteq\Omega\right):\left(\mu(E)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\forall x\in\Omega\setminus E:f(x)=g(x)\right).

For
g\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,B(\mathcal{H}),\mu)
we define the multiplication operator
M_{g}\in B(L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{H},\mu))
by

(M_{g}f)(x)=g(x)\cdot f(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Complexification

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space.

•

We denote the
complexification
of
\mathcal{H}
by

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\coloneqq{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}.

•

By
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
we denote the complex conjugation

??:\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}},\quad z\otimes v\mapsto\overline{z}\otimes v.

•

We will identify
\mathcal{H}
with the real subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}}={\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}
. Under this identification, we have
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathcal{H}+i\mathcal{H}
.

•

For an operator
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
, by
??\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
, we denote the operator with

A_{\mathbb{C}}(x+iy)=Ax+iAy\quad\forall x,y\in\mathcal{H}.

Tensor products

•

For two Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
we define the
tensor product Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}\,\widehat{??}\,\mathcal{K}

as the completion of the vector space
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}
with respect to the sesquilinear map on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}
defined by

\left\langle a\otimes b\middle|v\otimes w\right\rangle\coloneqq\left\langle a\middle|v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\left\langle b\middle|w\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}},

where
\left\langle\cdot\middle|\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the scalar product on
\mathcal{H}
and
\left\langle\cdot\middle|\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}
the scalar product on
\mathcal{K}
respectively (cf.
[
Sa71
, Sec. 1.20]
,
[
Ne17
, Def. 4.1]
).

•

For two Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
we consider the inclusion

\iota:B(\mathcal{H})\otimes B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K})

with

\iota(A\otimes B)(v\otimes w)=(Av)\otimes(Bw)

(cf.
[
Sa71
, Sec. 1.20]
,
[
Ne17
, Lem. 4.3]
). Now, given two von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{M}\subseteq B(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{N}\subseteq B(\mathcal{K})
, we define their tensor product by

\mathcal{M}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{N}\coloneqq\iota(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N})^{\prime\prime}.

1
Introduction

In algebraic quantum field theory (AQFT), objects of current interest are standard subspaces, i.e. real subspaces
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
of a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
that satisfy

{\tt V}\cap i{\tt V}=\{0\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{{\tt V}+i{\tt V}}=\mathcal{H}

(cf.
[
Ne21
]
,
[
MN21
]
,
[
NÓ21
]
,
[
NØÓ21
]
). Given a standard subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, one has the densely defined Tomita operator

T_{\tt V}:{\tt V}+i{\tt V}\to\mathcal{H},\quad x+iy\mapsto x-iy

as well as the anti-unitary involution
J_{\tt V}
and the positive densely defined operator
\Delta_{\tt V}
that one obtains by the polar decomposition

T_{\tt V}=J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}

of the Tomita operator. This pair
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is referred to as the pair of modular objects of
{\tt V}
. In the context of standard subspaces, one often studies unitary one-parameter groups on
\mathcal{H}
whose semigroup in positive direction consists of unitary operators that map the standard subspace
{\tt V}
into itself, i.e. unitary one-parameter groups
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
with

U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

If the self-adjoint infinitesimal generator

\partial U\coloneqq\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{it}(U_{t}-\textbf{1})

of such a unitary one-parameter group
U
additionally is a positive operator with trivial kernel, i.e.

\partial U\geq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ker(\partial U)=\{0\},

one calls
({\tt V},U)
a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Two classical results about these non-degenerate standard pairs are given by Borchers’ Theorem (cf. Theorem
6.32
,
[
Bo92
, Thm. II.9]
,
[
Lo08
, Thm. 2.2.1]
) and the Longo–Witten Theorem (cf.
[
LW11
, Thm. 2.6]
). The theorem by Borchers states that, given a standard pair
({\tt V},U)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, one can, using
U
and the modular data
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
, construct a so-called strictly positive energy representation (cf. Definition
6.24
) of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. By the representation theory of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
, one then knows that there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a unitary operator

\psi:\mathcal{H}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}

with

\psi({\tt V})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi\circ U_{t}\circ\psi^{-1}=S_{t}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Here
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
denotes the Hilbert space of square-integrable
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-valued functions one
{\mathbb{R}}
that are invariant under the involution
\sharp
defined by

f^{\sharp}(x)=\overline{f(-x)},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

and by
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
we denote the corresponding Hardy space, i.e. the subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
whose Fourier transform vanishes on the negative half-line. Further,
S_{t}
denotes the unitary operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
defined by

(S_{t}f)(x)\coloneqq e^{itx}f(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Under the identification

\mathcal{H}\cong L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\tt V}\cong H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}

the theorem by Longo and Witten then states that the semigroup

\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)\coloneqq\{A\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}):A{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V},\,(\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}})\,U_{t}A=AU_{t}\}

is precisely given by the semigroup
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp}
of symmetric inner functions on the upper half-plane (cf. Definition
A.31
).

Since also unitary representations with non-positive infinitesimal generator are a subject of investigation in AQFT (cf.
[
Ni22
]
), in this thesis, we want to provide theorems similar to the ones of Borchers and of Longo–Witten for a more general framework of unitary one-parameter groups
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
with

U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

without the additional assumption that the infinitesimal generator of
U
is positive. We want to replace this assumption by the weaker assumption that – as it was the case for non-degenerate standard pairs – there exists an isomorphism between
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. By the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
,
[
LP64
, Thm. 1]
), this condition is equivalent to
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
being a so-called real regular one-parameter group.

In Chapter 2, we will introduce regular one-parameter groups, i.e. triples
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
of a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
, a closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
and a unitary one-parameter group
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
that satisfy

U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}\quad\forall t\geq 0,\qquad\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=\{0\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}=\mathcal{E}.

By the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
, Theorem
2.16
) these regular one-parameter groups are always unitarily equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
if
\mathcal{E}
is a real Hilbert space or to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
for some complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
if
\mathcal{E}
is a complex Hilbert space. In this chapter we show that the so obtained multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism (cf. Theorem
2.25
, Theorem
2.26
) and that – up to unitary equivalence – the space
\mathcal{E}_{+}
is unique, i.e. if
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
are both regular one-parameter groups, then

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)\cong(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)

via some isomorphism of regular one-parameter groups (cf. Theorem
2.36
).

In Chapter 3, we study the semigroup
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
of inner functions appearing in the context of the Longo–Witten Theorem. One-parameter semigroups in
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
are infinitesimally generated by operator-valued Pick functions (cf. Definition
A.41
), i.e. they are of the form

U^{F}_{t}\coloneqq e^{itF},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

for some Pick function
F
with values in
B(\mathcal{H})
. For
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}<\infty
we give criteria on the function
F
that are equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
being a regular one-parameter group (cf. Theorem
3.17
, Theorem
3.110
). In these cases, we are also interested in calculating the multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}_{F}
of the regular one-parameter group
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
. A formula for this multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}_{F}
is provided in Theorem
3.327
. An interesting open problem in this context is whether similar results also hold in the case of an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

Finally, we will prove that the dimension of the multiplicity space is compatible with composition of Pick functions: Given Pick functions
f
and
g
with values in
{\mathbb{C}}
and a Pick function
F
with values in
B(\mathcal{H})
such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{f})
,
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{g})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
are regular one-parameter groups, the function
f\circ F\circ g
is also a Pick function such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{f\circ F\circ g})
is a regular one-parameter group and for its multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}_{f\circ F\circ g}
one has

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{f\circ F\circ g}=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{f}\cdot\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{F}\cdot\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{g}

(cf. Corollary
3.19
, Theorem
3.43
).

In Chapter 4, we add another structure to regular one-parameter groups that corresponds to the involution
J_{\tt V}
in the context of standard subspaces. Concretely, we consider so-called reflection positive regular one-parameter groups. These are quadruples
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
, where
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a regular one-parameter group and
\theta
is an involution on
\mathcal{E}
with

\theta U_{t}=U_{-t}\theta\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a reflection positive Hilbert space, i.e.

\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle\geq 0\qquad\forall\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}

(cf. Definition
4.11
). This will lead us to the question for which functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\theta_{h})
is a reflection positive Hilbert space, where
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
denotes the set of essentially bounded measurable functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
with values in
\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K})
that are invariant under the involution
\flat
defined by

f^{\flat}(x)\coloneqq f(-x)^{*},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

and by
\theta_{h}
we denote the involution on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
defined by

(\theta_{h}f)(x)=h(x)\cdot f(-x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

This problem has already been described in
[
NÓ18
, Prob. 4.4.5]
and is in full generality still open and should be subject of further investigation. However we were able to classify all such functions
h
under the two additional assumptions that
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
and that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space. The maximality here is to be understood in the way that, for every subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\supseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
for which
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}),\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta_{h})
is a reflection positive Hilbert space, one has
\mathcal{E}_{+}=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
(cf. Definition
4.112
). A full characterization of the functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space is given in Theorem
4.329
.

Finally, given a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space, in Corollary
4.53
we give an explicit description of the Osterwalder–Schrader transform (cf.
[
NÓ18
, Def. 3.1.1]
, Definition
4.51
) of this reflection positive Hilbert space. Here, given a reflection positive Hilbert space
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
and defining

\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\coloneqq\{\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{+}:\left\langle\eta\middle|\theta\eta\right\rangle=0\},

the Osterwalder–Schrader transform
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}
of
\mathcal{E}_{+}
is defined as the Hilbert space completion of
\mathcal{E}_{+}/\penalty\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
with respect to the scalar product

\left\langle\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\middle|\xi+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}\coloneqq\left\langle\eta\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle,\quad\eta,\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}.

In Chapter 5, we apply our results from Chapter 4 to Hankel operators, i.e. bounded linear operators
{H\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))}
that satisfy

S_{t}^{*}H=HS_{t}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

(cf. Definition
4.324
), where

(S_{t}f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x),\quad f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),x\in{\mathbb{R}},t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

We will see that, given a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space, denoting by
p_{+}
the partial isometry from
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
onto its subspace
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, the operator

H_{h}\coloneqq p_{+}\theta_{h}p_{+}^{*}

is a positive Hankel operator with
{\left\lVert H_{h}\right\rVert\leq 1}
. The question we investigate in Chapter 5, which has already been under investigation in
[
ANS22
]
, is if every positive Hankel operator
H
with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
is of this form. In
[
ANS22
, Sec. 4]
it has already been shown that this is true if one allows to change the scalar product on
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. We prove that in fact, the statement is true without changing the scalar product (cf. Theorem
5.35
).

In Chapter 6 we apply the results of the previous chapters in the context of standard subspaces. Given a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a standard subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, and a strongly continuous one-parameter group
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
, we consider regular pairs, i.e. pairs
({\tt V},U)
, for which
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is a real regular one-parameter group and reflection positive regular pairs, i.e. pairs
({\tt V},U)
, for which
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
is a real reflection positive regular one-parameter group (cf. Definition
6.35
). First, we prove that both of these conditions are fulfilled if
({\tt V},U)
is a non-degenerate standard pair, to show that we are, in fact, dealing with a generalization of non-degenerate standard pairs (cf. Corollary
6.38
). We will also show that in both cases, the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is a maximal reflection positive Hilbert space (RPHS) (cf. Proposition
6.37
).

By the Lax–Phillips Theorem we know that, for every regular pair
({\tt V},U)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, the regular one-parameter group
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. Here the multiplicity free case
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
corresponds to the representation
U
being
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic, i.e.

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}U({\mathbb{R}})v}=\mathcal{H}

(cf. Proposition
6.310
).

In Theorem
6.43
, we show that, for all regular pairs
({\tt V},U)
, identifying

\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\qquad\text{and}\quad{\tt V}\cong{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},

the semigroup

\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)\coloneqq\{A\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}):A{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V},\,(\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}})\,U_{t}A=AU_{t}\}

is given by a subsemigroup of the semigroup
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp}
of symmetric inner functions and thereby provide a generalization of the Longo–Witten Theorem (cf.
[
LW11
, Thm. 2.6]
). In this theorem, we also provide some conditions under which semigroups in
\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)
extend to regular one-parameter groups by using the results from Chapter 3.

Many of our main results are summarized in the following table:

2
Regular one-parameter groups

In this chapter, we will introduce the concept of a regular one-parameter group (ROPG). Using the Lax–Phillips Theorem, we will introduce a standard form for these ROPGs and later, we will provide some basic theory about ROPGs.

2.1
ROPGs and the Lax–Phillips Theorem

Definition 2.11
.

Consider a triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
of a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
, a closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
and a unitary one-parameter group
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
.

(a)

We call
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
a
semi-regular one-parameter group (??)
, if

U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}\quad\forall t\geq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=\{0\}.

(b)

We define the unitary one-parameter group
\check{U}:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
by
\check{U}_{t}=U_{-t}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(c)

We call
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
a
regular one-parameter group (??)
, if
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp},\check{U})
are both a SROPG.

(d)

We call a (S)ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
real/complex, if
\mathcal{E}
is real/complex.

(e)

We say that two ROPGs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U^{\prime})
are equivalent, if there exists a unitary operator
\psi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that

\psi(\mathcal{E}_{+})=\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\circ U_{t}=U_{t}^{\prime}\circ\psi\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We call such an operator
\psi
an
isomorphism between
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U^{\prime})
.

Remark 2.12
.

For
t\geq 0
one has
U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}
, if and only if

\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}\subseteq(U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+})^{\perp}=U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}=\check{U}_{-t}\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp},

which is equivalent to
\check{U}_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}
. Further

\left(\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\check{U}_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}\right)^{\perp}=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\check{U}_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}\right)^{\perp}}=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\check{U}_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}.

Therefore
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a ROPG, if and only if

U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}\quad\forall t\geq 0

and

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=\{0\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}=\mathcal{E}.

The following theorem gives us a normal form for ROPGs:

Theorem 2.13
.

(Lax–Phillips)
(cf.
[
LP64
, Thm. 1]
[
NÓ18
, Thm. 4.4.1]
,
[
Fr20
, Thm. 3.3]
)

For every real/complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
, the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\tau)
is a real/complex ROPG, where the one-parameter group
??:{\mathbb{R}}\to U(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}))
is the translation action defined by

(\tau_{t}f)(p)=f(p-t),\qquad t,p\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Further, every real/complex ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\tau)
for some real/complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 2.14
.

In the notation used in the theorem we identify
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},\mathcal{K})
with the subspace

\left\{f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}):f\big|_{(-\infty,0]}\equiv 0\right\}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}).

The version of the Lax–Phillips Theorem that we will use in the following is slightly different, since we will change to the Fourier transformed picture. For this, we need some definitions:

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a real Hilbert space and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the complex conjugation on
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(a)

In the following we identify
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
with the subspace

\left\{f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}):\left(\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}\right)\,f(x)\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}}\right\}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}).

(b)

By
\sharp:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
, we denote the involution defined by

??(x)=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}f(-x).

(c)

For a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
we consider the Fourier transform
??:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
given by

(\mathcal{F}f)(p)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ipx}f(x)\,dx

for every
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

To change to the Fourier-transformed picture, we first notice that

(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\tau_{t}f)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ipx}f(p-t)\,dp=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(p+t)x}f(x)\,dp=e^{itx}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}f)(x).

Further, for a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
, we have

(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}f)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ipx}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}f(p)\,dp=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ipx}f(p)\,dp=(\mathcal{F}^{-1}f)^{\sharp}(x),

so

\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}.

Further, for a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, by
[
RR94
, Thm. 5.28]
, we have

\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),

where
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
denotes the
\mathcal{H}
-valued Hardy space (cf. Section
A.1
).

With this information, one immediately gets a version of the Lax–Phillips Theorem in the Fourier transformed picture:

Theorem 2.16
.

(Lax–Phillips, complex Fourier version)

For every complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
with the one-parameter group

??:{\mathbb{R}}\to U(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})),\quad(S_{t}f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x)

is a complex ROPG and every complex ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
for some complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
.

Theorem 2.17
.

(Lax–Phillips, real Fourier version)

For every real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
with the one-parameter group

S:{\mathbb{R}}\to U(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}),\quad(S_{t}f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x)

is a real ROPG and every real ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
.

The one-parameter group
S
appearing in the last two theorems will appear often in the following sections. Many times, an object of interest will be its commutant
S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}
. To compute this commutant, we need the following theorem:

Theorem 2.18
.

(cf.
[
RD75
, Thm. 1]
)

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces and let
M\subseteq B(\mathcal{H})
and
N\subseteq B(\mathcal{K})
be von Neumann algebras. Then

(M\,\overline{\otimes}\,N)^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\,\overline{\otimes}\,N^{\prime}.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
S:{\mathbb{R}}\to U(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}))
be the one-parameter group defined by

(S_{t}f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x).

Then

S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}=M\left(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))\right).

Proof.

Considering the functions
e_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with
e_{t}(x)=e^{itx}
and identifying

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})\cong L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K},

we have that

S_{t}=M_{e_{t}}\otimes\textbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then, by Proposition
A.115
, the set
S({\mathbb{R}})
spans a weakly dense subspace in
M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\,\overline{\otimes}\,{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
. This, by Theorem
2.18
, yields

\displaystyle S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\,\overline{\otimes}\,{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}}\right)^{\prime}=M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))^{\prime}\,\overline{\otimes}\,({\mathbb{C}}\textbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}})^{\prime}=M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\,\overline{\otimes}\,B(\mathcal{K}),

using
[
Sa71
, Cor. 2.9.3]
in the last step. The statement now follows since, by
[
Sa71
, Thm. 1.22.13]
, we have

M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\,\overline{\otimes}\,B(\mathcal{K})=M\left(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))\right).\qed

2.2
Formulas for the multiplicity space

In this section, given a ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
, we will provide some ways to calculate the space
\mathcal{K}
appearing in the Lax–Phillips Theorem, thereby showing that this space
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism.

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
be a complex ROPG and
\mathcal{K}
a complex Hilbert space such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
. Then

B(\mathcal{K})\cong(U({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}\})^{\prime}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)=(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
. Let
A\in(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})}\})^{\prime}
. Then
A\in S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}
, by Proposition
2.19
, implies that
A=M_{f}
with
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))
. Further,
{A\in\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})}\}^{\prime}}
implies

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})\quad\text{and}\quad M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}),

which, by Proposition
A.113
, yields

f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}))\quad\text{and}\quad f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{K})),

so
f=C\cdot\textbf{1}
for some
C\in B(\mathcal{K})
by Corollary
A.112
.

Conversely, for every
C\in B(\mathcal{K})
one has
M_{C\cdot\textbf{1}}\in(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})}\})^{\prime}
, so

(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})}\})^{\prime}=B(\mathcal{K})\cdot M_{\textbf{1}}.\qed

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
be a real ROPG and
\mathcal{K}
a real Hilbert space such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
. Then

B(\mathcal{K})^{2}\cong(U({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}\})^{\prime}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)=(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
. Let
A\in(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}}\})^{\prime}
. Then
A\in S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}
, by Proposition
2.19
, implies that
A=M_{f}
with
{f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))}
. Further,
{A\in\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}}\}^{\prime}}
implies

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\quad\text{and}\quad M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}.

Since
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}+iH^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
, this implies

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\quad\text{and}\quad M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),

which, by Proposition
A.113
and Corollary
A.112
, yields
f=C\cdot\textbf{1}
for some
C\in B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
. Further,
AL^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
implies that
[\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}},C]=0
, so
C\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\}^{\prime}
. Conversely, for every
C\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\}^{\prime}
one has
M_{C\cdot\textbf{1}}\in(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}}\})^{\prime}
, so

(S({\mathbb{R}})\cup\{P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}}\})^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\}^{\prime}\cdot M_{\textbf{1}}.

The statement then follows by the fact that

\Phi:B(\mathcal{K})^{2}\to\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\}^{\prime},\quad\Phi(A,B)(x+iy)=Ax+iBy,\quad A,B\in B(\mathcal{K}),\quad x,y\in\mathcal{K}

defines an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a real Hilbert space and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then

\left(Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=\begin{cases}Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}&\text{if }\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
\{0\}&\text{if }\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus i{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\end{cases}

with
Q_{\lambda}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
defined by

Q_{\lambda}(z)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{z-\overline{\lambda}}

(cf. Proposition
A.17
).

Proof.

For
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
, one has

\displaystyle(Q_{\lambda}\cdot v)^{\sharp}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}(Q_{\lambda}(-\overline{z})\cdot v)=\overline{Q_{\lambda}(-\overline{z})}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v

\displaystyle=\overline{\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{-\overline{z}-\overline{\lambda}}}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{z+\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v=Q_{-\overline{\lambda}}(z)\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v.

Therefore
(Q_{\lambda}\cdot v)^{\sharp}=Q_{\lambda}\cdot v
, if and only if

Q_{-\overline{\lambda}}(z)\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v=Q_{\lambda}(z)\cdot v\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

For
v\neq 0
this implies that the map

{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\ni z\mapsto\frac{Q_{\lambda}(z)}{Q_{-\overline{\lambda}}(z)}=\frac{z+\lambda}{z-\overline{\lambda}}

is constant, which yields
\lambda=-\overline{\lambda}
and therefore
\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. For
\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
we then have
(Q_{\lambda}\cdot v)^{\sharp}=Q_{\lambda}\cdot v
, if and only if
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}v=v
. We conclude that
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
is equivalent to
\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}}\cong\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a Hilbert space and set

\mathcal{E}_{+}\coloneqq\begin{cases}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})&\text{if }\mathcal{K}\text{ complex}\\
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}&\text{if }\mathcal{K}\text{ real}.\end{cases}

Further, let

A\coloneqq p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}(\partial S)p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}^{*}\in B(\mathcal{E}_{+}).

Then, if
\mathcal{K}
is a complex Hilbert space, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)=Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K},

and if
\mathcal{K}
is a real Hilbert space, one has

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)=\begin{cases}Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}&\text{if }\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
\{0\}&\text{if }\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We first consider the case that
\mathcal{K}
is a complex Hilbert space. Since

\partial S=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{S_{t}-\textbf{1}}{it}=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{M_{e^{it\mathrm{Id}}}-\textbf{1}}{it}=M_{\mathrm{Id}},

for
f\in D(A)
, we have
(Af)(z)=zf(z)
. Then, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,
v\in\mathcal{K}
and
f\in D(A)
, by Proposition
A.17
, we get

\left\langle Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\middle|Af\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|\lambda f(\lambda)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}=\lambda\left\langle v\middle|f(\lambda)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}=\lambda\left\langle Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\middle|f\right\rangle=\left\langle\overline{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\middle|f\right\rangle,

so
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\in D(A^{*})
with
A^{*}Q_{\lambda}\cdot v=\overline{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}\cdot v
and therefore
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)
.

Now, conversely, let
f\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)
. For every
v\in\mathcal{K}
and
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

(Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v)(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{1}{z-\overline{a}}-\frac{1}{z-\overline{b}}\right)\cdot v=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\overline{a}-\overline{b}}{(z-\overline{a})(z-\overline{b})}\cdot v,

so
Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v\in D(A)
with

\displaystyle(A(Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v))(z)

\displaystyle=z\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\overline{a}-\overline{b}}{(z-\overline{a})(z-\overline{b})}\cdot v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{z-\overline{a}}-\frac{\overline{b}}{z-\overline{b}}\right)\cdot v=(\overline{a}Q_{a}\cdot v-\overline{b}Q_{b}\cdot v)(z).

This leads to

\displaystyle\left\langle v\middle|\overline{\lambda}f(a)-\overline{\lambda}f(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v\middle|\overline{\lambda}f\right\rangle=\left\langle Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v\middle|A^{*}f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle A(Q_{a}\cdot v-Q_{b}\cdot v)\middle|f\right\rangle=\left\langle\overline{a}Q_{a}\cdot v-\overline{b}Q_{b}\cdot v\middle|f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=a\left\langle v\middle|f(a)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}-b\left\langle v\middle|f(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}=\left\langle v\middle|af(a)-bf(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}.

Since this is true for every
v\in\mathcal{K}
, we get

(\overline{\lambda}-a)f(a)=(\overline{\lambda}-b)f(b)

and therefore, since
Q_{\lambda}(b)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{b-\overline{\lambda}}
, we have

f(b)=\frac{a-\overline{\lambda}}{b-\overline{\lambda}}f(a)=Q_{\lambda}(b)\cdot 2\pi i(a-\overline{\lambda})f(a)=(Q_{\lambda}\cdot v)(b)

with
v\coloneqq 2\pi i(a-\overline{\lambda})f(a)\in\mathcal{K}
. This shows that

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)=Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}.

Now, in the case of a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
, analogously to the complex case, one shows that
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)
if
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
, so if
\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}
(cf. Lemma
2.23
). Also, as in the complex case, one shows that, for every
f\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)
, one has

\left\langle v\middle|\overline{\lambda}f(a)-\overline{\lambda}f(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}=\left\langle v\middle|af(a)-bf(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}

for every
a,b\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}
. But since
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{K}
, this also implies

\left\langle v\middle|\overline{\lambda}f(a)-\overline{\lambda}f(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}=\left\langle v\middle|af(a)-bf(b)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}

for every
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
and therefore, as in the complex case, one concludes that

f(b)=(Q_{\lambda}\cdot v)(b)

with
v\coloneqq 2\pi i(a-\overline{\lambda})f(a)
for every
b\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Since both functions
f
and
Q_{\lambda}\cdot v
are holomorphic and coincide on
i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, they are equal, so
f\in Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Using Lemma
2.23
, this shows that

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)=(Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=\begin{cases}Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K}&\text{if }\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\\
\{0\}&\text{if }\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.\end{cases}\qed

Theorem 2.25
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
be a complex ROPG and
\mathcal{K}
a complex Hilbert space such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
. Further, let

A\coloneqq p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}(\partial U)p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}^{*}\in B(\mathcal{E}_{+}).

Then, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

\mathcal{K}\cong\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
2.24
.
∎

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
be a real ROPG and
\mathcal{K}
a real Hilbert space such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
. Further, let

A\coloneqq p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}(\partial U)p_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}^{*}\in B(\mathcal{E}_{+}).

Then, for every
\lambda\in i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, one has

\mathcal{K}\cong\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
2.24
.
∎

Since the kernel
\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-A^{*}\right)
appearing in the last two theorems just depends on
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and not on the choice of
\mathcal{K}
, this theorem shows that
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism. We therefore can formulate the following definition:

Definition 2.27
.

For every complex/real ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.16
/Theorem
2.17
) and Theorem
2.25
/Theorem
2.26
, there exists an — up to isomorphism — unique complex/real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that the ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
or
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
respectively. We call this Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
the
multiplicity space of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
.

In order to not having to prove everything separately for real and complex ROPGs, we now show how they are linked to each other:

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a real Hilbert space,
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
be a closed subspace and
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
be a unitary one-parameter group. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a real ROPG, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex ROPG. In this case, if the multiplicity space of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is
\mathcal{K}
, then the multiplicity space of
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
is given by
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}=(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E}_{+}+i\mathcal{E}_{+})=U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}+iU_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+},

which immediately implies that
(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}
, if and only if
U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}
. Further, we get

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}=\left(\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\right)+i\left(\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\right)

and

\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}=\left(\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\right)+i\left(\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\right).

This proves that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a real ROPG, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex ROPG.

Now let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
be a real ROPG with multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
. Then there exists a isomorphism
\psi
of the real ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
. Then, the map
\psi_{\mathbb{C}}
is an isomorphism of complex ROPG. This, together with the fact that

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}+iL^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\cong L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}_{\mathbb{C}}

and

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}+iH^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\cong H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}_{\mathbb{C}}

implies that the complex ROPG
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),S)
and therefore has multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

In the following sections, we will always first prove our results just for complex ROPGs and will later translate our final theorems also to the real case using Proposition
2.28
.

2.3
Independence of the multiplicity space from
\mathcal{E}_{+}

In this section, we want to answer the question of whether the multiplicity space of a ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
depends on the subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}
when one fixes the Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
and the unitary one-parameter group
U
. For this we consider our standard example
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
and want to vary the space
\mathcal{E}_{+}
, so we consider ROPGs of the form
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
for a closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
and want to calculate their multiplicity space.

The theorem by Halmos (
[
Ha61
, Thm. 3]
) that we will use to solve this problem has been formulated for the Hardy space on the unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
instead of the Hardy space on the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, but via Cayley transform we will be able to switch between these two pictures. To formulate this theorem by Halmos, we need some definitions:

Definition 2.31
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space.

(a)

We write

L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})\coloneqq L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K},\mu)\quad\text{and}\quad L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},B(\mathcal{K}))\coloneqq L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},B(\mathcal{K}),\mu),

where
\mu
is the Haar measure on the group
{\mathbb{T}}
normalized by
\mu({\mathbb{T}})=1
. Further, we write
H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K})\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})
for the corresponding Hardy space on the disc (cf. Definition
A.116
).

(b)

For
\Omega={\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}}
, we call
F\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))

rigid
, if there exists a subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
such that for almost every
z\in\Omega
the operator
F(z)
is a partial isometry with initial subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}
.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. We then say a closed subspace
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

reduces an operator
A\in\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{H})
if
[A,P_{\mathfrak{M}}]=0
. We say that a closed subspace
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is
irreducible for
A
, if no non-trivial subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
reduces
A
.

Theorem 2.33
.

(cf.
[
Ha61
, Thm. 3]
)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and let
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})
be a closed subspace. Then
\mathfrak{M}
is irreducible for the operator
M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}\in\mathrm{B}\left(L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})\right)
and satisfies
M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, if and only if there exists a rigid function
F\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))
such that

\mathfrak{M}=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K}).

To use the previous theorem, we have to switch from the unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
to the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. We will do this by using the biholomorphic Cayley transform

h(z)\coloneqq\frac{z-i}{z+i}

that has the property that

h({\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{T}}\setminus\{1\}\quad\text{and}\quad h({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{D}}.

Then
h
induces an isometric isomorphism of the Hilbert spaces
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})
and
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
given by

\Gamma:L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{K})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\quad(\Gamma f)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}(x+i)}(f\circ h)\left(x\right),

which which preserves the corresponding Hardy spaces, i.e.

\Gamma(H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K}))=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})

(cf.
[
RR94
, Cor. 5.24, Sec. 5.15]
). Using this map
\Gamma
, we get the following analogue to Theorem
2.33
:

Theorem 2.34
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
{\mathfrak{N}}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
be a closed subspace such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),{\mathfrak{N}},S)
is a SROPG. Then there exists a rigid function
F\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))
such that

{\mathfrak{N}}=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}).

Proof.

We first show that the subspace
\mathfrak{M}\coloneqq\Gamma^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}
is irreducible for
M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}
. So let
V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
be a reducing subspace for
M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}
. Then
[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}},P_{V}]=0
and therefore, since
M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}
is unitary, we also get

[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}^{*},P_{V}]=[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}^{*},P_{V}^{*}]=-[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}},P_{V}]^{*}=-0^{*}=0.

This implies that

[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}^{n},P_{V}]=[M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}^{n}},P_{V}]=0\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

By Proposition
A.118
we have

\overline{{\rm span}\{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}^{n}:n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}}),

where the closure is taken with respect to the weak-
*
-topology on
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Therefore

[M_{f},P_{V}]=0\quad\forall f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Using Cayley transform to switch to the upper half-plane, setting
\widetilde{V}\coloneqq\Gamma V
, we get

[M_{f},P_{\widetilde{V}}]=0\quad\forall f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Since
S_{t}=M_{e_{t}}
for the functions
e_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with
e_{t}(x)\coloneqq e^{itx}
, we get

[S_{t},P_{\widetilde{V}}]=0\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Therefore, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we get

\widetilde{V}=P_{\widetilde{V}}\widetilde{V}=S_{t}S_{-t}P_{\widetilde{V}}\widetilde{V}=S_{t}P_{\widetilde{V}}S_{-t}\widetilde{V}\subseteq S_{t}\widetilde{V}=S_{t}\Gamma V\subseteq S_{t}\Gamma\mathfrak{M}=S_{t}{\mathfrak{N}},

so

\widetilde{V}\subseteq\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}{\mathfrak{N}}=\{0\}

which yields
\widetilde{V}=\{0\}
and therefore also

V=\Gamma^{-1}\widetilde{V}=\{0\}.

This shows that no non-trivial subspace of
\mathfrak{M}
reduces
U
, and therefore
\mathfrak{M}
is irreducible. Further, by Proposition
A.115
, we have

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}}\mathfrak{M}

\displaystyle\subseteq H^{\infty}({\mathbb{D}},{\mathbb{C}})\cdot\mathfrak{M}=H^{\infty}({\mathbb{D}},{\mathbb{C}})\cdot\Gamma^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}=\Gamma^{-1}\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})\cdot{\mathfrak{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{-1}\left(\overline{\bigcup_{t\geq 0}M_{e_{t}}{\mathfrak{N}}}\right)=\Gamma^{-1}\left(\overline{\bigcup_{t\geq 0}S_{t}{\mathfrak{N}}}\right)\subseteq\Gamma^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}=\mathfrak{M}.

Therefore we can apply Theorem
2.33
and get a rigid function
\widetilde{F}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))
such that

\mathfrak{M}=M_{\widetilde{F}}H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K}).

Transforming to the upper half-plane, for the rigid function

F\coloneqq\widetilde{F}\circ h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K})),

we get

{\mathfrak{N}}=\Gamma^{-1}\mathfrak{M}=\Gamma^{-1}M_{\widetilde{F}}H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K})=M_{F}\Gamma^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{K})=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}).\qed

Given a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is a ROPG, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.16
), there exists another complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),S)
. The following result tells us that in fact
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}
:

Corollary 2.35
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
be a closed subspace such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is a ROPG. Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.34
there exists a rigid function
F\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))
such that

\mathcal{E}_{+}=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}).

Now, we define

P:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\quad(Pf)(x)=P_{F(x)\mathcal{K}}f(x).

Then
P
is a projection with

PM_{F}=M_{F}\qquad\text{and}\qquad P\circ S_{t}=S_{t}\circ P,\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Now, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=S_{t}M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=S_{t}PM_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=PS_{t}M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})\subseteq PL^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),

so

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}\subseteq PL^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}).

This yields
P=\textbf{1}
and therefore
P_{x}=\textbf{1}
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, which implies that
F(x)\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Choosing such an element
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the operator
F(x_{0})
then is a partial isometry with initial subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}
and final subspace
\mathcal{K}
and therefore
I\coloneqq F(x_{0})^{*}
is an isometry with initial subspace
\mathcal{K}
and final subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}
. We now define
G\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{K}))
by

G(x)\coloneqq F(x)I.

Then

M_{G}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=M_{F}IH^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},{\mathfrak{U}})=M_{F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{E}_{+},

identifying
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},{\mathfrak{U}})
with the subspace

\{f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}):(\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+})\,f(z)\in{\mathfrak{U}}\}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}).

Further, since
I
is an isometry with initial subspace
\mathcal{K}
and final subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}
and for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
the operator
F(x)
is a partial isometry with initial subspace
{\mathfrak{U}}
and final subspace
\mathcal{K}
, for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
the operator
G(x)
is a partial isometry with initial subspace
\mathcal{K}
and final subspace
\mathcal{K}
and therefore
G(x)
is unitary. This implies that also
M_{G}
is unitary. We now set

\psi\coloneqq M_{G}^{-1}\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})).

Then

\psi\left(\mathcal{E}_{+}\right)=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})

and

\psi\circ S_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.\qed

Theorem 2.36
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a Hilbert space and
U
be a unitary one-parameter group on
\mathcal{E}
. Further, let
\mathcal{E}_{+},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
be two closed subspaces such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
are ROPGs. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
.

Proof.

We first consider the case of complex ROPGs. Then, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.16
), there exists a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that the ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
. Let
\psi
be the corresponding isomorphism of ROPGs. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
, using the same isomorphism
\psi
, is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\psi\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},S)
. By Corollary
2.35
we know that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),\psi\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},S)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
and therefore also
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
are equivalent.

In the case of real ROPGs, we notice that, by Proposition
2.28
,
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U)
are equivalent, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
are equivalent, which, as just seen, is indeed the case.
∎

Remark 2.37
.

This theorem shows that, fixing a Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
and a unitary one-parameter group
U
on
\mathcal{E}
, there is — up to equivalence — only one possible choice of a subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a ROPG.

3
ROPGs generated by Pick functions

In the last section, we answered the question, given a ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
, what happens when one fixes the Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
and the unitary one-parameter group
U
and varies the subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}
. In this chapter, we want to answer the question, what happens when one fixes the Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
and the subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}
and varies the unitary one-parameter group
U
, so we will have two ROPGs of the form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U^{\prime})
. In the complex case, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem, we then find a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),S)
via some isomorphism
\psi
of ROPGs. Using that same isomorphism
\psi
, the ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U^{\prime})
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),\psi U^{\prime}\psi^{-1})
. Therefore, our question of interest is, for which unitary one-parameter groups
U
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U)
is a complex ROPG and if so, how to calculate the corresponding multiplicity space.

3.1
Regular Pick functions

We will not give a complete characterization of all unitary one-parameter groups
U
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U)
is a complex ROPG, but we will do so under two more assumptions. The first assumption is that our multiplicity space
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional. Our second assumption is, that the unitary one-parameter group
U
and our standard unitary one-parameter group
S
commute, i.e.

U_{t}S_{s}=S_{s}U_{t}\quad\forall s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

This last condition, by Proposition
2.19
, is equivalent to
U_{t}\in M\left(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))\right)
for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the condition

U_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\quad\forall t\geq 0,

by Proposition
A.113
, implies that

U_{t}\in M\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))\right)\quad\forall t\geq 0.

The unitarity of
U_{t}
then implies that

U_{t}\in M\left(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))\right)\quad\forall t\geq 0

(cf. Definition
A.31
), which, by Theorem
A.44
, implies that
U
is infinitesimally generated by a Pick function, defined as follows:

Definition 3.11
.

(cf. Definition
A.41
)
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space.

(a)

We call a function
{F\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))}
an
operator-valued Pick function
if

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))\coloneqq\frac{1}{2i}(F(z)-F(z)^{*})\geq 0,\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(b)

We write
\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
for the set of operator-valued Pick functions.

(c)

We write
\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
for the set of Pick functions
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
, for which the limit

F_{*}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F(x+i\varepsilon)

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and is self-adjoint. We say such a Pick function has self-adjoint boundary values.

(d)

We set

\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\coloneqq\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B({\mathbb{C}}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B({\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}

and, in the latter case, we say these functions have real boundary values.

Since we want to analyze ROPGs, we are interested in Pick functions, that are infinitesimal generators for ROPGs. Here, we make the following definition:

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. We call a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}

regular
if

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}).

We write
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
for the set of all regular Pick functions.

Remark 3.13
.

Given this definition, a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
is regular, if and only if the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
with

??_{t}\coloneqq M_{e^{itF_{*}}},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

is a complex ROPG. Here, we use that the condition

e^{itF_{*}}\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\quad\forall t\geq 0

is fulfilled for every Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
, since
e^{itF}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
for all
t\geq 0
.

The goal of this section now is to give a classification of all regular Pick functions. For this, we will use the fact that the boundary values of a Pick function are a measurable function (cf. Lemma
A.45
) and, therefore, can be measurably diagonalized using the following results:

Theorem 3.14
.

(cf.
[
QR14
, Thm. 2.1]
)

Let
X
be a measurable space and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Further, let
{F:X\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)}
be measurable such that
F(x)
is a positive definite matrix for every
x\in X
. Then there exist measurable functions
\Lambda,U:X\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)
such that
\Lambda(x)
is diagonal,
U(x)
is unitary and
F(x)=U(x)\Lambda(x)U(x)^{*}
for all
x\in X
.

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
X
be a measurable space and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Further, let
{F:X\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)}
be measurable such that
F(x)
is a self-adjoint matrix for every
x\in X
. Then there exist measurable functions
\Lambda,U:X\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)
such that
\Lambda(x)
is diagonal,
U(x)
is unitary and
F(x)=U(x)\Lambda(x)U(x)^{*}
for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

By applying Theorem
3.14
to the function
\exp\circ F
, we obtain measurable functions
\widetilde{\Lambda},U
such that
\widetilde{\Lambda}(x)
is diagonal,
U(x)
is unitary and
(\exp\circ F)(x)=U(x)\widetilde{\Lambda}(x)U(x)^{*}
for all
x\in X
. The statement then follows by choosing
\Lambda\coloneqq\log\circ\widetilde{\Lambda}
.
∎

We now can formulate our first classification theorem of regular Pick functions using the following definition:

Definition 3.16
.

For a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
and
M\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

??\coloneqq\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}:F_{*}(y)\text{ exists and }M\cap{\rm Spec}(F_{*}(y))\neq\emptyset\}.

Further, for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we set

D(F,x)\coloneqq D(F,\{x\})=\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}:F_{*}(y)\text{ exists and }x\in{\rm Spec}(F_{*}(y))\}.

Theorem 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

F
is regular.

(b)

{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.

(c)

\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(d)

{\mathbb{R}}\setminus D(F,x)
has non-zero Lebesgue measure for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(e)

D(F,x)
is a Lebesgue zero-set for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(c):

Let
F
be regular and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Further, let
v\in\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. Then

\{0\}=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\supseteq\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}{\mathbb{C}}v=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itx}{\mathbb{C}}v={\mathbb{C}}v,

which implies
v=0
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):

Assume that
{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Let
h\in\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. Now, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, let
F(z)^{*}=D(z)+N(z)
be the Jordan–Chevalley Decomposition of
F(z)^{*}
into a diagonalizable matrix
D(z)
and a nilpotent matrix
N(z)
. Further, setting
n\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}
, we choose a basis
v_{1}(z),\dots,v_{n}(z)
of
\mathcal{H}
of eigenvectors of
D(z)
and let
\lambda_{1}(z),\dots,\lambda_{n}(z)
be the corresponding eigenvalues, so

D(z)v_{k}(z)=\lambda_{k}(z)v_{k}(z)

for all
k=1,\dots,n
. Then, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
k=1,\dots,n
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{z}\cdot v_{k}(z)\middle|e^{itF_{*}}h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle v_{k}(z)\middle|e^{itF(z)}h(z)\right\rangle=\left\langle e^{-itF(z)^{*}}v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle e^{-it\lambda_{k}(z)}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\left(-itN(z)\right)^{j}\right)v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle.

with
Q_{z}
as in Proposition
A.17
.
This yields

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-it\overline{\lambda_{k}(z)}}}{t^{n}}\left\langle Q_{z}\cdot v_{k}(z)\middle|e^{itF_{*}}h\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{1}{j!t^{n-j}}\left(-iN(z)\right)^{j}\right)v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle.

We further have

\left|\left\langle Q_{z}\cdot v_{k}(z)\middle|e^{itF_{*}}h\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\lVert Q_{z}\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert v_{k}(z)\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert h\right\rVert\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\frac{e^{-it\overline{\lambda_{k}(z)}}}{t^{n}}\right|=\frac{e^{-t\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\lambda_{k}(z))}}{t^{n}}\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0,

using that by assumption
\lambda_{k}(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and therefore
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\lambda_{k}(z))>0
. Then, for
t\to\infty
, we get

0=\left\langle\frac{1}{n!}\left(-iN(z)\right)^{n}v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle.

Therefore

\left\langle Q_{z}\cdot v_{k}(z)\middle|e^{itF_{*}}h\right\rangle=\left\langle e^{-it\lambda_{k}(z)}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{j!}\left(-itN(z)\right)^{j}\right)v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle.

Now, repeating the same argument, one shows that

0=\left\langle\frac{1}{j!}\left(-iN(z)\right)^{j}v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle

for all
j=0,\dots,n
. Especially, for
j=0
, we get

0=\left\langle v_{k}(z)\middle|h(z)\right\rangle.

Since this is true for all
k=1,\dots,n
and the vectors
v_{1}(z),\dots,v_{n}(z)
form a basis of
\mathcal{H}
, we have
h(z)=0
for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and therefore
h=0
, so

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}.

On the other hand, using the same argument for the operator-valued Pick function
F^{\flat}
on the lower half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
defined by

F^{\flat}(z)\coloneqq-F(\overline{z})^{*},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

one gets

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF^{\flat}_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}.

Then, using that

F^{\flat}_{*}=-F^{*}_{*}=-F_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})^{\perp},

as seen in Remark
2.12
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{itF^{\flat}_{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp}=\{0\}^{\perp}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),

so
F
is regular.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(b):

Assume that
{\rm Spec}(F(z))\not\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
for some
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Since, by Proposition
A.42
(a), we have
{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
, there exists
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and a
v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
with
F(z)v=xv
. Then, by Proposition
A.42
(b), we have
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))v=0
. We now define the Pick function

f:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad w\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle.

Then

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(z))=\left\langle v\middle|\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))v\right\rangle=0

and therefore, by Lemma
A.43
, we get that
f
is constant. So
