\left\langle v\middle|\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(w))v\right\rangle=\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(w))=\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(z))=0

for all
w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Since
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(w))\geq 0
, this implies that

0=\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(w))v=\frac{1}{2i}(F(w)-F(w)^{*})v

and therefore
F(w)^{*}v=F(w)v
for all
w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.
We now define the function

g:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad w\mapsto\left\lVert(F(w)-x)v\right\rVert^{2}.

Since

\displaystyle g(w)

\displaystyle=\left\langle F(w)v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle-x\left\langle F(w)v\middle|v\right\rangle-x\left\langle v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle+x^{2}\left\langle v\middle|v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle F(w)^{*}v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle-x\left\langle F(w)^{*}v\middle|v\right\rangle-x\left\langle v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle+x^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle v\middle|F(w)^{2}v\right\rangle-2x\left\langle v\middle|F(w)v\right\rangle+x^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2},

the function
g
is holomorphic with
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(g)\equiv 0
, which implies that
g
is constant. Therefore, for every
w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\left\lVert(F(w)-x)v\right\rVert^{2}=g(w)=g(z)=0,

which implies

F(w)v=xv.

Taking limits, this also implies

F_{*}(y)v=xv\qquad\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Now, for every function
h\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\setminus\{0\}
, we have have
h\otimes v\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\setminus\{0\}
with

(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})(h\otimes v)=0,

so especially
\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\neq\{0\}
.

(d)
\Rightarrow
(c):

Assume that there exists
v\in\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
with
v\neq 0
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
v\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, by Theorem
A.14
, we have
v(y)\neq 0
for almost every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
and therefore

\ker(x\textbf{1}-F_{*}(y))\supseteq{\mathbb{C}}v(y)\neq\{0\}

for almost every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This implies that the Lebesgue measure of
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus D(F,x)
is zero.

(e)
\Rightarrow
(d):

This follows immediately from the fact that
{\mathbb{R}}
has non-zero Lebesgue measure.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(e):

Assume that
D(F,x)
is no Lebesgue zero-set for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then there is a
R>0
such that
\lambda_{1}(D(F,x)\cap[-R,R])>0
, denoting by
\lambda_{1}
the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Setting
n\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}
, by Corollary
3.15
there exists a measurable function
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)
such that
U(x)
is unitary for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and functions
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that, after the identification
\mathcal{H}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, we have

F_{*}(y)=U(y)\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(d_{1}(y),\dots,d_{n}(y))U(y)^{*}

for almost every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
. For
k=1,\dots,n
, we define the measurable sets

M_{k}\coloneqq d_{k}^{-1}(x)\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}d_{j}^{-1}(x).

Denoting by
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
the canonical ONB of
\mathcal{H}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, we now consider the measurable function

g\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{M_{k}\cap[-R,R]}Ue_{k}.

One has

\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{1}(M_{k}\cap[-R,R])=\lambda_{1}(D(F,x)\cap[-R,R])\in]0,2R],

so
g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\setminus\{0\}
.
Further, for almost every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle F_{*}(y)g(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{M_{k}\cap[-R,R]}(y)F_{*}(y)U(y)e_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{M_{k}\cap[-R,R]}(y)d_{k}(y)U(y)e_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{M_{k}\cap[-R,R]}(y)xU(y)e_{k}=xg(y),

so
g\in\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})
. Since
g\big|_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus[-R,R]}\equiv 0
, we know that
g\notin H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})
by Theorem
A.14
. Denoting by
P_{\pm}
the projections onto
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{\pm},\mathcal{H})
and setting
g_{\pm}\coloneqq P_{\pm}g
, we therefore have
g_{+}\neq 0
. Now, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

e^{itx}g_{+}+e^{itx}g_{-}=e^{itx}g=e^{itF_{*}}g=e^{itF_{*}}g_{+}+e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}.

For
t\geq 0
we have
e^{itF_{*}}g_{+}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, so applying
P_{\pm}
to both sides, we get

e^{itx}g_{+}=e^{itF_{*}}g_{+}+P_{+}e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{itx}g_{-}=P_{-}e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}.

This implies

\left\lVert P_{+}e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert P_{-}e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert g_{-}\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert e^{itx}g_{-}\right\rVert^{2}=0,

so
P_{+}e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}=0
and therefore

e^{itx}g_{+}=e^{itF_{*}}g_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{itx}g_{-}=e^{itF_{*}}g_{-}

for all
t\geq 0
. This implies that

g_{+},g_{-}\in\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}}),

so since
g_{+}\neq 0
, we especially get

\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\neq\{0\}.\qed

We now will see some consequences of this theorem:

Corollary 3.18
.

A function
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
is regular, if and only if
f
is non-constant.

Proof.

If
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
is constant, than, since
f
has real boundary values, there is a
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
f\equiv\lambda
, so
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits f(z)=0
for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. If on the other hand
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
is non-constant, then, by Lemma
A.43
, we have
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits f(z)>0
for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Therefore
f
is non-constant, if and only if

{\rm Spec}(f(z))=\{f(z)\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
which, by Theorem
3.17
, is equivalent to the function
f
being regular.
∎

Since, by Theorem
3.17
, for
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

f(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm Spec}(f(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

the operators

F(f(z))\in B(\mathcal{H})\quad\text{and}\quad f(F(z))\in B(\mathcal{H})

are defined. Therefore, the maps

\circ:\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\to\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H})),\quad(F\circ f)(z)\coloneqq F(f(z))

and

\circ:\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\times\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\to\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H})),\quad(f\circ F)(z)\coloneqq f(F(z))

are also defined. The following theorem shows that their image, in fact, is again
\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
:

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. Further, let
f,g\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
all be regular. Then
f\circ F\circ g
is also regular.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.17
, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\{f(z)\}={\rm Spec}(f(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\quad\{g(z)\}={\rm Spec}(g(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Therefore, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we get

{\rm Spec}((f\circ F\circ g)(z))=f({\rm Spec}(F(g(z))))\subseteq f({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

which by Theorem
3.17
implies that
f\circ F\circ g
is regular.
∎

With help of this corollary, we can prove a theorem with a generalization of condition (e) in Theorem
3.17
:

Theorem 3.110
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

F
is regular.

(b)

D(F,M)
is a Lebesgue zero-set for all Lebesgue zero-sets
M\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):

Follows immediately from Theorem
3.17
, using the fact that
\{x\}
is a Lebesgue zero-set for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):

We assume that there is a Lebesgue zero-set
M\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
such that
D(F,M)
has positive Lebesgue measure. By Corollary
3.15
there exists a measurable function
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathrm{Mat}(n\times n)
such that
U(x)
is unitary for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and measurable functions
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
such that, after the identification
\mathcal{H}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, we have

F_{*}(y)=U(y)\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(d_{1}(y),\dots,d_{n}(y))U(y)^{*}

for every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
F_{*}(y)
exists and
d_{1}(y),\dots,d_{n}(y)=\infty
for every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
F_{*}(y)
doesn’t exist. Then

D(F,M)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}d_{k}^{-1}(M).

So, by assumption, there exists
K\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
d_{K}^{-1}(M)
has positive Lebesgue measure. We now choose
R>0
such that

\lambda_{1}\left(d_{K}^{-1}(M)\cap[-R,R]\right)>0.

Then

\mu(E)\coloneqq\lambda_{1}\left(d_{K}^{-1}(M\cap E)\cap[-R,R]\right)

defines a measure
\mu
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with

\mu({\mathbb{R}})=\lambda_{1}\left(d_{K}^{-1}(M)\cap[-R,R]\right),

which implies

0<\mu({\mathbb{R}})\leq 2R<\infty,

so
\mu
is non-zero and finite. Further, since
M
is a Lebesgue zero-set and
{\mu({\mathbb{R}}\setminus M)=0}
, it is also Lebesgue-singular. Therefore, by Theorem
A.46
,

F_{\mu}(z)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)

defines a Pick function with real boundary-values. Further, by Theorem
A.46
, setting

S_{\mu}\coloneqq\left\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F_{\mu}(x+i\varepsilon))=+\infty\right\},

we have
\mu(E)=\mu(E\cap S_{\mu})
for every measurable subset
E\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. In particular, this implies that

0\neq\mu({\mathbb{R}})=\mu(S_{\mu})=\lambda_{1}\left(d_{K}^{-1}(M\cap S_{\mu})\cap[-R,R]\right).

Further, defining the Pick function

\eta:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\quad z\mapsto-\frac{1}{z},

we get

(\eta\circ F_{\mu})_{*}(x)=0

for every
x\in S_{\mu}
. For
x\in d_{K}^{-1}(M\cap S_{\mu})\cap[-R,R]
, by definition, we have that

\emptyset\neq(M\cap S_{\mu})\cap{\rm Spec}(F_{*}(x)),

so

\emptyset\neq(\eta\circ F_{\mu})_{*}(M\cap S_{\mu})\cap{\rm Spec}((\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F)_{*}(x))=\{0\}\cap{\rm Spec}((\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F)_{*}(x))

and therefore

0\in{\rm Spec}((\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F)_{*}(x)).

We then obtain

D(\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F,0)\supseteq d_{K}^{-1}(M\cap S_{\mu})\cap[-R,R]

and therefore
D(\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F,0)
is no Lebesgue zero-set, so, by Theorem
3.17
, we have that
\eta\circ F_{\mu}\circ F
is non-regular. Since
\eta
and
F_{\mu}
are non-constant and therefore, by Corollary
3.18
, regular, we get, by Corollary
3.19
, that
F
is non-regular. ∎

After now having some characterizations for regular Pick functions, i.e. the Pick functions
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG, we now want to show how to use these characterizations for real ROPGs. This question is answered in the following theorem:

Theorem 3.111
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
U^{F}
defines a unitary one-parameter group on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
, if and only if
F=F^{\sharp}
with

F^{\sharp}(z)\coloneqq-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}F(-\overline{z})\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

In this case the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},U^{F})
is a real ROPG with multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
, if and only if the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG with multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

One has

e^{itF_{*}}\cdot L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},

if and only if

\displaystyle\left(e^{itF_{*}}\cdot f\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\left(e^{itF_{*}}\cdot f\right)^{\sharp}(x)=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}e^{itF_{*}(-x)}f(-x)

\displaystyle=e^{-it\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}F_{*}(-x)\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}f(-x)=e^{itF^{\sharp}_{*}(x)}f^{\sharp}(x)=e^{itF^{\sharp}_{*}(x)}f(x)

for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
and almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This is the case, if and only if

F_{*}(x)=F^{\sharp}_{*}(x)

for almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, which is equivalent to
F=F^{\sharp}
. The second statement follows by Proposition
2.28
.
∎

3.2
The multiplicity free case

In the last section, given a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we have seen for which Pick functions
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG and likewise, given a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, for which
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},U^{F})
is a real ROPG.

The goal of the following sections will be, given a regular Pick function
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
or
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}
respectively, to calculate the multiplicity space for the complex ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
or the real ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},U^{F})
respectively.

We will later provide an answer in the general case of any finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. In this section, we will discuss the multiplicity-free case
\mathcal{H}={\mathbb{C}}
. Although not generalizable to a case with multiplicities, this proof gives a good idea of the behavior of Pick functions and how they produce multiplicities.

By Theorem
A.46
, every function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
is of the form

F(z)=C+Dz+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)

with
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
D\geq 0
and some singular Borel measure
\mu
on
{\mathbb{R}}
supported on the set

S_{\mu}\coloneqq\left\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(x+i\varepsilon))=+\infty\right\}.

We set

S_{F}\coloneqq\begin{cases}S_{\mu}&\text{if }D=0\\
S_{\mu}\cup\{\infty\}&\text{if }D>0\end{cases}

(cf. Remark
A.47
). With this definition, we can formulate our theorem:

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
S_{F}
is a non-empty closed subset of
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG and its multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
is given by

\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}&\text{if }|S_{F}|=n\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{C}})&\text{if }|S_{F}|=\infty.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
S_{F}
is non-empty, the function
F
is non-constant and therefore, by Corollary
3.18
, the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG. We now calculate its multiplicity space.

By Lemma
A.49
there is a countable family
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)_{j\in J}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\times\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
such that

\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus S_{F}=\bigsqcup_{j\in J}\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right).

One has
S_{F}=\{a_{j}:j\in J\}
and therefore
|S_{F}|=|J|
. Further, by Proposition
A.410
, for every
j\in J
, the map

F_{j}\coloneqq F_{*}\big|_{\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)}:\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)\to{\mathbb{R}}

is monotonically increasing, bijective, and continuously differentiable. Therefore, for every function
g\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cong L^{1}\left(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},{\mathbb{C}}\right)
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus S_{F}}g(x)\,dx=\sum_{j\in J}\int_{(a_{j},b_{j})}g(x)\,dx=\sum_{j\in J}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\left(F_{j}^{-1}(y)\right)\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}(y)\,dy.

This implies that the map

V:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to\ell^{2}(J,L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})),\quad f\mapsto\left(\sqrt{\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}}\cdot\left(f\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)\right)_{j\in J}

is a unitary isomorphism of Hilbert spaces. Further, by the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem, the map

T:\ell^{2}(J,L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})),\quad T\left(\left(f_{j}\right)_{j\in J}\right)(x)=\left(f_{j}(x)\right)_{j\in J},

is a unitary isomorphism of Hilbert spaces. Then, for
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\displaystyle(T\circ V)(U^{F}_{t}f)(x)

\displaystyle=(T\circ V)\left(e^{itF_{*}}f\right)(x)=T\left(\left(\sqrt{\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}}\cdot e^{it\left(F_{*}\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)}\left(f\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)\right)_{j\in J}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}(x)}\cdot e^{it\left(F_{*}\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)(x)}\left(f\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)(x)\right)_{j\in J}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}(x)}\cdot e^{itx}\left(f\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)(x)\right)_{j\in J}

\displaystyle=e^{itx}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\left(F_{j}^{-1}\right)^{\prime}(x)}\cdot\left(f\circ F_{j}^{-1}\right)(x)\right)_{j\in J}

\displaystyle=e^{itx}\cdot\left(T\circ V\right)(f)(x)=\left(S_{t}\circ T\circ V\right)(f)(x),

so

(T\circ V)\circ U^{F}_{t}=S_{t}\circ(T\circ V)\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Now set

\mathcal{E}_{+}\coloneqq\left(T\circ V\right)\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})).

Then, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\displaystyle S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}

\displaystyle=\left(S_{t}\circ\left(T\circ V\right)\right)\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)=\left(\left(T\circ V\right)\circ U^{F}_{t}\right)\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right),

so, using that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
is a complex ROPG, we get

S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=\left(T\circ V\right)\left(U^{F}_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\subseteq\left(T\circ V\right)\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)=\mathcal{E}_{+}\quad\forall t\geq 0

and

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}=\left(T\circ V\right)\left(\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U^{F}_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)=\left(T\circ V\right)\left(\{0\}\right)=\{0\}

and

\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}}=\left(T\circ V\right)\left(\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}U^{F}_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}\right)=\left(T\circ V\right)\left(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\right)=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})).

This implies that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is a complex ROPG, which is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
via the isomorphism
T\circ V
. Further, by Theorem
2.36
the complex ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})),\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})),S)
. So, setting
{\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})}
, we have that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
and therefore
\mathcal{K}=\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})
is the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),U^{F})
. The statement then follows since

\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{|J|}={\mathbb{C}}^{|S_{F}|}

if
|J|=|S_{F}|\in{\mathbb{N}}
and

\ell^{2}(J,{\mathbb{C}})\cong\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{C}})

if
|J|=|S_{F}|=\infty
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

One has
|S_{F}|\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if and only if the measure in the Herglotz representation of
F
is supported on a finite set, i.e. if there are
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

F(z)=C+Dz+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{a_{k}}{\lambda_{k}-z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

for some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
D\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. In this case, one has

|S_{F}|=\begin{cases}n&\text{if }D=0\\
n+1&\text{if }D>0.\end{cases}

We will now also provide an analogue to Theorem
3.21
in the real case:

Theorem 3.23
.

Let
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
S_{F}
is a non-empty closed subset of
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\sharp},U^{F})
is a real ROPG, if and only if

F(z)=-\overline{F(-\overline{z})}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and in this case its multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
is given by

\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}&\text{if }|S_{F}|=n\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{R}})&\text{if }|S_{F}|=\infty.\end{cases}

Proof.

This follows immediately by Theorem
3.111
and Theorem
3.21
.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

The condition
F(z)=-\overline{F(-\overline{z})}
, for a Pick function

F(z)=C+Dz+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)

is equivalent to the measure
\mu
being symmetric, i.e.
\mu(A)=\mu(-A)
for all measurable subsets
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. In this case, the Pick function can be written as

\displaystyle F(z)

\displaystyle=C+Dz-\frac{\mu(\{0\})}{z}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}+\frac{1}{-\lambda-z}\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+Dz-\frac{\mu(\{0\})}{z}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{2z}{\lambda^{2}-z^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda),\qquad\qquad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

3.3
The general case

After considering the one-dimensional case, in this section, given any finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a regular Pick function
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
, we want to calculate the multiplicity space for the complex ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
, as well as provide an analogue in the real case. We will do this by using Theorem
2.25
, in which appears the operator

p\left(\partial U^{F}\right)p^{*}=pM_{F_{*}}p^{*}=M_{F}

with
p\coloneqq p_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})}
.
Therefore, given a Pick function
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have to better understand the kernel
\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. We will see that this kernel has a property that we will call
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stability. Therefore, the following subsection will be dedicated to the theory of
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces.

3.3.1
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces and finite Blaschke–Potapov products

Throughout this subsection, by
\perp
we will denote the orthogonal complement in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
and by
??
the orthogonal complement in the Hilbert space
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space. We set

??\coloneqq P_{H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})}\in B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})).

A subspace
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
is called
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable
, if

P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}V\subseteq V

for every
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

It turns out that
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces are closely related to SROPGs:

Proposition 3.32
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space. A subspace
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
is
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable, if and only if
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),V^{\perp_{+}},S)
is a SROPG.

Proof.

We first assume that
V
is a
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace, so especially
P_{+}S_{t}^{*}V\subseteq V
for every
t\geq 0
since
S_{t}=M_{e_{t}}
for the functions
e_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

??(x)\coloneqq e^{itx}.

Then, for
t\geq 0
,
f\in V^{\perp_{+}}
and
g\in V
, one has

\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}g\right\rangle=\left\langle P_{+}f\middle|S_{t}^{*}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|P_{+}S_{t}^{*}g\right\rangle=0,

using that
P_{+}S_{t}^{*}g\in V
. This shows that
S_{t}V^{\perp_{+}}\subseteq V^{\perp_{+}}
for
t\geq 0
. Since also

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}V^{\perp_{+}}\subseteq\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\},

we get that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),V^{\perp_{+}},S)
is a SROPG.

Now, conversely assume that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),V^{\perp_{+}},S)
is a SROPG. Then, for
t\geq 0
,
f\in V^{\perp_{+}}
and
g\in V
, one has

\left\langle f\middle|P_{+}S_{t}^{*}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|g\right\rangle=0,

using that
S_{t}f\in V^{\perp_{+}}
. This shows that

P_{+}M_{e_{t}}^{*}V=P_{+}S_{t}^{*}V\subseteq V\quad t\geq 0,

which, by Proposition
A.115
implies the statement.
∎

Corollary 3.33
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space. A subspace
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
is
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable, if and only if it is of the form

V=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}

for some function
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
.

Proof.

Let
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
be
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable. Then, by Proposition
3.32
and Theorem
2.34
there exists a rigid function
\varphi\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{H}))
such that

M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=V^{\perp_{+}}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

The last inclusion in particular implies
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{H}))
by Proposition
A.113
.

Conversely, for every
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{H}))
, one has

S_{t}\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)=M_{\varphi}\left(S_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)\subseteq M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\quad\forall t\geq 0

and

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)\subseteq\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}.

Therefore
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),S)
is a SROPG, which, by Proposition
3.32
, is equivalent to
\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}
being a
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace.
∎

When working with
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces, the following result is often useful:

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
\varphi,\psi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Then

P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}P_{+}M_{\psi}^{*}=P_{+}M_{\psi\cdot\varphi}^{*}.

Proof.

We have

P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}(\textbf{1}-P_{+})L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})=P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})\subseteq P_{+}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})=\{0\},

so
P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}(\textbf{1}-P_{+})=0
and therefore

P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}P_{+}=P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*},

which implies

P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}P_{+}M_{\psi}^{*}=P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}M_{\psi}^{*}=P_{+}\left(M_{\psi}M_{\varphi}\right)^{*}=P_{+}M_{\psi\cdot\varphi}^{*}.\qed

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
be a finite-dimensional
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace. Then, if
V\neq\{0\}
, there exist
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that
Q_{\omega}\cdot v\in V
.

Proof.

Since
V
is
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable, we have

P_{+}S_{t}^{*}V\subseteq V\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Further, for
s,t\geq 0
and
f\in V
, by Lemma
3.34
,
one has

\left(P_{+}S_{s}^{*}\right)\left(P_{+}S_{t}^{*}\right)=P_{+}S_{(s+t)}^{*}.

Since
\left\lVert P_{+}S_{t}^{*}\right\rVert\leq 1
, this implies that

M_{t}\coloneqq(P_{+}S_{t}^{*})\big|_{V}^{V}

defines a one-parameter semigroup of contractions. Therefore, by the Lumer–Phillips Theorem (cf.
[
EN00
, Thm. 3.15, Cor. 3.20]
), there exists an operator
A\in B(V)
such that

M_{t}=e^{tA}\quad\forall t\geq 0.

As an operator on a finite-dimensional vector space, the operator
A
has at least one eigenvalue with a corresponding eigenvector, so there exist
\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
f\in V\setminus\{0\}
such that
Af=\mu f
. This implies that

e^{t\mu}f=e^{tA}f=M_{t}f\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Applying Fourier transform, for all
t\geq 0
, we get

e^{t\mu}(\mathcal{F}f)(p)=(\mathcal{F}M_{t}f)(p)=(\mathcal{F}f)(p+t)

for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. This implies that there exists a
\widetilde{v}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(\mathcal{F}f)(p)=\begin{cases}e^{p\mu}\widetilde{v}&\text{for }p>0\\
0&\text{for }p\leq 0,\end{cases}

using that
f\in V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, which yields that
\mathcal{F}f
is supported on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then the fact that
\mathcal{F}f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
implies
\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits\mu<0
. Setting
\omega=-i\overline{\mu}
we therefore have
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Also we set
v\coloneqq\sqrt{2\pi}\cdot\widetilde{v}\neq 0
. Then, using that
f\in V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, inverse Fourier transformation gives us

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{ipz}e^{p\mu}\,dp\cdot\widetilde{v}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{ip(z-\overline{\omega})}\,dp\cdot v=\frac{i}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{z-\overline{\omega}}\cdot v=Q_{\omega}(z)\cdot v.\qed

We will use this lemma to link
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces to so-called Blaschke–Potapov products, which are defined as follows:

Definition 3.36
.

(cf.
[
CHL22
]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space.

(a)

For
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
we define
\varphi_{\omega}\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
(cf. Definition
A.31
) by

\varphi_{\omega}(z)\coloneqq\frac{z-\omega}{z-\overline{\omega}}.

(b)

A
Blaschke–Potapov factor
is a function of the form

\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))

for some
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and some finite rank projection
P\in B(\mathcal{H})
.

(c)

For
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{H}
we define the corresponding Blaschke–Potapov factor

??\coloneqq(\textbf{1}-P_{{\mathbb{C}}v})+\varphi_{\omega}P_{{\mathbb{C}}v}.

(d)

A
finite Blaschke–Potapov product
is a function of the form

u\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))

with
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, finite rank projections
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}\in B(\mathcal{H})
and
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
.

(e)

The
degree
of a finite Blaschke–Potapov product

\varphi=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))

is defined as

??\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits P_{n}\mathcal{H}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

Remark 3.37
.

(a)

In the case
\mathcal{H}={\mathbb{C}}
Blaschke–Potapov factors and finite Blaschke–Potapov products are precisely Blaschke factors and finite Blaschke products (cf. Definition
A.33
).

(b)

For a finite-dimensional subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{H}
,
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
we have

u\left(\varphi_{\omega}P_{V}+(1-P_{V})\right)=\left(\varphi_{\omega}P_{uV}+(1-P_{uV})\right)u.

This identity shows that the product of two finite Blaschke–Potapov products is again a finite
Blaschke–Potapov
product.

(c)

We will see that the degree is well defined in Proposition
3.39
.

(d)

By definition, for two finite Blaschke–Potapov products
\varphi
and
\psi
, one has

\deg(\varphi\cdot\psi)=\deg(\varphi)+\deg(\psi).

(e)

If
P
is a finite rank projection and
(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
is an orthogonal basis of
P\mathcal{H}
, then

\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{\omega,b_{j}}.

Therefore every finite Blaschke–Potapov product
\varphi
is of the form

\varphi=u\prod_{n=1}^{\deg(\varphi)}\varphi_{\omega_{n},v_{n}}

with
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{\deg(\varphi)}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{\deg(\varphi)}\in\mathcal{H}
and
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
.

The following lemma will be useful later when analyzing finite Blaschke–Potapov products:

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space,
v\in\mathcal{H}
and
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then

\left(Q_{\omega}\cdot v\right)^{\perp_{+}}=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Proof.

For
v=0
both sides are equal to
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, so we can assume that
v\neq 0
. One has
f\in\left(Q_{\omega}\cdot v\right)^{\perp_{+}}
, if and only if

0=\left\langle Q_{\omega}\cdot v\middle|f\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|f(\omega)\right\rangle.

Writing
f=\widetilde{f}\oplus f_{v}
with
\widetilde{f}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}\ominus{\mathbb{C}}v)
and
f_{v}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}v)\cong H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, this is equivalent to the function
f_{v}
being zero in
\omega
, so, by Proposition
A.35
,
f_{v}=\varphi_{\omega}\cdot g
for some
g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Therefore

\left(Q_{\omega}\cdot v\right)^{\perp_{+}}=(\textbf{1}-P_{{\mathbb{C}}v})H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})+\varphi_{\omega}P_{{\mathbb{C}}v}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).\qed

Using this lemma, we can connect
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspaces to Blaschke–Potapov products:

Proposition 3.39
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. Then
V
is a finite-dimensional
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace, if and only if there exists a finite Blaschke–Potapov product
\varphi
such that

V=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}

and in this case

\deg(\varphi)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits V.

Proof.

We first prove by induction over
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, that every
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
with
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits V=N
is of the form
V=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}
for some finite Blaschke–Potapov product
\varphi
with
\deg(\varphi)=N
. For
N=0
we have

V=\{0\}=\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}=\left(M_{\textbf{1}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}

with
\deg(\textbf{1})=0
.

We now assume that the statement is true for some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and let
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
be a
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable
subspace with
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits V=N+1
. Then, by Lemma
3.35
, there exist
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
with
Q_{\omega}\cdot v\in V
. Then, by Lemma
3.38
, we get

V^{\perp_{+}}\subseteq({\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)^{\perp_{+}}=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Using that
M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{-1}=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}
, this yields

M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V^{\perp_{+}}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Now, we set

W\coloneqq\left(M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V^{\perp_{+}}\right)^{\perp_{+}}.

Then

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=P_{+}\left(M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}P_{+}V^{\perp}\right)^{\perp}=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}\left(P_{+}V^{\perp}\right)^{\perp}=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}\left(V+H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{H})\right)=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V.

So, for
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, by Lemma
3.34
, one has

\displaystyle P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}W

\displaystyle=P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}\cdot\varphi}^{*}V

\displaystyle=P_{+}M_{\varphi\cdot\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}V\subseteq P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V=W

and therefore
W
is
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable. Further, using that

M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}Q_{\omega}(z)\cdot v=\frac{z-\overline{\omega}}{z-\omega}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{z-\overline{\omega}}\cdot v=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{z-\omega}\cdot v=Q_{\overline{\omega}}(z)\cdot v,

we have

\displaystyle M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}(V\ominus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)\oplus M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}({\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}(V\ominus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\overline{\omega}}\cdot v.

Therefore, since
Q_{\overline{\omega}}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
and

V\ominus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v\subseteq({\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)^{\perp_{+}}=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})

we get

\displaystyle W=P_{+}M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}V=P_{+}\left(M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}(V\ominus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\overline{\omega}}\cdot v\right)=M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}^{*}(V\ominus{\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v),

which implies

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits W=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits V-\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\left({\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega}\cdot v\right)=(N+1)-1=N.

We, therefore, can apply the assumption of our induction and get a finite Blaschke–Potapov product
\psi
with
\deg(\psi)=N
such that

W=\left(M_{\psi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}
. Setting
\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{\omega,v}\cdot\psi
, we get

\deg(\varphi)=\deg(\varphi_{\omega,v}\cdot\psi)=\deg(\psi)+\deg(\varphi_{\omega,v})=N+1

and

V=\left(M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}W^{\perp_{+}}\right)^{\perp_{+}}=\left(M_{\varphi_{\omega,v}}M_{\psi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}.

For the converse implication, we assume that
\varphi
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product, so

\varphi=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}\varphi_{\omega_{n},v_{n}}

for some
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\mathcal{H}
and
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
. Setting

W_{n}\coloneqq u\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{\omega_{j},v_{j}}\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),\qquad n=0,\dots,N,

we have
W_{0}\supseteq W_{1}\supseteq\dots\supseteq W_{N}
and

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits(W_{n}^{\perp}\cap W_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\left(u\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{\omega_{j},v_{j}}\cdot\left(\left(\varphi_{\omega_{n},v_{n}}\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp}\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits(M_{\varphi_{\omega_{n},v_{n}}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}))^{\perp_{+}}=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\left({\mathbb{C}}Q_{\omega_{n}}\cdot v_{n}\right)=1,

for
n=1,\dots,N
, using Lemma
3.38
in the penultimate step. Then, for
V\coloneqq\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}
, we have

V=W_{N}^{\perp}\cap W_{0}=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{N}\left(W_{n}^{\perp}\cap W_{n-1}\right)

and therefore

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits V

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\left(W_{n}^{\perp}\cap W_{n-1}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}1=N=\deg(\varphi)<\infty.

It remains to show that
V
is a
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace, but this follows by Corollary
3.33
.
∎

Finally, we will provide a way to calculate the degree of a finite Blaschke–Potapov product. For this, we need the following definition:

Definition 3.310
.

For a non-empty open subset
U\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
,
\xi\in U
and
f\in\mathcal{O}(U\setminus\{\xi\})
with Laurent series

f(z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{n}(z-\xi)^{n}

we define the
order of
f
in
\xi
by

??(f,\xi)\coloneqq\min\{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}:a_{n}\neq 0\}

if that minimum exists. Else we set

\mathrm{Ord}(f,\xi)\coloneqq-\infty

if
a_{n}\neq 0
for arbitrarily small
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and

\mathrm{Ord}(f,\xi)\coloneqq\infty

if
a_{n}=0
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Further, for an open subset
U\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}
with
\infty\in U
and
f\in\mathcal{O}(U\setminus\{\infty\})
, we set

\mathrm{Ord}(f,\infty)=\mathrm{Ord}\left(z\mapsto f\left(\frac{1}{z}\right),0\right).

Remark 3.311
.

One can easily see that

\mathrm{Ord}(f\cdot g,\xi)=\mathrm{Ord}(f,\xi)+\mathrm{Ord}(g,\xi)

if

\mathrm{Ord}(f,\xi)\neq-\infty\neq\mathrm{Ord}(g,\xi).

This especially implies that

\mathrm{Ord}(c\cdot f,\xi)=\mathrm{Ord}(f,\xi)

for every constant
c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.

Proposition 3.312
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
\varphi
be a finite Blaschke–Potapov product in
H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Then

\deg(\varphi)=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi,\xi).

Proof.

For a Blaschke–Potapov factor

\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)

we have

\det(\varphi_{\omega}(z)P+(1-P))=\left(\frac{z-\omega}{z-\overline{\omega}}\right)^{\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits P\mathcal{H}},

so

\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)),\xi\right)=\begin{cases}\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits P\mathcal{H}&\text{if }\xi=\omega\\
0&\text{if }\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{\omega\}\end{cases}

and therefore

\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)),\xi)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits P\mathcal{H}.

Now, for a finite Blaschke–Potapov product

\varphi=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))

and
\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(u\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))\right),\xi\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det(u)\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))),\xi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})),\xi\right),

using that
\det(u)\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})),\xi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})),\xi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits P_{n}\mathcal{H}=\deg(\varphi).\qed

3.3.2
The kernel space

In this subsection, given a regular Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
, we will apply our results obtained in the last subsection to the kernel space
\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
appearing in Theorem
2.25
. We first prove that, in fact,
\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
is a
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable subspace:

Lemma 3.313
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
the space
\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
is
M_{H^{\infty}}^{*}
-stable.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
g\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
. Then, for
f\in D(M_{F})
, we have

\left\langle P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}g\middle|(\lambda\textbf{1}-M_{F})f\right\rangle=\left\langle g\middle|M_{\varphi}(\lambda\textbf{1}-M_{F})f\right\rangle=\left\langle g\middle|(\lambda\textbf{1}-M_{F})M_{\varphi}f\right\rangle=\left\langle(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*})g\middle|M_{\varphi}f\right\rangle=0,

where we use that
M_{\varphi}f\in D(F)
with
M_{F}(M_{\varphi}f)=M_{\varphi}M_{F}f
. So
P_{+}M_{\varphi}^{*}g\in\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
.
∎

This lemma together with Corollary
3.33
implies that there exists a function
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
such that

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}.

We want to give an explicit formula for
\varphi
. For this, we need the following lemmata:

Lemma 3.314
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be a regular Pick function. Then

\Phi_{F}:L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H})),\quad f\mapsto f\circ F_{*}

is a well-defined linear contraction.

Proof.

For a function
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
the expression
f\circ F_{*}
makes sense as an element in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
, since for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
the boundary value
F_{*}(x)
is self-adjoint and therefore has real eigenvalues. Also it is clear that this map is linear and satisfies
\left\lVert\Phi_{F}\right\rVert\leq 1
since
\left\lVert f\circ F_{*}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. It remains to show that this map factorizes over the quotient map

q:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})/\penalty\mathcal{N}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H})),

where, by
\mathcal{N}
, we denote the subspace of almost everywhere null functions. So let
f\in\mathcal{N}
. Then there is a Lebesgue zero-set
M
such that
f\big|_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus M}\equiv 0
and

\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:f\circ F_{*}\neq 0\}\subseteq\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:{\rm Spec}(F_{*}(x))\cap M\neq\emptyset\}=D(F,M),

which, by Theorem
3.110
is a Lebesgue zero-set. Therefore
f\circ F_{*}=0\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
.
∎

Lemma 3.315
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be complex Hilbert spaces with
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}<\infty
and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular. Further, let

\psi:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})

be a unitary map such that

\psi\circ U^{F}_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi\qquad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},

where
S_{t}\coloneqq M_{e^{it\mathrm{Id}}}
. Then, for every
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, one has

\psi\circ M_{h\circ F_{*}}=M_{h}\circ\psi.

Proof.

The linear maps

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})),\quad h\mapsto\psi^{-1}\circ M_{h}\circ\psi

and

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})),\quad h\mapsto M_{h\circ F_{*}}

are both bounded, where for the boundedness of the second one we use Lemma
3.314
. Further, they coincide on the subset
\{e^{it\mathrm{Id}}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\subseteq L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Since these functions span a weakly dense subspace by Proposition
A.115
, the two functions coincide, so, for every
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, we have

\psi^{-1}\circ M_{h}\circ\psi=M_{h\circ F_{*}}.\qed

Proposition 3.316
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=(M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}))^{\perp_{+}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
and
\psi:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
be an isomorphism between
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S)
. Then, by Lemma
2.24
, we have

\psi\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K},

so, by Lemma
3.38
and Lemma
3.315
, we have

\displaystyle\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)^{\perp_{+}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{-1}(Q_{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{+}}=\psi^{-1}M_{\varphi_{\lambda}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle=M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}\psi^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),

which proves the equation since the kernel is a closed subspace.
∎

Corollary 3.317
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then, one has

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)<\infty,

if and only if
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product and in this case

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=\deg(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.313
and Proposition
3.39
we have that

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)<\infty,

if and only if there exists a finite Blaschke–Potapov product
\varphi
with
\deg(\varphi)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)
such that

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=\left(M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}}.

By Proposition
3.316
we also have

\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)=\left(M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)^{\perp_{+}},

so we get

M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})

and therefore, by Corollary
A.114
, we have

\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F=\varphi\cdot u

for some unitary operator
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
. This shows that

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)<\infty,

if and only if
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product with

\deg(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F)=\deg(\varphi)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right).\qed

With these results, we get a characterization of the multiplicity space of a ROPG infinitesimally generated by a Pick function:

Proposition 3.318
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
. Then
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}<\infty
, if and only if for one – or equivalently for all –
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
the function
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product. In this case

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\deg(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F)=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F,\xi)\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Proof.

This follows immediately by Corollary
3.317
and Proposition
3.312
, since by Theorem
2.25
we have

\mathcal{K}\cong\ker\left(\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}-M_{F}^{*}\right)\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.\qed

3.3.3
Holomorphic extensions

To avoid dealing with infinities, in the following, we switch from the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
to the unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
via Cayley transform, so we consider the holomorphic map:

??:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{D}},\quad z\mapsto\frac{z-i}{z+i}.

Setting
f_{\lambda}\coloneqq\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
for a regular Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we would like to write the sum appearing in Proposition
3.318
, using the argument principle, as

\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F,\xi)=\sum_{\omega\in{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{Ord}(f_{\lambda},\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{f_{\lambda}\circ\gamma}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

for some closed path
\gamma
in
{\mathbb{C}}
enclosing the closed unit disc
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
. For this to be true, we would need that the function
f_{\lambda}
has a holomorphic extension to a simply connected domain containing the closed unit disc
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
. We will later see that the right notion of extendability is the following:

Definition 3.319
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. We call
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}

\lambda
-extendable
, if there exists a finite set
E_{\lambda}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
such that

\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to B(\mathcal{H})

can be holomorphically extended to a function
F_{\lambda}:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus E_{\lambda}\to B(\mathcal{H})
, such that, for every
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
, the matrix coefficient

{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus E_{\lambda}\ni z\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|F_{\lambda}(z)w\right\rangle\in{\mathbb{C}}

is meromorphic. For a
\lambda
-extendable Pick function
F
, we write
??
for the minimal choice of
E_{\lambda}
and
??
for the corresponding extension.

To classify the
\lambda
-extendable Pick functions, we need the following lemmata:

Lemma 3.320
.

(cf.
[
CHL22
, Cor. 3.7, Def. 3.3]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and let
\varphi:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to B(\mathcal{H})
be an inner function. Then
\varphi
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product, if and only if there is a finite Blaschke product
\theta\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
(cf. Definition
A.33
) such that

M_{\theta}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Lemma 3.321
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
be normal. Then, for every
S\in B(\mathcal{H})
, one has

{\rm Spec}(A+S)\subseteq{\rm Spec}(A)+U_{\leq\left\lVert S\right\rVert}(0).

Proof.

Let

\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus({\rm Spec}(A)+U_{\leq\left\lVert S\right\rVert}(0)).

Then

\mathop{{\rm dist}}\nolimits(\mu,{\rm Spec}(A))>\left\lVert S\right\rVert.

Since
A
and therefore
A-\mu\textbf{1}
is normal, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert(A-\mu\textbf{1})^{-1}\right\rVert^{-1}

\displaystyle=\min\{|\lambda|:\lambda\in{\rm Spec}(A-\mu\textbf{1})\}

\displaystyle=\min\{|\lambda-\mu|:\lambda\in{\rm Spec}(A)\}=\mathop{{\rm dist}}\nolimits(\mu,{\rm Spec}(A))>\left\lVert S\right\rVert,

which implies that

(A-\mu\textbf{1})+S=(A+S)-\mu\textbf{1}

is invertible and therefore
\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\rm Spec}(A+S)
.
∎

We now can formulate our theorem characterizing
\lambda
-extendable Pick functions using the following definition:

Definition 3.322
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. We call a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}

rational
, if
there are
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
,
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}

such that

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}

and we call

??\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits(D)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits(A_{j})

the
degree of
F
. Further, we write
??
for the set of rational Pick functions and set

\deg(F)\coloneqq\infty

for all
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
.

Theorem 3.323
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product.

(b)

F
is
\lambda
-extendable.

(c)

F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):

Let

\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\varphi_{\omega_{n}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n}))

with
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, projections
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}\in B(\mathcal{H})
and
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
. All the functions
\varphi_{\omega_{n}}
can be holomorphically extended to
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{\overline{\omega_{n}}\}
, so setting
E\coloneqq\{\overline{\omega_{1}},\dots,\overline{\omega_{N}}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
, we get that
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
can be holomorphically extended to a function on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus E
. To get the holomorphic extension in
\infty
, we notice that

(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F)\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\frac{1}{z}-\omega_{n}}{\frac{1}{z}-\overline{\omega_{n}}}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})\right)=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1-\omega_{n}z}{1-\overline{\omega_{n}}z}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})\right),

which shows that
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
can be holomorphically extended to
\infty
with the value

F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(\infty)=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1-\omega_{n}\cdot 0}{1-\overline{\omega_{n}}\cdot 0}P_{n}+(1-P_{n})\right)=u\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(P_{n}+(1-P_{n})\right)=u.

That, for every
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
, the matrix coefficient

{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus E_{\lambda}\ni z\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|F(z)w\right\rangle\in{\mathbb{C}}

is meromorphic, follows from the fact that all the functions
\varphi_{\omega_{n}}
,
n=1,\dots,N
, are meromorphic.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):

Since
F
is
\lambda
-extendable, all the matrix coefficients of
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
are meromorphic and therefore rational functions. Since their poles are located in
E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
, we can write

F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(z)=\left(\prod_{\omega\in E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}(z-\omega)^{-m_{\omega}}\right)P(z),

where
P
is a matrix-valued function whose entries are polynomials and
m_{\omega}\in{\mathbb{N}}
for every
\omega\in E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
. Since
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
can be holomorphically extended to
\infty
, we know that, for all
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
, the degree of the polynomial
{\mathbb{C}}\ni z\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|P(z)w\right\rangle
is at most
\sum_{\omega\in E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}^{N}m_{\omega}
. This shows that for the function

g(z)=\left(\prod_{\omega\in E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}(z-\overline{\omega})^{-m_{\omega}}\right)P(z)

one has
g\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Further, defining the Blaschke product

\theta\coloneqq\prod_{\omega\in E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}\varphi_{\overline{\omega}}^{m_{\omega}}

we have
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}=\theta g
. We now define
h\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
by
h(z)\coloneqq g(\overline{z})^{*}
. Then, for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(x)=\theta(x)h(x)^{*}.

Since
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
and
\theta
have unitary boundary values, also
h
has unitary boundary values and therefore
\theta=F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}h
, which yields

M_{\theta}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=M_{F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}M_{h}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq M_{F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=M_{\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

By Lemma
3.320
this implies that
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a finite Blaschke–Potapov product.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c):

Since
F
is
\lambda
-extendable, all the matrix coefficients of
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
are meromorphic and therefore rational functions. This implies that also
\det\circ\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}-\textbf{1}\right)
is a rational function and therefore the set

T\coloneqq\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:1\in{\rm Spec}\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(x)\right)\}

is finite. For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus T
, we have
\varphi_{\lambda}^{-1}\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(x)\right)\in B(\mathcal{H})
,
which implies that the limit

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F(x+i\varepsilon)=\varphi_{\lambda}^{-1}\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(x+i\varepsilon)\right)=\varphi_{\lambda}^{-1}\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(x)\right)

exists. This implies that for every
v\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\,\left\langle v\middle|F(x+i\varepsilon)v\right\rangle\neq\infty,

so the measure corresponding to the Pick function

{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\ni z\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|F(z)v\right\rangle,

by Theorem
A.46
is supported on the finite set
T
. Together with
[
Sc20
, Thm. 4.3.4]
, this implies that there are operators
(A_{t})_{t\in T}\subseteq S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and an operator
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
and an operator
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
such that

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{t\in T}\frac{1}{t-z}A_{t},

which implies that
F
is a rational Pick function.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(b):

Let
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
, so

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}

for some
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
,
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
. By replacing
F
with
F\circ m^{-1}
, where
m
is an automorphism of the Riemann sphere with
m({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, chosen in a suitable way, for example as the Möbius transformation

m(z)=\frac{1}{\xi-z}\quad\text{for }\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\},

we can, without loss of generality, assume that
D=0
, so

F(z)=C+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}.

Then
F
can be holomorphically extended to a function

\widetilde{F}:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}\to B(\mathcal{H}).

Now, for
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we define

F_{k}:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k-1},\lambda_{k+1},\lambda_{m}\}\to B(\mathcal{H}),\quad z\mapsto C+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}

and set

K\coloneqq\max\left\{\left\lVert F_{k}(\lambda_{k})\right\rVert:k=1,\dots,m\right\}.

Since the spectra
({\rm Spec}(A_{k}))_{k=1,\dots,m}
are all finite, there are
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}>0
such that

{\rm Spec}(A_{k})\subseteq\{0\}\cup[a_{k},\infty)\quad\forall k\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

We now choose
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{m}>0
such that the sets

U_{\varepsilon_{1}}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,U_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\lambda_{m})

are pairwise disjoint and

\left\lVert F_{k}(z)-F_{k}(\lambda_{k})\right\rVert\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}\right|a_{k}\geq 2K+4

for every
z\in U_{\varepsilon_{k}}(\lambda_{k})\setminus\{\lambda_{k}\}
. Then, for every
z\in U_{\varepsilon_{k}}(\lambda_{k})\setminus\{\lambda_{k}\}
, one has

\left\lVert\widetilde{F}(z)-\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}A_{k}\right\rVert=\left\lVert F_{k}(z)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert F_{k}(\lambda_{k})\right\rVert+\left\lVert F_{k}(z)-F_{k}(\lambda_{k})\right\rVert\leq K+1

and

{\rm Spec}\left(\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}A_{k}\right)\subseteq\{0\}\cup{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{\leq\left|\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}\right|a_{k}}(0)\subseteq\{0\}\cup{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{\leq 2K+4}(0).

Therefore, by Lemma
3.321
, using that
A_{k}
is self-adjoint and therefore
\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}A_{k}
is normal, we get

\displaystyle{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{F}(z))

\displaystyle\subseteq{\rm Spec}\left(\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{k}}A_{k}\right)+U_{\leq K+1}(0)\subseteq\left(\{0\}\cup{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{\leq 2K+4}(0)\right)+U_{\leq K+1}(0)

\displaystyle=U_{\leq K+1}(0)\cup{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{\leq(2K+4)-(K+1)}(0)=U_{\leq K+1}(0)\cup{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{\leq K+3}(0)

\displaystyle\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus U_{1}(-(K+2)i).

This shows, that, denoting for
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\omega}
the holomorphic extension of the function
\varphi_{\omega}
to
{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus\{\overline{\omega}\}
, the function
\widetilde{\varphi}_{(K+2)i}\circ\widetilde{F}
is bounded on the open set

U\coloneqq\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}U_{\varepsilon_{k}}(\lambda_{k})\setminus\{\lambda_{k}\}.

Further, for every
k=1,\dots,m
, the set
\{\lambda_{k}\}\cup U
is an open neighbourhood of
\lambda_{k}
. Then Riemann’s Theorem on removable singularities implies that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{(K+2)i}\circ\widetilde{F}
can be holomorphically extended to
\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
, which gives us a holomorphic extension

H:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus I\to B(\mathcal{H})

of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{(K+2)i}\circ\widetilde{F}
and therefore of
\varphi_{(K+2)i}\circ F
, where

I\coloneqq\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:-(K+2)i\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{F}(z))\right\}.

We now notice that
z\in I
, if and only if

\det\left(\widetilde{F}(z)+(K+2)i\right)=0,

which, since
\det
is a polynomial in its entries and
\widetilde{F}
has meromorphic matrix coefficients, implies that
I
is finite. Since

{\rm Spec}(H(z))={\rm Spec}(\varphi_{(K+2)i}(F(z)))=\varphi_{(K+2)i}\left({\rm Spec}(F(z))\right)\subseteq\varphi_{(K+2)i}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{D}}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

by Theorem
3.17
and

{\rm Spec}(H(x))\subseteq\partial{\mathbb{D}}\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}

because
F
has self-adjoint boundary values, we have
I\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
.

Analogously, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one argues that

J_{\lambda}\coloneqq\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\overline{\lambda}\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{F}(z))\right\}

is a finite subset of
{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
. This implies that

H_{\lambda}:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus(I\cup J_{\lambda})\to B(\mathcal{H}),\quad z\mapsto(\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}_{(K+2)i}^{-1}\circ H)(z)

defines an holomorphic extension of
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
to
{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus(I\cup J_{\lambda})
with
I\cup J_{\lambda}
being finite.
Since
\varphi_{\lambda}
and all matrix coefficients of
F
are rational functions, also all matrix coefficients of
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
are rational functions, which implies that
F
is
\lambda
-extendable. ∎

Corollary 3.324
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
. Then
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}<\infty
, if and only if
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
. In this case, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, there exists
R_{\lambda}>1
such that the function

f_{\lambda}\coloneqq\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{C}}

has a holomorphic extension
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}:R_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and then for every
r\in(1,R_{\lambda})
one has

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

for the closed path

\gamma_{r}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\quad t\mapsto re^{it}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.318
and Theorem
3.323
we immediately get that
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}<\infty
, if and only if
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
and that in this case, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F,\xi).

Further, by Theorem
3.323
, the function
F
is
\lambda
-extendable, which immediately implies that there exists a finite subset
E\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
such that the function
f_{\lambda}
can be holomorphically extended to
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus E
. Setting

R_{\lambda}\coloneqq 1+\mathop{{\rm dist}}\nolimits(E,\overline{{\mathbb{D}}})\in(1,\infty),

we have that
R_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus E
and therefore there exists a holomorphic extension
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}
of
f_{\lambda}
to
R_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}
. For this extension, we get

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F,\xi)=\sum_{\omega\in{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1},\omega)=\sum_{\omega\in{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{Ord}(\widetilde{f}_{\lambda},\omega).

Further, by Proposition
3.318
the function
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
is a Blaschke–Potapov product and therefore no holomorphic extension of
\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
has a zero on
{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\setminus{\mathbb{D}}
, which implies that
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}
has no zeros on
R_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}\setminus{\mathbb{D}}
. For
r\in(1,R_{\lambda})
the argument principle then gives us

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\sum_{\omega\in{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{Ord}(\widetilde{f}_{\lambda},\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial(r{\mathbb{D}})}\frac{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\xi)}{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}(\xi)}\,d\xi=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

for the closed path

\gamma_{r}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\quad t\mapsto re^{it}.\qed

3.3.4
The multiplicity space and the degree of Pick functions

In this subsection, we will provide a way to express the degree of a regular Pick function as an integral in a similar fashion as the integral

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

appearing in Corollary
3.324
, and finally, we will compare these two integrals to obtain an explicit formula for the multiplicity space of ROPGs that are infinitesimally generated by a Pick function. We start with the following lemma:

Lemma 3.325
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
be regular. Let
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
,
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and let
\widetilde{F}
be the holomorphic extension of
F
to
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
. Then, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}),x\right)=\begin{cases}-\mathrm{rank}(D)&\text{if }x=\infty\\
-\mathrm{rank}(A_{k})&\text{if }x=\lambda_{k}\\
0&\text{if }x\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}.\end{cases}

Proof.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
, we have
\widetilde{F}(x)\in S(\mathcal{H})
and therefore
\det(\widetilde{F}(x)-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1})\neq 0
, so
\mathrm{Ord}(\det\circ(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}),x)=0
.

For
x=\infty
, let
P
be the projection onto
\ker(D)
and set

Q(z)\coloneqq P+\frac{1}{z}(\textbf{1}-P)

Then

\det(Q(z))=\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)^{\mathrm{rank}(D)},

so

\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ Q,\infty\right)=\mathrm{rank}(D).

Further, defining the partial isometries

p\coloneqq P\big|^{\ker(D)}:\mathcal{H}\to\ker(D)\quad\text{and}\quad q\coloneqq(1-P)\big|^{\ker(D)^{\perp}}:\mathcal{H}\to\ker(D)^{\perp}

and using that
Q(z)\cdot zD=D=(1-P)D
, we have

\displaystyle\det\left(Q(z)\left(\widetilde{F}(z)-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(Q(z)\left(C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(Q(z)\left(C+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)+(1-P)D\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{|z|\to\infty}\det\left(P(C-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1})+(1-P)D\right)=\det(p(C-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1})p^{*})\cdot\det(qDq^{*})

\displaystyle=\det(pCp^{*}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1})\cdot\det(qDq^{*})\neq 0,

where the last inequality follows since
\det(qDq^{*})\neq 0
by definition of
q
and
\det(pCp^{*}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1})\neq 0
follows by the fact that
pCp^{*}
is self-adjoint and therefore just has real eigenvalues. This implies that

\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(Q\cdot\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\right),\infty\right)=0

and therefore

\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right),\infty\right)=-\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ Q,\infty\right)=-\mathrm{rank}(D).

Now, for
x=\lambda_{k}
, we consider the Möbius transform

m(z)\coloneqq\lambda_{k}-\frac{1}{z}.

Then, using that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\left(\lambda_{k}-\frac{1}{z}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{z}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}\cdot\frac{z}{z+\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}}{z+\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}-\frac{\frac{1}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}}{z+\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}+\frac{\frac{1}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}}{\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}-z},

we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{F}\circ m)(z)

\displaystyle=C+\left(\lambda_{k}-\frac{1}{z}\right)D+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\left(\lambda_{k}-\frac{1}{z}\right)}A_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(C+\lambda_{k}D+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}}A_{j}\right)+zA_{k}+\frac{1}{0-z}D+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}\frac{\frac{1}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}}{\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}-z}A_{j},

so by the previous argument, using that
F\circ m
is again regular by Corollary
3.18
and Corollary
3.19
, we get

\displaystyle-\mathrm{rank}(A_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}\circ m-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right),\infty\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\circ m,m^{-1}(\lambda_{k})\right)=\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right),\lambda_{k}\right).\qed

Proposition 3.326
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
be regular and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then there exists
M_{\lambda}>1
and a meromorphic extension
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}
on
M_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}
of the function

g_{\lambda}\coloneqq\det\circ(F-\overline{\lambda})\circ h^{-1}:{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{C}}

such that
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}
has no zeros or poles on
M_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
. Then, for every
r\in(1,M_{\lambda})
, one has

\deg(F)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

for the closed path

\gamma_{r}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\quad t\mapsto re^{it}.

Proof.

Let
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
,
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and let
\widetilde{F}
be the holomorphic extension of
F
to
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
. Then all poles of
F
are located on
{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
and by Theorem
3.17
we have

{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This shows that defining the holomorphic extension

g\coloneqq\det\circ(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda})\circ h^{-1}:{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{1,h(\lambda_{1}),\dots,h(\lambda_{m})\}\to{\mathbb{C}}

of
g_{\lambda}
, all zeroes of
g
are located in
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
and since

\widetilde{F}(\overline{z})=\widetilde{F}(z)^{*},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

all poles of
g
are located in
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
. Further, since
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
the function
g
is rational and therefore the set
g^{-1}(\{0\})
is finite. The first statement then follows by setting

M_{\lambda}\coloneqq 1+\mathop{{\rm dist}}\nolimits\left({\mathbb{D}},g^{-1}(\{0\})\right)

and

\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\coloneqq g\big|_{M_{\lambda}{\mathbb{D}}}.

Now, using that
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}
has no zeros and that all its poles are located on
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
, for every
r\in(1,M_{\lambda})
, the argument principle gives us

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\omega\in\partial{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\circ h^{-1},\omega\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}}\mathrm{Ord}\left(\det\circ\left(\widetilde{F}-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right),x\right)=\mathrm{rank}(D)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{rank}(A_{j})=\deg(F),

using Lemma
3.325
in the penultimate step.
∎

Now, we can finally state our theorem:

Theorem 3.327
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular and let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{F})
. Then

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\deg F,

so

\mathcal{K}\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}^{\deg F}&\text{if }F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})\\
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{C}})&\text{if }F\notin\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}).\end{cases}

Proof.

We first consider the case
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. We choose
R_{\lambda}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}
as in Corollary
3.324
and
M_{\lambda}
and
\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}
as in Proposition
3.326
, so
\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}
is an extension of

f_{\lambda}\coloneqq\det\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1}

and

\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}
is an extension of

g_{\lambda}\coloneqq\det\circ(F-\overline{\lambda})\circ h^{-1}

and for every
r\in(1,R)
with
R\coloneqq\min\{R_{\lambda},M_{\lambda}\}
we have

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz\quad\text{and}\quad\deg(F)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

for the closed path

\gamma_{r}:[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\quad t\mapsto re^{it}.

We now fix
r\in(1,R)
and consider the homotopy of paths

H:[0,1]\times[0,2\pi]\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad(s,t)\mapsto\det\left(\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(re^{it})-s\textbf{1}\right),

where
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
is defined as in Definition
3.319
. For
z\in{\mathbb{D}}
we have

\det\left(\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)-0\cdot\textbf{1}\right)=\det\left(\left(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)\right)=f_{\lambda}(z)

and using that

\varphi_{\lambda}(\omega)-1=\frac{\omega-\lambda}{\omega-\overline{\lambda}}-1=\frac{\overline{\lambda}-\lambda}{\omega-\overline{\lambda}}

we get

\displaystyle\det\left(\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)-1\cdot\textbf{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)-\textbf{1}\right)=\det\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\lambda}-\lambda}{F-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)\right)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\lambda}-\lambda)^{\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}}\cdot\det\left(\left(\left(F-\overline{\lambda}\textbf{1}\right)\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)\right)^{-1}=(\overline{\lambda}-\lambda)^{\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}}\cdot g_{\lambda}^{-1}.

Therefore

H(0,\cdot)=\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad H(1,\cdot)=(\overline{\lambda}-\lambda)^{\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}}\cdot\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{r},

which especially implies that

H(\{1\}\times[0,2\pi])\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}.

We now show that also
H([0,1)\times[0,2\pi])\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. For this we notice that, for every
B(\mathcal{H})
-valued Blaschke-Potapov factor
\varphi_{\omega}P+(1-P)
and every
v\in\mathcal{H}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{-}\setminus\{\overline{\omega}\}
, one has

|\varphi_{\omega}(z)|=\left|\frac{z-\omega}{z-\overline{\omega}}\right|\geq 1

and therefore

\left\lVert(\varphi_{\omega}(z)P+(1-P))v\right\rVert^{2}=|\varphi_{\omega}(z)|^{2}\cdot\left\lVert Pv\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert(1-P)v\right\rVert^{2}\geq\left\lVert Pv\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert(1-P)v\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}.

This implies that also for the function
F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
, which is a finite Blaschke-Potapov product by Theorem
3.323
, one has
