\left\lVert F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(z)v\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert v\right\rVert

and therefore

{\rm Spec}\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}(z)\right)\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{D}}

for all
v\in\mathcal{H}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{-}\setminus E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}
.
This yields

{\rm Spec}\left(\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)\right)\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{D}}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\left(\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\cup h(E_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}})\right)\subseteq R{\mathbb{D}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},

so especially

[0,1)\cap{\rm Spec}\left(\left(F_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{min}}\circ h^{-1}\right)(z)\right)=\emptyset\quad\forall z\in R{\mathbb{D}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}.

This implies

H([0,1)\times[0,2\pi])\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},

so we have

H([0,1]\times[0,2\pi])\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}.

Then, by the homotopy invariance of the integral, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{H(0,\cdot)}\frac{1}{z}\,dz=\int_{H(1,\cdot)}\frac{1}{z}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{(\overline{\lambda}-\lambda)^{\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{H}}\cdot\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz=\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz=-\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz,

which yields

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{f}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{g}_{\lambda}\circ\gamma_{r}}\frac{1}{z}\,dz=\deg(F).

Now, in the case
F\notin\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})
, by definition we have
\deg(F)=\infty
and by Proposition
3.318
we also have
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\infty
. Further, by assumption, one has
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\cong L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
and since
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
is separable, also
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
has to be separable, which implies that also
\mathcal{K}
is separable and therefore
\mathcal{K}\cong\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

After giving a formula for the multiplicity space for complex ROPGs that are infinitesimally generated by Pick functions, we want to do the same for real ROPGs:

Theorem 3.328
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space,
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular with
F=F^{\sharp}
and let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},U^{F})
. Then

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\deg F,

so

\mathcal{K}\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{R}}^{\deg F}&\text{if }F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})\\
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{R}})&\text{if }F\notin\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}).\end{cases}

Proof.

This follows immediately by Theorem
3.111
and Theorem
3.327
.
∎

Remark 3.329
.

For a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
of the form

F(z)=C+zD+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

one has

F^{\sharp}(z)=\left(-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}C\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)+z\left(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}D\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{-\lambda_{j}-z}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}A_{j}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}\right),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Therefore the condition
F=F^{\sharp}
is fulfilled, if and only if
F
is of the form

F(z)=C+zD-\frac{1}{z}A_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-z}A_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{-\lambda_{j}-z}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}A_{j}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}\right),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

with
C\in S(\mathcal{H})
,
D\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
,
A_{0}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
,
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in S(\mathcal{H})_{+}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

such that

-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}C\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=C,\quad\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}D\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=D\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}A_{0}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=A_{0}.

Example 3.330
.

We consider the function

F:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to\mathrm{Mat}(2\times 2,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad z\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&z\end{pmatrix},

which is a function in
\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
, since for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, one has

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(z)\end{pmatrix}\geq 0

and for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
the matrix

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&x+i\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&x\end{pmatrix}

is self-adjoint. Further, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\det(t\textbf{1}-F(z))=(t-1)(t-z)-1=(t^{2}-t-1)+z(1-t),

which implies

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\det(t\textbf{1}-F(z)))=(1-t)\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(z),

so
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\det(t\textbf{1}-F(z)))=0
, if and only if
t=1
, but for
t=1
, we have

\det(\textbf{1}-F(z))=-1\neq 0,

so, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

{\rm Spec}(F(z))\cap{\mathbb{R}}=\emptyset.

Since by Proposition
A.42
, we have
{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
, we get
{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and therefore
F
is regular by Theorem
3.17
. Since

F(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}+z\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

by Theorem
3.327
, for the multiplicity space
\mathcal{K}
of
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}^{2}),U^{F})
we get

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=\deg(F)=\mathrm{rank}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=1

and therefore
\mathcal{K}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
.

One can also see this by considering the function
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. By Proposition
3.318
, the function
\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F
should be a Blaschke–Potapov product with

\deg(\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}=1.

In fact, one can calculate that

\varphi_{\lambda}\circ F=u\cdot\left(\varphi_{\omega}\cdot P+(\textbf{1}-P)\right)

for

\omega\coloneqq\lambda+\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\qquad u\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\overline{\lambda}}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in U({\mathbb{C}}^{2})

and the projection

P\coloneqq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{|1-\lambda|^{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{|1-\lambda|^{2}}&\frac{-1}{1-\lambda}\\
\frac{-1}{1-\overline{\lambda}}&1\end{pmatrix}\in B({\mathbb{C}}^{2})

with
\mathrm{rank}(P)=1
.

In Theorem
3.21
, we have seen that in the case of a scalar-valued Pick function, the dimension of the multiplicity space is exactly the number of copies of
{\mathbb{R}}
appearing in the boundary values. This also reflects in this matrix-valued example since every real number appears exactly one time as an eigenvalue on the boundary as the following graph shows:

-7

Plot of the eigenvalues of
x\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&x\end{pmatrix}

3.4
The degree of Pick functions and composition

In Corollary
3.19
we have seen that for regular Pick functions
f,g\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
and a regular Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
the composition
f\circ F\circ g
is again regular. By Theorem
3.327
we know that the dimension of the multiplicity space of the complex ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{f\circ F\circ g})
is given by
\deg(f\circ F\circ g)
. In this section, we want to answer the question of whether there is an easy way to calculate the degree
\deg(f\circ F\circ g)
if one knows the degrees of the Pick functions
f,g
and
F
. In other words: We will investigate how the degree behaves under composition. For this, we make some definitions:

Definition 3.41
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces. Then, we define the canonical unitary maps

??:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),\quad f\otimes v\mapsto f\cdot v

and

??\coloneqq T_{\mathcal{H}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}}\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\otimes\textbf{1}\right):L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}).

Finally, considering the unitary map

??:\mathcal{H}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H},\quad v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v,

we define

??\coloneqq\left(T_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(\textbf{1}\otimes\sigma_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{K}}\right)\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\otimes\textbf{1}\right):L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H}.

Lemma 3.42
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}
be complex Hilbert spaces and let
g\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Further, let

\psi:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})

be a unitary map such that

\psi\circ U^{g}_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi

for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for every
F\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
, one has

\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)=\left(E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)\circ M_{F\circ g_{*}}.

Proof.

For
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
and
v\in\mathcal{H}
, choosing a basis
(w_{j})_{j\in J}
of
\mathcal{K}
, we have

\displaystyle(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}})(f\otimes v)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(\textbf{1}\otimes\sigma_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{K}}\right)\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\right)\left(f\otimes v\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(\textbf{1}\otimes\sigma_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{K}}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{j\in J}\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\otimes w_{j}\otimes v\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(T_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{j\in J}\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\otimes v\otimes w_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot v\right)\otimes w_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot Fv\right)\otimes w_{j}.

Then, choosing a basis
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{H}
, we get

\displaystyle(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}})(f\otimes v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot Fv\right)\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot v_{i}\right)\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot v_{i}\right)\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle w_{j}\middle|f\right\rangle\cdot w_{j}\right)\otimes v_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot f\right)\otimes v_{i}\right).

Therefore, for
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
v\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right))(f\otimes v)

\displaystyle=(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}})((\psi f)\otimes v)

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot\psi f\right)\otimes v_{i}\right).

Then, using that
F
and therefore also
\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle
is a bounded function and applying Lemma
3.315
, yields

\displaystyle(\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right))(f\otimes v)

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|Fv\right\rangle\cdot\psi f\right)\otimes v_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\left(\psi(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|(F\circ g_{*})v\right\rangle\cdot f)\right)\otimes v_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{i\in I}\left(\left\langle v_{i}\middle|(F\circ g_{*})v\right\rangle\cdot f\right)\otimes v_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)\left((F\circ g_{*})\cdot f\cdot v\right)

\displaystyle=\left(E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\circ M_{F\circ g_{*}}\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}\right)\left(f\otimes v\right),

so

\left(M_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)=E_{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\circ M_{F\circ g_{*}}\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}.\qed

With help of this lemma, we can now prove our theorem:

Theorem 3.43
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
f,g\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular Pick functions. Then

\deg(f\circ F\circ g)=\deg(f)\cdot\deg(F)\cdot\deg(g).

Proof.

By Theorem
3.327
, there exist Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{K}_{f},\mathcal{K}_{F},\mathcal{K}_{g}
and unitary operators

\displaystyle\psi_{f}

\displaystyle:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f})

\displaystyle\psi_{F}

\displaystyle:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{F})

\displaystyle\psi_{g}

\displaystyle:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{g})

such that

\displaystyle\psi_{f}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)

\displaystyle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{f})\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{f}\circ U^{f}_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi_{f}

\displaystyle\psi_{F}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)

\displaystyle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{F})\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{F}\circ U^{F}_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi_{F}

\displaystyle\psi_{g}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)

\displaystyle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{g})\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{g}\circ U^{g}_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi_{g}

and

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{f}

\displaystyle=\deg(f)

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{F}

\displaystyle=\deg(F)

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{g}

\displaystyle=\deg(g).

The idea of the proof is now to have a sequence of complex ROPGs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
such that each one of them is equivalent to the next one and the first one is given by
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),U^{f\circ F\circ g})
and the last one is given by
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}),S_{t})
. The Hilbert spaces and isomorphisms of ROPGs as well as the unitary one-parameter groups are indicated in the following diagram (the spaces
\mathcal{E}_{+}
are omitted since they are given by the corresponding Hardy spaces):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e^{it(f\circ F\circ g)_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{g})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e^{it(f\circ F)_{*}}\otimes\textbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e^{itf_{*}}\otimes\textbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{F})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e^{itf_{*}}\otimes\textbf{1}\otimes\textbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{t}\otimes\textbf{1}\otimes\textbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{F}\otimes\textbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\otimes\textbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\otimes\textbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}}}

We will now carry out the details. First, we define the map

\psi_{0}\coloneqq I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{F}:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}).

Since all occurring maps are unitary and preserve the corresponding Hardy spaces,
\psi
is unitary and we have

\psi_{0}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}).

Further

\displaystyle S_{t}\circ\psi_{0}

\displaystyle=S_{t}\circ I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{F}=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(S_{t}\circ\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{F}

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(\psi_{f}\circ U^{f}_{t}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{F}=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ U^{f}_{t}\circ\psi_{F}

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}}^{\mathcal{K}_{F}}\circ\left(\psi_{f}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{F}\circ U^{f\circ F}_{t}=\psi_{0}\circ U^{f\circ F}_{t},

where the penultimate equality follows by Lemma
3.315
. We now define the map

\psi\coloneqq I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(\psi_{0}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right):L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g})

Since all occurring maps are unitary and preserve the corresponding Hardy spaces,
\psi
is unitary and we have

\psi\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right)=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g}).

We now show that
\psi
is also an intertwining operator. We have

\displaystyle S_{t}\circ\psi

\displaystyle=S_{t}\circ I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(\psi_{0}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(S_{t}\circ\psi_{0}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(\psi_{0}\circ U^{f\circ F}_{t}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(\psi_{0}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(U^{f\circ F}_{t}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{K}_{f}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{F}}^{\mathcal{K}_{g}}\circ\left(\psi_{0}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ\left(E_{\mathcal{K}_{g}}^{\mathcal{H}}\circ\left(\psi_{g}\otimes\textbf{1}\right)\circ T_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\right)\circ U^{f\circ F\circ g}_{t}=\psi\circ U^{f\circ F\circ g}_{t},

where the penultimate equality follows by Lemma
3.42
. The statement now follows with Theorem
3.327
by

\deg(f\circ F\circ g)=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{f}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{F}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{K}_{g})=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{f}\cdot\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{F}\cdot\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}_{g}=\deg(f)\cdot\deg(F)\cdot\deg(g).\qed

Now, the question is, composing with which functions from left and right leaves the degree of a Pick function unchanged. By Theorem
3.43
these are exactly the Pick functions
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
with
\deg(f)=1
(such a function is non-constant and therefore by Corollary
3.18
always regular). We will see that these are exactly the Möbius transformations:

Definition 3.44
.

We write
??\left({\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\right)
for the group of Möbius transformations of the Riemann sphere
{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}
, i.e. for the set of functions
m:{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}
such that there are
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
ad-bc=1
and

m(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}.

Further, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{m\in\mathrm{Mob}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\right):m({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{C}}_{+}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{m\in\mathrm{Mob}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{\infty}\right):(\exists a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}}):ad-bc=1\text{ and }m(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right\}.

Proposition 3.45
.

One has

\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\{f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}:\deg(f)=1\}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.327
, for
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
, one has that
\deg(f)=1
, if and only if
f
is of the form

f(z)=c+dz

with
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
d>0
or of the form

f(z)=c+\frac{a}{\lambda-z}=\frac{(c\lambda+a)-cz}{\lambda-z}

with
c,\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a>0
. In both cases
f\in\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Conversely, for
f\in\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, there exist
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
ad-bc=1
and

f(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}.

For
c\neq 0
, we have

f(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\frac{acz+bc}{cz+d}=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\left(a+\frac{bc-ad}{cz+d}\right)=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\left(a-\frac{1}{cz+d}\right)=\frac{a}{c}+\frac{\frac{1}{c^{2}}}{-\frac{d}{c}-z}

and for
c=0
, we have
ad=ad-bc=1
and therefore

f(z)=\frac{az+b}{d}=\frac{b}{d}+\frac{a}{d}z=\frac{b}{d}+a^{2}z,

so in every case
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
with
\deg(f)=1
.
∎

Corollary 3.46
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular.
Then, for
f,g\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\deg(f\circ F\circ g)=\deg(F),

if and only if
f,g\in\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

Proof.

This follows immediately by Proposition
3.45
and Theorem
3.43
.
∎

Corollary 3.47
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}
be regular.
Then, for
m,n\in\mathrm{Mob}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, one has

m\circ F\circ n\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})\Leftrightarrow F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}).

Proof.

By Corollary
3.46
and Theorem
3.327
, we have

m\circ F\circ n\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H})\Leftrightarrow\deg(m\circ F\circ n)<\infty\Leftrightarrow\deg(F)<\infty\Leftrightarrow F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{H}).\qed

4
Reflection positivity on the Hardy space

In the last two chapters, we were analyzing ROPGs. In this chapter, we will add another structure to them. Concretely we will consider quadruples
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
, where
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a ROPG and
\theta
is an involution on
\mathcal{E}
with certain compatibility conditions. At the beginning of this chapter, we will show that, to classify these reflection positive ROPGs, one needs to better understand so-called reflection positive Hilbert spaces, which we will introduce in the following section:

4.1
Reflection positive Hilbert spaces and ROPGs

Definition 4.11
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Def. 2.1.1, Def. 4.2.1]
)
Consider a triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{E}
, a closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
and a unitary involution
\theta\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
.

(a)

We call
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
a
reflection positive Hilbert space (??)
, if

\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle\geq 0\qquad\forall\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}.

(b)

We call a quadruple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
of a RPHS
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
and a strongly continuous unitary one-parameter group
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
a
reflection positive unitary one-parameter group
, if

U_{t}\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}\qquad\forall t\geq 0

and

\theta\circ U_{t}=U_{-t}\circ\theta\qquad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(c)

We call a quadruple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
a
reflection positive ROPG
, if
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
is a reflection positive unitary one-parameter group and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a ROPG.

(d)

We say that a reflection positive Hilbert space, unitary one-parameter group, or ROPG is real/complex, if
\mathcal{E}
is real/complex.

(e)

We say that two reflection positive ROPGs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
are equivalent, if there exists a unitary operator
\psi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that
\psi
is an isomorphism between the ROPGs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U^{\prime})
that additionally satisfies

\psi\circ\theta=\theta^{\prime}\circ\psi.

We call such an operator
\psi
an
isomorphism between
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},U^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
.

We first want to investigate the relation between real and complex reflection positive ROPGs. Here we have the following propositions:

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a real Hilbert space,
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
be a closed subspace and
\theta\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
be an involution. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a real RPHS, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},\theta_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex RPHS.

Proof.

Using that the scalar product on
\mathcal{E}
is real-valued and that
\theta
is a unitary involution, we get

\left\langle v\middle|\theta w\right\rangle=\left\langle\theta w\middle|v\right\rangle=\left\langle w\middle|\theta v\right\rangle\quad\forall v,w\in\mathcal{E}.

This yields

\left\langle(v+iw)\middle|\theta_{\mathbb{C}}(v+iw)\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+\left\langle w\middle|\theta w\right\rangle+i\left\langle v\middle|\theta w\right\rangle-i\left\langle w\middle|\theta v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+\left\langle w\middle|\theta w\right\rangle\quad\forall v,w\in\mathcal{E},

which immediately implies that

\left(\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}\right)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\left(\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta_{\mathbb{C}}\xi\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall\xi\in(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}\right).\qed

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a real Hilbert space,
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
be a closed subspace,
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
be a unitary one-parameter group and
\theta\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
be an involution. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
is a real reflection positive ROPG, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}},\theta_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex reflection positive ROPG.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.28
we have that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U)
is a real ROPG, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},U_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex ROPG. Further, by Proposition
4.12
, we have that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a real RPHS, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},\theta_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex RPHS. The statement then follows since

\theta_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}\theta_{\mathbb{C}}=(\theta U_{t}\theta)_{\mathbb{C}}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

and therefore

\theta_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{t})_{\mathbb{C}}\theta_{\mathbb{C}}=(U_{-t})_{\mathbb{C}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\theta U_{t}\theta=U_{-t}.\qed

This theorem shows that we can reduce our analysis of reflection positive ROPGs to the complex case. Given a complex reflection positive ROPG
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.16
), there exists a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\widetilde{\theta})
for some involution
\widetilde{\theta}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
. To classify the involutions
\widetilde{\theta}
for which
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\widetilde{\theta})
is a reflection positive ROPG, we need the following definitions:

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
X
be a set.

(a)

For function
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to X
we define the function

??f:{\mathbb{R}}\to X\quad x\mapsto f(-x).

(b)

For
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))
we set

??\coloneqq M_{h}\circ R\in B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})).

(c)

We define the involution

\flat:L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K})\right)\to L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K})\right),\quad??\left(x\right)\coloneqq(Rh)(x)^{*}=h\left(-x\right)^{*}.

(d)

We write
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))
for the set of functions
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))
such that
f(x)\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K})
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In the case of
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{C}}
we write
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})
for the set of functions
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cong L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B({\mathbb{C}}))
such that
f(x)\in{\mathbb{T}}\cong\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}})
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

The following results will show how these definitions relate to reflection positive ROPGs of the form
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta)
:

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
\theta\in B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}))
. Then

\theta\circ S_{t}=S_{-t}\circ\theta\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},

if and only if there exists
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))
such that
\theta=\theta_{h}
.

Proof.

We have

S_{t}\circ\theta=S_{t}\circ\left(\theta\circ R\right)\circ R=\left(\theta\circ R\right)\circ S_{t}\circ R+[S_{t},\theta\circ R]\circ R=\theta\circ S_{-t}+[S_{t},\theta\circ R]\circ R.

This implies that

\theta\circ S_{t}=S_{-t}\circ\theta\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},

if and only if

\theta\circ R\in S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}=M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))),

using Proposition
2.19
in the last step. This is equivalent to the existence of
h\in M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K})))
such that
\theta\circ R=M_{h}
, so
\theta=M_{h}\circ R=\theta_{h}
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}))
. Then
\theta_{h}
is a unitary involution, if and only if
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
.

Proof.

Since
R
is unitary, the operator
\theta_{h}
is unitary, if and only if
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))
. Further, we have

\theta_{h}\circ\theta_{h}=M_{h}\circ R\circ M_{h}\circ R=M_{h}\circ M_{Rh}\circ R\circ R=M_{h\cdot Rh},

which shows that
\theta_{h}
is an involution, if and only if one has
h\cdot Rh=\textbf{1}
, which is equivalent to

Rh=h^{-1}=h^{*}

and therefore to
h^{\flat}=h
.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
\theta\in B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}))
be a unitary involution. Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta)
is a reflection positive ROPG, if and only if there exists a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
such that

1.

\theta=\theta_{h}
.

2.

(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS.

Proof.

This follows immediately by Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.16
.
∎

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complex Hilbert space and
h,h^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
such that the quadruples
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h^{\prime}})
are reflection positive ROPGs. Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h^{\prime}})
are equivalent, if and only if there exists
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K})
such that
h^{\prime}=uhu^{*}
.

Proof.

If
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h^{\prime}})
are equivalent there exists a unitary operator
\psi\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}))
such that

\psi H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\qquad\psi\circ S_{t}=S_{t}\circ\psi\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi\circ\theta_{h}=\theta_{h^{\prime}}\circ\psi.

The last of these conditions implies that
\psi\in S({\mathbb{R}})^{\prime}
, so by Proposition
2.19
there exists a function
f\in M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K})))
such that
\psi=M_{f}
. The condition

\psi H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})

together with the unitarity of
\psi
then implies

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K})\quad\text{and}\quad M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},\mathcal{K}),

so, by Proposition
A.113
, we have

f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}))\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{K}))=B(\mathcal{K})\cdot\textbf{1},

using Corollary
A.112
in the last step. Therefore, since
\psi=M_{f}
is unitary, there exists
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K})
such that
f=u\cdot\textbf{1}
. This implies

\theta_{h^{\prime}}=\psi\circ\theta_{h}\circ\psi^{-1}=M_{u\cdot\textbf{1}}\circ M_{h}\circ R\circ M_{u^{*}\cdot\textbf{1}}=M_{uhu^{*}}\circ R=\theta_{uhu^{*}}

and therefore
h^{\prime}=uhu^{*}
.

If conversely
h^{\prime}=uhu^{*}
, then the operator
\psi\coloneqq M_{u\cdot\textbf{1}}
is an isomorphism between the reflection positive ROPGs
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h^{\prime}})
.
∎

With these results about our standard example, we get a normal form for complex reflection positive ROPGs:

Theorem 4.19
.

(Normal form for reflection positive ROPGs – complex version)

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
be a complex reflection positive ROPG. Then there exists a — up to isomorphism – unique complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a — up to conjugation with a unitary operator — unique function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\theta_{h})
is a reflection positive ROPG which is equivalent to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
.

Proof.

By the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.16
), there exists a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),S,\widetilde{\theta})
for some involution
\widetilde{\theta}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K})
. By Theorem
2.25
, this Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism. Further, by Proposition
4.17
, we have
\widetilde{\theta}=\theta_{h}
for some function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
. By Proposition
4.18
, this function is unique up to conjugation with a unitary operator.
∎

We now want to use this normal form for complex reflection positive ROPGs to also get a normal form for real reflection positive ROPGs. For this, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 4.110
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a real Hilbert space and
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\flat}
. Then
\theta_{h}
defines a unitary involution on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
, if and only if
h=h^{\sharp}
with

??(x)\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}h(-x)\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

denoting by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}
the complex conjugation on
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In this case
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S,\theta_{h})
is a real reflection positive ROPG, if and only if
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),S,\theta_{h})
is a complex reflection positive ROPG.

Proof.

One has

\theta_{h}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},

if and only if

\theta_{h}f=(\theta_{h}f)^{\sharp}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}R\theta_{h}f=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}\theta_{Rh}Rf=\theta_{h^{\sharp}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}Rf=\theta_{h^{\sharp}}f^{\sharp}=\theta_{h^{\sharp}}f

for all
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
. This is the case, if and only if
h=h^{\sharp}
. The second statement follows by Proposition
4.13
.
∎

Theorem 4.111
.

(Normal form for reflection positive ROPGs – real version)

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
be a real reflection positive ROPG. Then there exists a — up to isomorphism — unique real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a — up to conjugation with a unitary operator — unique function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\flat}
with
h=h^{\sharp}
such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S,\theta_{h})
is a reflection positive ROPG which is equivalent to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
.

Proof.

By the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
), there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},U,\theta)
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S,\widetilde{\theta})
for some involution
\widetilde{\theta}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
. By Theorem
2.25
, this Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
is unique up to isomorphism. Then, by Proposition
4.13
, the quadruple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),S,\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complex reflection positive ROPG. Therefore, by Proposition
4.17
and Lemma
4.110
, there exists
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\flat}
with
h=h^{\sharp}
such that
\widetilde{\theta}=\theta_{h}
. By Proposition
4.18
, this function is unique up to conjugation with an operator in
\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

In Theorem
4.19
and Proposition
4.17
we have seen that classifying all complex reflection positive ROPGs comes down to classifying all functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}))^{\flat}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. To also classify the real reflection positive ROPGs, we additionally have to determine which of these functions satisfy the condition
h=h^{\sharp}
.

We will not be able to answer this question in full generality. However, we will do so under two more conditions. The first one is that we deal with the multiplicity free case
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{C}}
, so we ask for which functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. In this case, we have

h^{\sharp}(x)=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}}h(-x)=\overline{h(-x)}=h(-x)^{*}=h^{\flat}(x)=h(x),

so the extra condition for real reflection positive ROPGs is automatically fulfilled. The second condition is that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS in the following sense:

Definition 4.112
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
be a RPHS. We call
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)

maximal
if, for every closed subspace
\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
with
\mathcal{E}_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime}
, the triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime},\theta)
is a RPHS, if and only if
\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}_{+}
.

So the rest of this chapter we will dedicate to answering the question for which functions
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS. We will answer this question in Theorem
4.329
. Checking if a RPHS is maximal can often be difficult. The following theorem gives us an equivalent condition that is sometimes easier to check:

Theorem 4.113
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
be a RPHS. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a maximal RPHS.

(b)

There exists a dense subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
such that
V\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
.

(c)

One has
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
be a maximal RPHS and set

V\coloneqq\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\cap\left(\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}\right).

We now want to show that
V
is dense in
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
. So let
v\in V^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
. We want to show that
v=0
. For
\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, we have
\xi=\xi^{\theta}+\xi^{-\theta}
, where

\xi^{\pm\theta}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(\xi\pm\theta\xi)\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm\theta}.

One also has

\xi^{\theta}=\xi-\xi^{-\theta}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta},

so
\xi^{\theta}\in V
and therefore, using that
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\perp\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
and
v\in V^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
, we get

\left\langle\xi\middle|v\right\rangle=\left\langle\xi^{\theta}\middle|v\right\rangle+\left\langle\xi^{-\theta}\middle|v\right\rangle=0+0=0

for every
\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, so
v\in\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}
. On the other hand, for every
\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
and every
t\in{\mathbb{K}}
(assuming that
\mathcal{E}
is a Hilbert space over the field
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
), using that
v\in\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
and that
\left\langle\xi\middle|v\right\rangle=0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle(\xi+tv)\middle|\theta(\xi+tv)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle+\left|t\right|^{2}\left\langle v\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+t\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+\overline{t}\left\langle v\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle+\left|t\right|^{2}\left\langle v\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+t\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta v\right\rangle+\overline{t}\left\langle\theta v\middle|\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle+\left|t\right|^{2}\left\langle v\middle|v\right\rangle+t\left\langle\xi\middle|v\right\rangle+\overline{t}\left\langle v\middle|\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle+\left|t\right|^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}\geq 0.

This shows that the triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v,\theta)
is a RPHS and therefore
\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v=\mathcal{E}_{+}
due to the maximality of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
. This yields
v\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
and therefore
v\in\mathcal{E}_{+}\cap\mathcal{E}_{+}^{\perp}
, so
v=0
. This implies that
V^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\theta}=\{0\}
and therefore
V
is dense in
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
.

Let
V\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
be a dense subspace such that
V\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
. Then, for every
v\in V
, there exist
v_{+}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
v^{-\theta}\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
such that
v=v_{+}+v^{-\theta}
. We now show that this composition is unique, so assume that there is
u_{+}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
u^{-\theta}\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
such that
v=u_{+}+u^{-\theta}
. Then

\mathcal{E}_{+}\ni u_{+}-v_{+}=\left(v-u^{-\theta}\right)-\left(v-v^{-\theta}\right)=v^{-\theta}-u^{-\theta}\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta},

so

u_{+}-v_{+}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}\cap\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}.

Since for every
\xi\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}\setminus\{0\}
one has

\left\langle\xi\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle=\left\langle\xi\middle|-\xi\right\rangle=-\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}<0,

we have
\mathcal{E}_{+}\cap\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}=\{0\}
and therefore
u_{+}-v_{+}=0
, which implies
u_{+}=v_{+}
and therefore also
u^{-\theta}=v^{-\theta}
. So, the composition is unique and therefore defines linear maps

C:V\to\mathcal{E}_{+},\quad v\mapsto v_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad D:V\to\mathcal{E}^{-\theta},\quad v\mapsto v^{-\theta},

of which we now want to show that they are continuous. For
v\in V\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\left(\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}\right)^{\perp}
one has

0\leq\left\langle v_{+}\middle|\theta v_{+}\right\rangle=\left\langle(v-v^{-\theta})\middle|\theta(v-v^{-\theta})\right\rangle=\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert v^{-\theta}\right\rVert^{2},

so

\left\lVert v^{-\theta}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert

and therefore
\left\lVert D\right\rVert\leq 1
. Also, we have

\left\lVert v^{+}\right\rVert=\left\lVert v-v^{-\theta}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert+\left\lVert v^{-\theta}\right\rVert\leq 2\left\lVert v\right\rVert,

so
\left\lVert C\right\rVert\leq 2
. Since
V
is dense in
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
and both
\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
are closed, we can uniquely extend the two maps to continuous maps

\widetilde{C}:\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\to\mathcal{E}_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{D}:\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\to\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}.

Now let
w\in\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
. Then there exists a sequence
(w_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq V
such that
w_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}w
. We get

w=\lim_{n\to\infty}w_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}Cw_{n}+Dw_{n}=\widetilde{C}w+\widetilde{D}w\in\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}.

Since
w\in\mathcal{E}^{\theta}
was chosen arbitrary, we get
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
and let
v\in\mathcal{E}
such that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v,\theta)
is a reflection positive Hilbert space. We want to show that
v\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
. Let
v=v^{\theta}+v^{-\theta}
with
v^{\pm\theta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm\theta}
. Since
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
there are
v_{+}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
u\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}
such that
v^{\theta}=v_{+}+u
. We have

\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v\ni v_{+}-v=\left(v^{\theta}-u\right)-\left(v^{\theta}+v^{-\theta}\right)=-u-v^{-\theta}\in\mathcal{E}^{-\theta},

which yields
v_{+}-v=0
, since

\left(\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v\right)\cap\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}=\{0\}

due to the reflection positivity of the triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+}+{\mathbb{K}}v,\theta)
. Therefore
v=v_{+}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, which shows that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a maximal RPHS.
∎

Corollary 4.114
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
be a real RPHS. Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a maximal RPHS, if and only if
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}},\theta_{\mathbb{C}})
is a maximal RPHS.

Proof.

We have

(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{\mathbb{C}}}=(\mathcal{E}^{\theta})_{\mathbb{C}}\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}+(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{\mathbb{C}}}=(\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta})_{\mathbb{C}}.

This shows that

\mathcal{E}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}+\mathcal{E}^{-\theta}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{\mathbb{C}}}\subseteq(\mathcal{E}_{+})_{\mathbb{C}}+(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{\mathbb{C}}}.

The statement then follows by Theorem
4.113
.
∎

4.2
Involutions with fixed points in the Hardy space

Our first step in classifying all functions
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS is to classify all such functions under the additional assumption that
\theta_{h}
has a fixed point in
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, i.e. that
\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\neq\{0\}
. We will later drop that assumption and consider the general case.

4.2.1
Fixed points in the Hardy space and outer functions

We start by proving the following result that, given a RPHS
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
, one can split off inner functions (cf. Definition
A.31
) from the function
h
:

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
and
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Then the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})h})
is a RPHS, if and only if
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. In this case also the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS.

Proof.

The triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})h})
is a RPHS, if and only if

0\leq\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})h}f\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|\varphi\varphi^{\flat}hRf\right\rangle=\left\langle\varphi^{*}f\middle|hR(\varphi^{*}f)\right\rangle\quad\forall f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

This is equivalent to

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall f\in M_{\varphi^{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
being a RPHS. Since
M_{\varphi}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and therefore

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

this especially implies

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

so
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS.
∎

In this subsection, we want to use this lemma to show that, for a function
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS, the kernel
\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})
just contains outer functions, which are defined as follows:

Definition 4.22
.

(cf. Definition
A.21
)

(a)

For
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
and an almost everywhere defined function
K:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
with

I(K)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left|\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp<\infty

we define the function
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\left(z\right)=C\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p-z}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and call such a function an
outer function
.

(b)

We set
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(1,K)
.

(c)

We write
\mathrm{Out}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
for the set of outer functions on
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and set

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Remark 4.23
.

Since

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\left(z\right)\in{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\exp({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}^{\times},

we have
0\notin\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and therefore also
0\notin\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. This also shows that, for an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, expressions like
\frac{1}{F}
are defined.

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS and

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\emptyset.

Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.

Proof.

We choose

F\in\left(\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\setminus\{0\}.

Then

F=\theta_{h}F=h\cdot RF

and therefore

h=\frac{F}{RF}.

For
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
we get

\theta_{h}(fF)=\frac{F}{RF}R(fF)=\frac{F}{RF}(Rf)(RF)=(Rf)F,

so, for
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm R}
, we have
\theta_{h}(fF)=\pm fF
and therefore

\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm R}F}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm\theta_{h}}.

Further, by Corollary
A.24
, we have

\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{R}F+L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-R}F}=\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})F}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}\oplus L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}},

which yields

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm\theta_{h}}=\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm R}F}.

Also by Corollary
A.24
, we have

\overline{\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\right)F}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

wherefore, for every
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{R}
, there are sequences

(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\quad\text{and}\quad(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})

such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}(f_{n}+g_{n})F=fF.

This also implies

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(R(f_{n}+g_{n}))F

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}(R(f_{n}+g_{n}))\theta_{h}F=\theta_{h}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}(f_{n}+g_{n})F\right)

\displaystyle=\theta_{h}(fF)=(Rf)\theta_{h}F=(Rf)F=fF.

If we then set

s_{n}\coloneqq\frac{f_{n}+Rg_{n}}{2}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

we get

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(s_{n}+Rs_{n}\right)F

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\left(\frac{f_{n}+Rg_{n}}{2}\right)+R\left(\frac{f_{n}+Rg_{n}}{2}\right)\right)F

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(f_{n}+g_{n}\right)F+\lim_{n\to\infty}(R\left(f_{n}+g_{n}\right))F\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(fF+fF\right)=fF.

This shows that the set

V\coloneqq\left\{(f+Rf)F:f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right\}

is dense in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{R}F
and therefore also in
\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{R}F}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}
. For every element
v=(f+Rf)F\in V
, we have

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=(f+Rf)F=2fF-(f-Rf)F

\displaystyle\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})F+L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-R}F\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}},

so

V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}}.

This, by Theorem
4.113
, implies that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.
∎

Corollary 4.25
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. Then

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq\{0\}\cup\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Proof.

Let
f\in\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. If
f=0
then obviously
f\in\{0\}\cup\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. If
f\neq 0
, then, by Theorem
A.32
, there exists an outer function
{F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}
and an inner function
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

f=\varphi\cdot F.

We set

k\coloneqq(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\flat}.

Then

h=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})k

and therefore, by Lemma
4.21
the triples
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
are RPHSs. Since

k=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}h=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}\frac{f}{Rf}=\frac{\varphi^{-1}f}{R(\varphi^{-1}f)}=\frac{F}{RF},

we have

F\in\ker(\theta_{k}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

By Proposition
4.24
this implies that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
is a maximal RPHS. Since we have
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, this implies that

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi^{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

which yields
\varphi^{*}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by Proposition
A.113
and therefore
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
. On the other hand
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, so by Corollary
A.112
we get that
\varphi=C\cdot\textbf{1}
for some
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
. This implies that

f=\varphi\cdot F=C\cdot F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).\qed

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. If

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}

then there exists an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

h=\frac{F}{RF}

and the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.25
we immediately get an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

F=\theta_{h}F=h\cdot RF,

which is equivalent to
h=\frac{F}{RF}
. This, by Proposition
4.24
, implies that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.
∎

4.2.2
Involutions with fixed points and reflection positive functions

In Proposition
4.26
we have seen that the functions
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
, we are looking for, are of the form
h=\frac{F}{RF}
for some outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. To answer the question for which outer functions
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{\frac{F}{RF}})
is a maximal RPHS, we need the definition of a reflection positive function:

Definition 4.27
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Def. 4.1.3]
)

(a)

Let
X
be a non-empty set. A function

K:X\times X\to{\mathbb{R}}

is called a
positive definite kernel
if, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X
, the matrix

(K(x_{k},x_{j}))_{k,j=1,\dots,n}

is a positive semidefinite matrix.

(b)

A function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called
reflection positive
, if both kernels

{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\ni(s,t)\mapsto\varphi\left(s-t\right)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ni(s,t)\mapsto\varphi\left(s+t\right)

are positive definite.

In the following, we will use the Fourier transform

??:L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})

with

(\mathcal{F}_{1}f)(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itx}f(x)dx.

Since this function is injective, we can define the map

\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}:\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))\to L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),\quad\mathcal{F}_{1}f\mapsto f.

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
and let

F\in\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS.

(b)

\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS.

(c)

The function
{\mathbb{R}}\ni t\mapsto\varphi(t)\coloneqq\left\langle S_{t}F\middle|F\right\rangle
is reflection positive.

(d)

|F|^{2}=\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi
for some reflection positive function
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
.

(e)

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(C,\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)
for some
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
and some reflection positive function
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
with
I\left(\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)<\infty
.

Proof.

(a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b):

This follows immediately by Proposition
4.24
.

(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c):

The kernel

{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\ni(s,t)\mapsto\varphi(s-t)

is always positive definite, since, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\varphi\left(t_{k}-t_{j}\right)v_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}-t_{j}}F\middle|F\right\rangle v_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}}F\middle|S_{t_{j}}F\right\rangle v_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}S_{t_{k}}F\middle|\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j}S_{t_{j}}F\right\rangle\geq 0.

Therefore it remains to show that
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS, if and only if the function

K:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad(s,t)\mapsto\varphi(s+t)

is a positive definite kernel. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\varphi(t_{k}+t_{j})v_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}+t_{j}}F\middle|F\right\rangle v_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}}F\middle|S_{-t_{j}}F\right\rangle v_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}}F\middle|S_{-t_{j}}\theta_{h}F\right\rangle v_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{v_{k}}\left\langle S_{t_{k}}F\middle|\theta_{h}S_{t_{j}}F\right\rangle v_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}S_{t_{k}}F\right)\middle|\theta_{h}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j}S_{t_{j}}F\right)\right\rangle.

This shows that
K
is a positive definite kernel, if and only if

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall f\in\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F.

Since, by Theorem
A.23
, we have

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\overline{\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F},

this is equivalent to

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\forall f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore to the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
being a RPHS.

This follows immediately by

\varphi(t)=\left\langle S_{t}F\middle|F\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-itx}\left|F\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\,dx=(\mathcal{F}_{1}|F|^{2})(t),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We have
|F|^{2}=\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi
for some reflection positive function
\varphi
, if and only if
|F|=\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}
, which, by Theorem
A.22
, is equivalent to

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(C,\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)

for some
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
.
∎

This proposition shows that any fixed point
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
of an involution
\theta_{h}
, such that
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS, is of the form

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(C,\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)

for some reflection positive function
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
with

I\left(\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)<\infty.

The question we now have to answer is for which reflection positive function
\varphi
these two conditions are fulfilled. For this, we have to better understand reflection positive functions, and for this, we make the following definitions:

Definition 4.29
.

(a)

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. We denote the set of Borel measures on
X
by
??
and the subset of finite Borel measures on
X
by
??
.

(b)

For a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\right)
we set

??\left(t\right)\coloneqq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}e^{-\lambda\left|t\right|}d\mu\left(\lambda\right),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(c)

For a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
we set

??\left(p\right)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right),\quad p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}.

The following results will motivate this definition. Here we identify
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
with the set

\{\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\right):\mu(\{0\})=0\}.

Theorem 4.210
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Thm. 4.1.1, Ex. 4.1.4]
)

A function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is reflection positive, if and only if there exists a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\right)
such that
\varphi=\varphi_{\mu}
.

Proposition 4.211
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Then
\psi_{\mu}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with
\left\lVert\psi_{\mu}\right\rVert_{1}=\mu({\mathbb{R}}_{+})
and

\mathcal{F}_{1}\psi_{\mu}=\varphi_{\mu}.

Proof.

Since, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,dp=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+\left(\frac{p}{\lambda}\right)^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\,dp=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\left[\arctan(x)\right]_{-\infty}^{\infty}=\frac{\pi}{2}-\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\pi,

using the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem, we get

\displaystyle\mu({\mathbb{R}}_{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\pi\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,dp\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)\,dp=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\mu}(p)\,dp=\left\lVert\psi_{\mu}\right\rVert_{1}

and therefore
\psi_{\mu}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

To calculate the Fourier transform, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we define the function
f_{\lambda}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
by

f_{\lambda}(t)=e^{-\lambda|t|}.

Then, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we get

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{itx}f_{\lambda}(t)\,dt=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{itx}e^{-\lambda t}\,dt=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{t(-\lambda+ix)}\,dt=\left[\frac{1}{-\lambda+ix}e^{t(-\lambda+ix)}\right]_{0}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{\lambda-ix}

and therefore, due to the symmetry of
f_{\lambda}
, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{-}}e^{itx}f_{\lambda}(t)\,dt=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-itx}f_{\lambda}(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{\lambda+ix},

so

\sqrt{2\pi}\cdot(\mathcal{F}^{-1}f_{\lambda})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{itx}f_{\lambda}(t)\,dt=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda-ix}+\frac{1}{\lambda+ix}\right]=\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+x^{2}}\eqqcolon g_{\lambda}(x).

This implies

\mathcal{F}_{1}g_{\lambda}=\sqrt{2\pi}\cdot\mathcal{F}g_{\lambda}=2\pi\cdot f_{\lambda}

and therefore

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itp}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,dp=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot(\mathcal{F}_{1}g_{\lambda})(t)=f_{\lambda}(t)=e^{-\lambda|t|},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Therefore, using the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{1}\psi)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itp}\psi(p)\,dp=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itp}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)\,dp

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itp}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,dp\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-\lambda|t|}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\varphi_{\mu}(t).\qed

Corollary 4.212
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\right)
. Then
\varphi_{\mu}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
, if and only if
\mu(\{0\})=0
.

Proof.

We write

\mu=\mu(\{0\})\cdot\delta_{0}+\widetilde{\mu}

with
\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Then

\varphi_{\mu}=\mu(\{0\})\cdot\varphi_{\delta_{0}}+\varphi_{\widetilde{\mu}}=\mu(\{0\})\cdot\textbf{1}+\varphi_{\widetilde{\mu}}.

Since, by Proposition
4.211
, we have
\varphi_{\widetilde{\mu}}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
, this shows that
\psi_{\mu}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
, if and only if
\mu(\{0\})\cdot\textbf{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
. The latter is equivalent to
\mu(\{0\})=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.213
.

Let
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a reflection positive function. Then
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
, if and only if
\varphi=\varphi_{\mu}
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
.

Proof.

This follows immediately by Theorem
4.210
and Corollary
4.212
.
∎

We now want to see, for which measures
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
one has

I\left(\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi_{\mu}}\right)<\infty.

For this, we make some definitions:

Definition 4.214
.

(a)

We write
\left[0,\infty\right]
for the one-point compactification of
\left[0,\infty\right)
.

(b)

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right)
. For
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
we consider the bounded and continuous function
{\lambda\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}}
with
\infty\mapsto\frac{1+\infty^{2}}{p^{2}+\infty^{2}}\eqqcolon 1
and define

??(p)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\,d\nu\left(\lambda\right).

(c)

We define the map

??:\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\to\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right),\quad d(W(\mu))(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda),

Remark 4.215
.

That for
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
one actually has
W(\mu)\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
follows from the fact that, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, one has

0\leq(\lambda-1)^{2}=1+\lambda^{2}-2\lambda

and therefore
\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, so

(W(\mu))([0,\infty])=\int_{[0,\infty]}d(W(\mu))(\lambda)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mu({\mathbb{R}}_{+})<\infty.

Lemma 4.216
.

For
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
one has

\Psi_{W(\mu)}=\psi_{\mu}.

Proof.

For
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
one has

\displaystyle\Psi_{W(\mu)}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\,d(W\mu)\left(\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\psi_{\mu}(p).\qed

We now want to see that, for the functions
\Psi_{\nu}
with
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
one has
I(\Psi_{\nu})<\infty
. For this, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 4.217
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
. Then, one has the estimate

\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\vartheta(p)\leq\Psi_{\nu}(p)\leq\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\eta(p),\quad p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},

with the functions

\vartheta:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad p\mapsto\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\,\left|p\right|<1\\
\frac{1}{p^{2}}&\text{if}\,\left|p\right|\geq 1\end{cases}

and

\eta:{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad p\mapsto\begin{cases}\frac{1}{p^{2}}&\text{if }\left|p\right|<1\\
1&\text{if }\left|p\right|\geq 1.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\infty]
. Then, for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
with
\left|p\right|<1
, we have

1=\frac{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+p^{2}\lambda^{2}}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}

and for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
with
\left|p\right|\geq 1
, we get

\frac{1}{p^{2}}=\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+p^{2}\lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}=1,

so, for every
\lambda\in[0,\infty]
, we get

\vartheta(p)\leq\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\leq\eta(p).

The statement then follows by integrating over
\lambda\in[0,\infty]
with respect to the measure
\nu
.
∎

Lemma 4.218
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then

I(\Psi_{\nu})<\infty.

Proof.

Since
\nu\neq 0
, we have
\nu([0,\infty])>0
. Then, by Lemma
A.25
, we have
I(\Psi_{\nu})<\infty
, if and only if
I\left(\frac{1}{\nu([0,\infty])}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\right)<\infty
. By Lemma
4.217
, for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
with
|p|<1
, we have

0=\log(1)\leq\log\left(\frac{1}{\nu([0,\infty])}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\right)\leq\log\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)=-2\log(p)=2|\log(p)|,

and for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
with
|p|\geq 1
, we have

-2|\log(p)|=-2\log(p)=\log\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\leq\log\left(\frac{1}{\nu([0,\infty])}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\right)\leq\log(1)=0.

This shows that, for any
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, we have

\left|\log\left(\frac{1}{\nu([0,\infty])}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\right)\right|\leq 2|\log(p)|

and therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left|\log\left(\frac{1}{\nu([0,\infty])}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2|\log p|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp=4\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{|\log p|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|x|}{1+e^{2x}}\cdot e^{x}\,dx=4\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|x|}{e^{-x}+e^{x}}\,dx<\infty.\qed

Using this lemma, we can make the following definition:

Definition 4.219
.

For
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
we define

??\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)\in\mathrm{Out}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and

??\coloneqq\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}.

Remark 4.220
.

(a)

That
F_{\nu}
is defined follows by Lemma
4.218
, since we have

I\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}I\left(\Psi_{\nu}\right)<\infty.

(b)

To see that
h_{\nu}
actually defines an element in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
we first notice that, by Theorem
A.22
, we have
|F_{\nu}|=\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, using that

\Psi_{\nu}(-p)=\Psi_{\nu}(p),\quad p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},

we get

|h_{\nu}(x)|=\frac{F_{\nu}(x)}{|F_{\nu}(-x)|}=\frac{\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}(x)}}{\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}(-x)}}=1,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

and therefore
h_{\nu}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})
. This then implies that

h_{\nu}^{\flat}(x)=\overline{h_{\nu}(-x)}=\frac{1}{h_{\nu}(-x)}=\frac{RF_{\nu}(-x)}{F_{\nu}(-x)}=\frac{F_{\nu}(x)}{RF_{\nu}(x)}=h_{\nu}(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

and therefore
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
.

With this we get our theorem classifying involutions
\theta_{h}
with fixed points in the Hardy space such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a (maximal) RPHS:

Theorem 4.221
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS with
\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}
.

(b)

\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS with
\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}
.

(c)

h=h_{W(\mu)}
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

(a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b):

This follows immediately by Proposition
4.26
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c):

If
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS with

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\},

then, by Proposition
4.26
, there exists an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

h=\frac{F}{RF}.

For this function, we have

F\in\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore, by Proposition
4.28
, we have

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(C,\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)

for some
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
and some reflection positive function
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
with

I\left(\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)<\infty.

By Corollary
4.213
we then have
\varphi=\varphi_{\mu}
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. This, by Proposition
4.211
and Lemma
4.216
, yields

\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi=\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi_{\mu}=\psi_{\mu}=\Psi_{W(\mu)}

and therefore

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(C,\sqrt{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi}\right)=C\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{W(\mu)}}\right)=C\cdot F_{W(\mu)}.

This implies
