h=\frac{F}{RF}=\frac{C\cdot F_{W(\mu)}}{C\cdot RF_{W(\mu)}}=\frac{F_{W(\mu)}}{RF_{W(\mu)}}=h_{W(\mu)}.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(b):

If
h=h_{W(\mu)}
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
, we have

\theta_{h}F_{W(\mu)}=\frac{F_{W(\mu)}}{RF_{W(\mu)}}\cdot RF_{W(\mu)}=F_{W(\mu)}

and therefore

F_{W(\mu)}\in\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

so especially

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}.

Further, by Lemma
4.216
and Proposition
4.211
, we have

|F_{W(\mu)}|^{2}=\Psi_{W(\mu)}=\psi_{\mu}=\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\varphi_{\mu}

and therefore
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS by Proposition
4.28
. ∎

4.3
General involutions

In the last section, we have given a full characterization of the functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS under the extra condition that

\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}.

We have seen that these are precisely the functions of the form

h=h_{W(\mu)}

for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. In this section, we want to get rid of the extra assumption that
\theta_{h}
has a non-zero fixed point in the Hardy space.

4.3.1
Fixed points outside the Hardy space and outer functions

First we notice that the functions
F_{\nu}
and
h_{\nu}
are defined for general measures
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and not just for measures of the form
W(\mu)
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Although, by Theorem
4.221
, these involution
\theta_{h}
have no non-zero fixed point in the Hardy space, they still formally satisfy the equation

\theta_{h}F_{\nu}=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}RF_{\nu}=F_{\nu},

which we will interpret as them having a fixed point in a space slightly bigger than the Hardy space. We will now define this space:

Definition 4.31
.

(a)

We define the function
??\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by

\Lambda(z)\coloneqq\frac{iz}{(z+i)^{2}}.

(b)

We set

??\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}):\Lambda\cdot f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\}.

(c)

We define

??\coloneqq H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Remark 4.32
.

(a)

We have

\left|\frac{ix}{(x+i)^{2}}\right|=\frac{|x|}{1+x^{2}}\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}},

so by Lemma
A.27
we have

\Lambda=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(|\Lambda|)\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Since
|\Lambda|\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, by Theorem
A.22
, we get

\Lambda\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

(b)

Since
\Lambda\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we get

M_{\Lambda}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

(c)

Since
0\notin\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we also have
0\notin\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

The following lemma about the function
\Lambda
will be useful later:

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\varphi_{i}\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
be the inner function with

\varphi_{i}(z)=\frac{z-i}{z+i},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Then

\frac{\Lambda(x)}{(R\Lambda)(x)}=-\frac{\varphi_{i}(x)}{(R\varphi_{i})(x)}\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\frac{\Lambda(x)}{(R\Lambda)(x)}=\frac{\frac{ix}{(x+i)^{2}}}{\frac{-ix}{(-x+i)^{2}}}=-\frac{(-x+i)^{2}}{(x+i)^{2}}=-\frac{\frac{x-i}{x+i}}{\frac{-x-i}{-x+i}}=-\frac{\varphi_{i}(x)}{(R\varphi_{i})(x)}.\qed

Every function
f\in H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
can be written as
f=\frac{g}{\Lambda}
for some function
g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. This, in particular, implies that the limit

f(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}f(x+i\varepsilon)=\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}g(x+i\varepsilon)\right)\cdot\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\Lambda(x+i\varepsilon)\right)^{-1}

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
since the corresponding limits exist for outer functions and Hardy space functions. This, in particular, allows us to interpret functions
f\in H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
as almost everywhere defined functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
, similarly to outer functions and Hardy space functions. Further, we have

\infty>\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}\cdot|\Lambda(x)|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}\frac{x^{2}}{(1+x^{2})^{2}}dx,

so

f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},\frac{x^{2}}{(1+x^{2})^{2}}dx\right).

This motivates the following definition:

Definition 4.34
.

(a)

We define a measure
??
on
{\mathbb{R}}
by

d\rho(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{(1+x^{2})^{2}}dx.

(b)

For a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\flat}
we define an involution
??\in B(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},\rho\right))
by

\Theta_{h}f\coloneqq h\cdot Rf.

Remark 4.35
.

(a)

The boundedness of the function
x\mapsto\frac{x^{2}}{(1+x^{2})^{2}}
implies that

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},\rho).

(b)

By definition, we have

\Theta_{h}\big|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}^{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}=\theta_{h}.

(c)

As seen before, we can identify
H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
with a subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},\rho)
by taking boundary values.

The following proposition shows how useful this extension is since it shows that, although not every involution
\theta_{h}
has a non-zero fixed point in the Hardy space, the slightly extended function
\Theta_{h}
always has such a fixed point in the extended Hardy space:

Proposition 4.36
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\flat}
such that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS. Then

\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\{0\}.

Proof.

Since
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS, we know that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is not a RPHS and therefore there exists a function
f\in M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle<0.

Now, let

f=f_{+}+f_{-}\quad\text{with}\quad f_{\pm}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\pm\theta_{h}}.

Since

(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS, by Theorem
4.113
, there exists a function
{\widetilde{f}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}
and a function
\widetilde{f}_{-}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}}
such that

f_{+}=\widetilde{f}+\widetilde{f}_{-}.

We now set

g\coloneqq f-\widetilde{f}\in M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Then
\widetilde{g}\coloneqq M_{\varphi_{i}}g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Since

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle<0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle\widetilde{f}\middle|\theta_{h}\widetilde{f}\right\rangle\geq 0,

we have
f\neq\widetilde{f}
and therefore
g\neq 0
, so
\widetilde{g}\neq 0
. Further

g=f-\widetilde{f}=(f_{+}+f_{-})-(f_{+}-\widetilde{f}_{-})=f_{-}+\widetilde{f}_{-}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}},

so
\theta_{h}g=-g
. This yields

\theta_{h}\widetilde{g}=\theta_{h}M_{\varphi_{i}}g=M_{R\varphi_{i}}\theta_{h}g=-M_{R\varphi_{i}}g=-M_{\frac{R\varphi_{i}}{\varphi_{i}}}M_{\varphi_{i}}g=-M_{\frac{R\varphi_{i}}{\varphi_{i}}}\widetilde{g}=M_{\frac{R\Lambda}{\Lambda}}\widetilde{g},

using Lemma
4.33
in the last step. We now define

F\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{g}}{\Lambda}\in H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\setminus\{0\}.

Then

\Theta_{h}F=\Theta_{h}\frac{\widetilde{g}}{\Lambda}=\frac{1}{R\Lambda}\Theta_{h}\widetilde{g}=\frac{1}{R\Lambda}\theta_{h}\widetilde{g}\overset{(\bigstar)}{=}\frac{1}{R\Lambda}M_{\frac{R\Lambda}{\Lambda}}\widetilde{g}=\frac{\widetilde{g}}{\Lambda}=F,

so we have

F\in\left(\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\setminus\{0\}.\qed

In Corollary
4.25
we have seen that, for any
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
for which
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS, the kernel
\ker(\theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
just consists of outer functions. We now want to see that the same is true for the extended involution
\Theta_{h}
. For this we need the following analogue of Proposition
4.24
for the involution
\Theta_{h}
:

Proposition 4.37
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS and

\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\emptyset.

Then
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.

Proof.

By assumption, we can choose a function

F\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

We set

G\coloneqq\Lambda\cdot F\in\mathrm{Out}^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

We now set

V\coloneqq M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{1}\oplus M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\cdot G\subseteq L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\cdot G\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Our first goal is to prove that
V
is dense in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Since, by Proposition
A.35
, we have

M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\{f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}):f(i)=0\}

and
G(i)\neq 0
, we have

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}\overline{H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G},

using Theorem
A.23
for the last equality. This yields

\overline{{\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}\supseteq{\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}\overline{H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\supseteq H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G

and therefore, using that

(R\varphi_{i})(x)=\frac{-x-i}{-x+i}=\frac{x+i}{x-i}=\left(\varphi_{i}(x)\right)^{-1},

we have

\displaystyle\overline{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})G+{\mathbb{C}}G}

\displaystyle=\frac{G}{RG}\cdot\overline{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})RG+{\mathbb{C}}RG}=\frac{G}{RG}\cdot R\left(\overline{{\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}\right)

\displaystyle\supseteq\frac{G}{RG}\cdot R\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G\right)=\frac{G}{RG}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})RG=H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})G.

Using these two inclusions, we get

\displaystyle M_{\varphi_{i}}\overline{V}

\displaystyle=\overline{\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})+{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)G}

\displaystyle=\overline{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})G+{\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})G+{\mathbb{C}}G}+\overline{{\mathbb{C}}G+M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\overline{H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})G+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G}

\displaystyle=\overline{\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)G}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

using Corollary
A.24
in the last step. This yields

\overline{V}=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Since
\Theta_{h}F=F
, we have

h=\frac{F}{RF}=\frac{R\Lambda}{\Lambda}\cdot\frac{G}{RG}=-\frac{R\varphi_{i}}{\varphi_{i}}\cdot\frac{G}{RG},

using Lemma
4.33
in the last step. For
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
this yields

\displaystyle\theta_{h}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(f\cdot G)

\displaystyle=-\frac{R\varphi_{i}}{\varphi_{i}}\cdot\frac{G}{RG}\cdot R\left(\frac{f}{\varphi_{i}}\cdot G\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{R\varphi_{i}}{\varphi_{i}}\cdot\frac{G}{RG}\cdot\frac{Rf}{R\varphi_{i}}\cdot RG=-\frac{Rf}{\varphi_{i}}\cdot G=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}((-Rf)\cdot G).

This implies that
V
is
\theta_{h}
-invariant, since, for
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
,
g\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta_{h}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}f+z\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right)

\displaystyle=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(-R\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}f+z\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)\right)G\right)

\displaystyle=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(-Rg)-z\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}(-Rf)\right)G\right)\in V.

This also shows that an element

M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}f+z\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right)\in V

is
\theta_{h}
-invariant, if and only if
f=-Rg
and
z=0
, so

V^{\theta_{h}}=\left\{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(-Rg)+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right):g\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right\}.

Analogously we get

V^{-\theta_{h}}=\left\{M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(Rg)+z\textbf{1}+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right):g\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),z\in{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.

Then, for every
v=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(-Rg)+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right)\in V^{\theta_{h}}
, one has

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(-Rg)+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right)=gG+M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(-Rg)G)

\displaystyle=2gG-M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(M_{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}(Rg)+M_{\varphi_{i}}g\right)G\right)

\displaystyle\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})G+V^{-\theta_{h}}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}},

so

V^{\theta_{h}}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}}.

Writing
P\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(1+\theta_{h})
, we have

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}=\overline{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}}\supseteq\overline{V^{\theta_{h}}}=\overline{PV}\supseteq P\overline{V}\overset{(\bigstar)}{=}PL^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}},

so
\overline{V^{\theta_{h}}}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}
and therefore
V^{\theta_{h}}
is a dense subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\theta_{h}}
with

V^{\theta_{h}}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{-\theta_{h}}.

This, by Theorem
4.113
, implies that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.
∎

We can now prove analogues of Corollary
4.25
and Proposition
4.26
for the involution
\Theta_{h}
:

Corollary 4.38
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS. Then

\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq\{0\}\cup\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Proof.

Let
f\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. If
f=0
then obviously
f\in\{0\}\cup\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. If
f\neq 0
, then

f=\frac{g}{\Lambda}

for some function
g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Then, by Theorem
A.32
, there exist an inner function
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and an outer function
{G\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}
such that

g=\varphi\cdot G.

Setting

F\coloneqq\frac{G}{\Lambda}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

this yields

f=\frac{g}{\Lambda}=\frac{\varphi\cdot G}{\Lambda}=\varphi\cdot F.

We now set

k\coloneqq(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\flat}.

Then

h=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})k

and therefore, by Lemma
4.21
the triples
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
are RPHSs. Since

k=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}h=(\varphi\varphi^{\flat})^{-1}\frac{f}{Rf}=\frac{\varphi^{-1}f}{R(\varphi^{-1}f)}=\frac{F}{RF},

we have

F\in\ker(\Theta_{k}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

By Proposition
4.37
this implies that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
is a maximal RPHS. Since we have
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{k})
is a RPHS, this implies that

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi}^{-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=M_{\varphi^{*}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

which yields
\varphi^{*}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by Proposition
A.113
and therefore
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
. On the other hand
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, so by Corollary
A.112
we get that
\varphi=C\cdot\textbf{1}
for some
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
. This implies that

f=\varphi\cdot F=C\cdot F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).\qed

Corollary 4.39
.

Let
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}}
such that the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS. Then there exists an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

h=\frac{F}{RF}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.36
and Corollary
4.38
we have

\{0\}\neq\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq\{0\}\cup\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

so there exists an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that
F\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})
, so

F=\Theta_{h}F=h\cdot RF,

which is equivalent to
h=\frac{F}{RF}
.
∎

4.3.2
General involutions and measures

Now the question left to answer is for which functions
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, defining
h=\frac{F}{RF}
, the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS. In Theorem
4.221
we have seen that examples of such functions are given by the functions
F_{W(\mu)}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
for measures
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. In this subsection, we will first see that moreso, for every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, considering the functions
F_{\nu}
and
h_{\nu}=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}
, one has
F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and the triple
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h_{\nu}})
is a maximal RPHS. In the end it turns out that these are the only functions with this property. We start by showing that in fact
F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
for
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
:

Lemma 4.310
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then

|\Lambda(x)|^{2}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}(x)\leq\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

and
F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.25
and Remark
4.32
we have
\Lambda\cdot F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Further, by Lemma
A.25
and Lemma
4.217
we have

|F_{\nu}(x)|^{2}=|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(\Psi_{\nu})(x)|=\Psi_{\nu}(x)\leq\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}+1\right)=\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\frac{1+x^{2}}{x^{2}},\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}.

Since

|\Lambda(x)|=\frac{|x|}{1+x^{2}}

(cf. Remark
4.32
), we get

|\Lambda(x)\cdot F_{\nu}(x)|^{2}=|\Lambda(x)|^{2}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}(x)\leq\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\frac{1+x^{2}}{x^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{|x|}{1+x^{2}}\right)^{2}=\nu([0,\infty])\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}.

Since

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\pi<\infty,

this shows that
\Lambda\cdot F_{\nu}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Then, by Theorem
A.22
, we get
\Lambda\cdot F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and therefore

F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.
∎

Now, given a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, we want to show that
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h_{\nu}})
is a RPHS, i.e. that for every
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}f\right\rangle\geq 0.

For this, we make the following definition:

Definition 4.311
.

We define the map

??:\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right)\setminus\{0\}\to\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)

by

d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)\coloneqq\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Remark 4.312
.

For every
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, by Lemma
A.25
, we have

\left|F_{\left(c\cdot\nu\right)}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{2}=\left|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\Psi_{\left(c\cdot\nu\right)}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)\right|=\left|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(c\cdot\Psi_{\nu}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)\right|=c\cdot\left|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\Psi_{\nu}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)\right|=c\cdot\left|F_{\nu}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{2}

so

\displaystyle d(\mathcal{T}\left(c\cdot\nu\right))\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\left|F_{\left(c\cdot\nu\right)}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\left(c\cdot\nu\right)\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\left|F_{\nu}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\cdot c\,d\nu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\left|F_{\nu}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu\left(\lambda\right)=d(\mathcal{T}\nu)\left(\lambda\right),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

and therefore

\mathcal{T}\left(c\cdot\nu\right)=\mathcal{T}\nu.

We now want to show that, for every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and every
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,g(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda).

For this, we need the following proposition:

Proposition 4.313
.

Let
A\in B(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
and
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Further, for a function
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
we consider the function

f^{\tt V}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad\lambda\mapsto f(i\lambda).

If there exists a dense subspace
V\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

\left\langle f\middle|Ag\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f^{\tt V}(\lambda)}\,g^{\tt V}(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f,g\in V,

then
f^{\tt V}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)
for every
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and

\left\langle f\middle|Ag\right\rangle=\left\langle f^{\tt V}\middle|g^{\tt V}\right\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)}\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Proof.

For every
f\in V
we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}|f^{\tt V}(\lambda)|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)=\left\langle f\middle|Af\right\rangle<\infty,

so
f^{\tt V}\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
. Now let
f\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
. Since
V
is dense in
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, there exists a sequence
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
V
such that
{\left\lVert f_{n}-f\right\rVert\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0}
. Then

\left\lVert f_{n}^{\tt V}\right\rVert_{L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)}^{2}=\left\langle f_{n}^{\tt V}\middle|f_{n}^{\tt V}\right\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)}=\left\langle f_{n}\middle|Af_{n}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle f\middle|Af\right\rangle,

so the sequence
(f_{n}^{\tt V})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded in
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
, and therefore, by the Banach-Alaoglu Theorem, has a weakly-convergent subsequence. By replacing
(f_{n}^{\tt V})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with that subsequence, we can assume that there exists
F\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
such that
f_{n}^{\tt V}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}F
weakly. We now want to see that
F=f^{\tt V}
. For
0<a<b<\infty
and
A\subseteq\left[a,b\right]
, we have

\chi_{A}f_{n}^{\tt V}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\chi_{A}f^{\tt V}

uniformly by Proposition
A.18
. Then, using that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A}^{2}\,d\mu=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A}\,d\mu=\mu(A)\leq\mu([a,b])<\infty

and therefore
\chi_{A}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)\cap L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)
, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A}F\,d\mu=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A}f_{n}^{\tt V}\,d\mu=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A}f^{\tt V}\,d\mu.

Choosing

A_{\pm}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\left[a,b\right]:\pm\left(F-f^{\tt V}\right)\left(x\right)\geq 0\right\},

we get

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A_{\pm}}\left(F-f^{\tt V}\right)\,d\mu

and therefore

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi_{A_{+}}\left(F-f^{\tt V}\right)-\chi_{A_{-}}\left(F-f^{\tt V}\right)\,d\mu=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\left|F-f^{\tt V}\right|\,d\mu,

so
\mu
-almost everywhere we have

F\big|_{\left[a,b\right]}=f^{\tt V}\big|_{\left[a,b\right]}.

Since this is true for all
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
{0<a<b<\infty}
, we
\mu
-almost everywhere have
F=f^{\tt V}
, which shows that
f^{\tt V}\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
. On the other hand, this argument shows that the graph of the map

(\cdot)^{\tt V}:H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)

is closed, and therefore, by the Closed Graph Theorem, this map is continuous. Using this continuity, for two functions
f,g\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
, we can select sequences
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
V
such that
f_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}f
and
g_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}g
and get

\displaystyle\left\langle f\middle|Ag\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{m\to\infty}\left\langle f_{n}\middle|Ag_{m}\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f_{n}^{\tt V}(\lambda)}\,g_{m}^{\tt V}(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f^{\tt V}(\lambda)}\,g^{\tt V}(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)=\left\langle f^{\tt V}\middle|g^{\tt V}\right\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)}.\qed

Corollary 4.314
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then, for every
f,g\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,g(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}W(\mu))(\lambda).

Proof.

For every
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}S_{t}F_{W(\mu)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\middle|S_{-t}\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}F_{W(\mu)}\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\middle|S_{-t}F_{W(\mu)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{s+t}F_{W(\mu)}\middle|F_{W(\mu)}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(s+t)x}|F_{W(\mu)}(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{F}_{1}|F_{W(\mu)}|^{2}\right)(s+t)=\left(\mathcal{F}_{1}\Psi_{W(\mu)}\right)(s+t)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{F}_{1}\psi_{\mu}\right)(s+t)=\varphi_{\mu}\left(s+t\right),

using Lemma
4.216
and Proposition
4.211
for the last two steps. This yields

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}S_{t}F_{W(\mu)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\mu}\left(s+t\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-\lambda\left(s+t\right)}d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{e^{-\lambda s}F_{W(\mu)}\left(i\lambda\right)}\,e^{-\lambda t}F_{W(\mu)}\left(i\lambda\right)\left|F_{W(\mu)}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{-2}d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{\left(S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)}\,\left(S_{t}F_{W(\mu)}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)\left|F_{W(\mu)}\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d(W(\mu))\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{\left(S_{s}F_{W(\mu)}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)}\,\left(S_{t}F_{W(\mu)}\right)\left(i\lambda\right)\,d(\mathcal{T}W(\mu))\left(\lambda\right).

Then, by the sesquilinearity of the scalar product, we get

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}\,g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d(\mathcal{T}W(\mu))\left(\lambda\right)

for every
f,g\in\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F_{W(\mu)}
. Since
F_{W(\mu)}
is an outer function, by Theorem
A.23
, the subspace
\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F_{W(\mu)}
is dense in
H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
, so the statement follows by Proposition
4.313
.
∎

We now want to show that this corollary is not only true for measures of the form
W(\mu)
with
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
, but also for general measures
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. For this we will make use of the fact that, in a certain sense, we can approximate measures
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
by measures of the form
W(\mu)
with
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. The topology we will use for this is the weak-
*
-topology on the Banach space of complex finite Borel measures:

Definition 4.315
.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. We denote the set of complex Borel
measures on
X
by

??.

Further, we set

\mathcal{BM}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(X\right)\coloneqq\{\mu\in\mathcal{BM}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(X\right):|\mu|(X)<\infty\}.

Definition 4.316
.

Let
V
be a Banach space over a field
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
and
V^{\prime}
be its dual space. For
v\in V^{\prime}
and a sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
V^{\prime}
we write

v_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}v,

if
(v_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
v
with respect to the weak-
*
-topology, i.e. the topology induced by the linear functionals

\mathop{{\rm ev}}\nolimits_{x}:V^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{K}},\quad w\mapsto w(x),\qquad x\in V.

Remark 4.317
.

For an interval
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, the space
(C_{0}(I),\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty})
of complex-valued functions on
I
that vanish at infinity with the supremum norm is a Banach space. By the Riesz Representation Theorem (cf.
[
Ru86
, Thm. 6.19]
) its dual space is given by
\mathcal{BM}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(I\right)
. In particular
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathbb{C}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
is the dual space of the separable Banach space
C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})
and therefore carries a weak-
*
-topology given by the linear functionals

I_{f}:\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathbb{C}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad\nu\mapsto\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu

with
f\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
is closed in this topology.

The following lemmata will be useful later:

Lemma 4.318
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
and let
\left(\nu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
with
\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu
. Then

\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(p)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\Psi_{\nu}(p)

for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately by the fact that, for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, the function

f_{p}:[0,\infty]\ni\lambda\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}

is continuous, and therefore, we have

\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(p)=\int_{[0,\infty]}f_{p}\,d\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty]}f_{p}\,d\nu=\Psi_{\nu}(p).\qed

Lemma 4.319
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and let
\left(\nu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
with
\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu
.
Then, for every
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, one has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu_{n}\left(\lambda\right)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu\left(\lambda\right).

Proof.

We have

|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}=\left|\frac{i(i\lambda)}{(i\lambda+i)^{2}}\right|^{2}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1+\lambda)^{4}}\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Therefore, the function

f:[0,\infty]\ni\lambda\mapsto e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}=e^{-(s+t)\lambda}\frac{\lambda(1+\lambda^{2})}{(1+\lambda)^{4}}

is continuous with

f(0)=0=f(\infty).

This yields

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d\nu_{n}=\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d\nu.\qed

Lemma 4.320
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and let
\left(\nu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
with

\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\nu_{n}([0,\infty])<\infty.

Then, for every
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu_{n}}\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu_{n}}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu_{n}}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu}\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu}\right\rangle.

Proof.

For
\rho\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, by Lemma
4.310
, we have
F_{\rho}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and therefore
\Lambda F_{\rho}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Therefore, for
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we can define

\Phi_{\rho}(s,t)\coloneqq\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\rho}\middle|\theta_{h_{\rho}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\rho}\right\rangle.

Then, using that

(R\Lambda)(x)=\frac{-ix}{(-x+i)^{2}}=\frac{-ix}{(x-i)^{2}}=\overline{\Lambda(x)},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},

we get

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rho}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\rho}\middle|\theta_{h_{\rho}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\rho}\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\rho}\middle|S_{-t}(R\Lambda)\theta_{h_{\rho}}F_{\rho}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{(s+t)}\Lambda F_{\rho}\middle|\overline{\Lambda}F_{\rho}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(s+t)x}\overline{\Lambda(x)}^{2}|F_{\rho}(x)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(s+t)x}\overline{\Lambda(x)}^{2}\Psi_{\rho}(x)\,dx.

Now, setting

M\coloneqq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\nu_{n}([0,\infty])<\infty,

by Lemma
4.310
, we have

\left|e^{-i(s+t)x}\overline{\Lambda(x)}^{2}\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(x)\right|=|\Lambda(x)|^{2}\cdot\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(x)\leq\nu_{n}([0,\infty])\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\leq M\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}

for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

and by Lemma
4.318
we have

\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(x)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\Psi_{\nu}(x)

for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
. This, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, yields

\Phi_{\nu_{n}}(s,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(s+t)x}\overline{\Lambda(x)}^{2}\Psi_{\nu_{n}}(x)\,dx\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(s+t)x}\overline{\Lambda(x)}^{2}\Psi_{\nu}(x)\,dx=\Phi_{\nu}(s,t).\qed

We now prove that we can approximate measures
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
by measures of the form
W(\mu)
with
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
:

Lemma 4.321
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
. Then there exists a sequence
\left(\mu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
with

(W(\mu_{n}))([0,\infty])=\nu([0,\infty])\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}

and

W(\mu_{n})\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu.

Proof.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we define a measure
\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
by

\nu_{n}(A)\coloneqq\nu\left(\left[\frac{1}{n},n\right]\cap A\right)

for every measurable subset
A\subseteq\left[0,\infty\right]
. Further, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
set

\widetilde{\nu}_{n}\coloneqq\nu_{n}+\nu\left(\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)\cdot\delta_{\frac{1}{n}}+\nu\left(\left(n,\infty\right]\right)\cdot\delta_{n},

where for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
by
\delta_{x}
we denote the Dirac measure in
x
. Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, one has

\widetilde{\nu}_{n}(\left[0,\infty\right])=\nu\left(\left[\frac{1}{n},n\right]\right)+\nu\left(\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)+\nu\left(\left(n,\infty\right]\right)=\nu(\left[0,\infty\right]).

We now want to show that
\widetilde{\nu}_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu
. So let
f\in C(\left[0,\infty\right],{\mathbb{R}})
and
\varepsilon>0
. Since
f\in C(\left[0,\infty\right],{\mathbb{R}})
, there exists an
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\left|f(0)-f(x)\right|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{for every }x\in\left[0,\frac{1}{N}\right]

and

\left|f(\infty)-f(x)\right|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{for every }x\in\left[N,\infty\right].

Then, for every
n\geq N
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu-\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\widetilde{\nu}_{n}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)}f\,d\nu+\int_{\left(n,\infty\right]}f\,d\nu-\nu\left(\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)-\nu\left(\left(n,\infty\right]\right)\cdot f\left(n\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)}f(\lambda)-f\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\,d\nu(\lambda)+\int_{\left(n,\infty\right]}f(\lambda)-f\left(n\right)\,d\nu(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)}\left|f(\lambda)-f\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right|\,d\nu(\lambda)+\int_{\left(n,\infty\right]}\left|f(\lambda)-f\left(n\right)\right|\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right)}\varepsilon\,d\nu(\lambda)+\int_{\left(n,\infty\right]}\varepsilon\,d\nu(\lambda)\leq\int_{\left[0,\infty\right]}\varepsilon\,d\nu(\lambda)=\varepsilon\cdot\nu([0,\infty]).

Now, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the measure
\mu_{n}
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\widetilde{\nu}_{n}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Since
\nu_{n}
is finite,
\widetilde{\nu}_{n}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\setminus\left[\frac{1}{n},n\right]\right)=0
and the function

\lambda\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}

is bounded on
\left[\frac{1}{n},n\right]
, the measure
\mu_{n}
is finite, so we have
\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
and per construction we have
W(\mu_{n})=\widetilde{\nu}_{n}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Therefore

W(\mu_{n})\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu

and for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

(W(\mu_{n}))([0,\infty])=\widetilde{\nu}_{n}([0,\infty])=\nu([0,\infty]).\qed

We can now prove a generalization of Corollary
4.314
for all measures
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
:

Theorem 4.322
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then, for all
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, one has

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,g(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.321
, there exists a sequence
\left(\mu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
with

(W(\mu_{n}))([0,\infty])=\nu([0,\infty])\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}

such that

W(\mu_{n})\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu.

Now, by Corollary
4.314
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})}\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})}\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{(S_{s}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})})(i\lambda)}\,(S_{t}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})})(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}W(\mu_{n}))(\lambda).

Since, for every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{(S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)}\,(S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\cdot\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda),

by Lemma
4.319
, we get

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})}\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d(W(\mu_{n}))(\lambda)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}e^{-(s+t)\lambda}|\Lambda(i\lambda)|^{2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{(S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)}\,(S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda).

On the other hand

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,(W(\mu_{n}))([0,\infty])=\nu([0,\infty])<\infty,

so, by Lemma
4.320
, we have

\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{W(\mu_{n})}\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{W(\nu_{n})}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu}\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu}\right\rangle.

This yields

\left\langle S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu}\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu}\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{(S_{s}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)}\,(S_{t}\Lambda F_{\nu})(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)\quad\forall s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Now, by the sesquilinearity of the scalar product, we get

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,g(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)

for every
f,g\in{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})(\Lambda F_{\nu})
. Since, by Lemma
4.310
, we have
\Lambda F_{\nu}\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, by Theorem
A.23
, the subspace
{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})(\Lambda F_{\nu})
is dense in
H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
and therefore the statement follows by Proposition
4.313
.
∎

Corollary 4.323
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h_{\nu}}\right)
is a maximal RPHS.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.322
we get

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}f\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,f(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}|f(i\lambda)|^{2}\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)\geq 0\quad\forall f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

so
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h_{\nu}}\right)
is a RPHS. The maximality then follows by Proposition
4.37
, since, by Lemma
4.310
, we have

F_{\nu}\in\ker(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).\qed

We now know that, starting with a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, we get a maximal RPHS by
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h_{\nu}}\right)
. In the following, we want to see that, in fact, every maximal RPHS is of this form. For this, we need the concept of a Hankel operator:

Definition 4.324
.

(cf.
[
ANS22
]
)
A linear operator
H\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
is called
Hankel operator
, if
S_{t}^{*}H=HS_{t}
for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Lemma 4.325
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
. Then the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS, if and only if

H_{h}\coloneqq p_{+}\theta_{h}p_{+}^{*}

is a positive Hankel operator, where by
p_{+}:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
we denote the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
onto its subspace
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

Proof.

For every
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
we have

\left\langle f\middle|H_{h}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}g\right\rangle,

which immediately implies that
H_{h}
is a positive operator, if and only if
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS. Further, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
we get

\left\langle f\middle|H_{h}S_{t}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}S_{t}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}\theta_{h}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\theta_{h}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|H_{h}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}H_{h}g\right\rangle

and therefore
S_{t}^{*}H=HS_{t}
, so
H_{h}
is a Hankel operator.
∎

We have the following theorem about Hankel operators:

Theorem 4.326
.

(cf.
[
ANS22
, Sec. 3.2]
)

Let
H\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
be a positive Hankel operator. Then there exists a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
such that

\left\langle f\middle|Hg\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Corollary 4.327
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS. Then there exists a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
such that

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Proof.

This follows immediately by Lemma
4.325
and Theorem
4.326
.
∎

With this corollary, given a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS, we can relate the fixed points
F\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
(cf. Corollary
4.39
) to measures:

Proposition 4.328
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS and let

F\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Then there exists a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that
F=C\cdot F_{\nu}
.

Proof.

We will show that there exists a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that
|F|^{2}=\Psi_{\nu}
, since then by Theorem
A.22
there exists a constant
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that

F=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}})=C\cdot F_{\nu}.

For this, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, we define the function

F_{p,n}:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},\quad z\mapsto\frac{\left(z+i\right)^{4}n^{2}}{\pi\left(\left(z+\frac{i}{n}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)\left(zn+i\right)\left(z+in\right)}.

Since there exists a continuous extension of
F_{p,n}
to
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
and

\lim_{|z|\to\infty}F_{p,n}(z)=\frac{n}{\pi},

the function
F_{p,n}
is bounded and therefore we have
F_{p,n}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Further, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

F_{p,n}(i\lambda)=\frac{\left(i\lambda+i\right)^{4}n^{2}}{\pi\left(\left(i\lambda+\frac{i}{n}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)\left(i\lambda n+i\right)\left(i\lambda+in\right)}=\frac{1}{\pi}\cdot\frac{(1+\lambda)^{4}}{\left(p^{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+\lambda\right)\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)}>0.

Then also
\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, where by
\sqrt{F_{p,n}}
we denote the one of the two square roots of
F_{p,n}
on the simply connected domain
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
with
\sqrt{F_{p,n}}(i\lambda)>0
for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then

\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\cdot\Lambda F\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cdot H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we now set

g_{n}(x)\coloneqq\frac{x^{2}}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}.

Since
\Lambda F\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\infty>\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\Lambda(x)|^{2}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

and therefore also

\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx<\infty

for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Now, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, we define

a_{n}\coloneqq\int_{(-1,1)}g_{n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx,\quad b_{n}\coloneqq\int_{(-\infty,-1)\cup(1,\infty)}g_{n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

and

c_{p,n}\coloneqq\left\langle\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\middle|\theta_{h}\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle.

Note that
a_{n},b_{n}\geq 0
, since
g_{n}\geq 0
and
c_{p,n}\geq 0
since
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS.

Since the function

{\mathbb{R}}\ni x\mapsto|\Lambda(x)|

is symmetric and for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the function
{\mathbb{R}}\ni x\mapsto|F_{p,n}(x)|
is symmetric, by Lemma
A.26
, we have

(R\Lambda)(x)=\overline{\Lambda(x)}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(R\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\right)(x)=\overline{\sqrt{F_{p,n}}(x)},\qquad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Using
\theta_{h}F=F
, this yields

\displaystyle c_{p,n}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\middle|\theta_{h}\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle=\left\langle\theta_{h}\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\middle|\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(R\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\right)\left(R\Lambda\right)\theta_{h}F\middle|\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle=\left\langle\overline{\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda}F\middle|\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{p,n}(x)\Lambda(x)^{2}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(x+i\right)^{4}n^{2}}{\pi\left(\left(x+\frac{i}{n}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)\left(xn+i\right)\left(x+in\right)}\cdot\left(\frac{ix}{(x+i)^{2}}\right)^{2}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{-x^{2}n^{2}}{\left(\left(x+\frac{i}{n}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)\left(xn+i\right)\left(x+in\right)}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)+i\frac{2x}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}-ix\right)\left(1-i\frac{x}{n}\right)}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)-i\frac{2x}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+ix\right)\left(1+i\frac{x}{n}\right)}{\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{n\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)-i\frac{2x}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+ix\right)\left(1+i\frac{x}{n}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx.

Since
c_{p,n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, this implies

\displaystyle c_{p,n}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits(c_{p,n})=\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{n\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)-i\frac{2x}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+ix\right)\left(1+i\frac{x}{n}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits\left(n\left(\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)-i\frac{2x}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+ix\right)\left(1+i\frac{x}{n}\right)\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)(1-x^{2})+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}|F(x)|^{2}\,dx,

so defining the function

\displaystyle d_{p,n}(x)\coloneqq g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},

we have

c_{p,n}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}d_{p,n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx.

Then defining

\displaystyle f_{p,n}(x)\coloneqq d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\cdot\left(\chi_{\left(-1,1\right)}(x)\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\chi_{\left(-\infty,-1\right)\cup\left(1,\infty\right)}(x)\right),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},

we have

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{p,n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx=c_{p,n}+\frac{1}{\pi}a_{n}\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}b_{n}\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

This, by Theorem
B14
, yields

\displaystyle|F(p)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{|F(p)|^{2}+|F(-p)|^{2}}{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{p,n}(x)|F(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(c_{p,n}+\frac{1}{\pi}a_{n}\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}b_{n}\right)

for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
a_{n},b_{n},c_{p,n}\geq 0
this implies that the sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(b_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(c_{p,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
all are bounded. Therefore there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
(a_{n_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(b_{n_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are convergent. Setting

A\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{n_{k}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}b_{n_{k}},

we then get

|F(p)|^{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(c_{p,{n_{k}}}+\frac{1}{\pi}a_{n_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}b_{n_{k}}\cdot 1\right)=\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}c_{p,{n_{k}}}\right)+\frac{1}{\pi}A\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}B

for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Therefore, for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the sequence
(c_{p,n_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is convergent. In this case, we set

C_{p}\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}c_{p,{n_{k}}}.

Now, by Corollary
4.327
, there exists a Borel measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
such that

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

This, using that

\Lambda(i\lambda)=\frac{ii\lambda}{(i\lambda+i)^{2}}=\frac{\lambda}{(1+\lambda)^{2}},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

and that
\sqrt{F_{p,n}}(i\lambda)>0
, yields

\displaystyle c_{p,n}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\middle|\theta_{h}\sqrt{F_{p,n}}\Lambda F\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{\sqrt{F_{p,n}}(i\lambda)\Lambda(i\lambda)F(i\lambda)}\cdot\sqrt{F_{p,n}}(i\lambda)\Lambda(i\lambda)F(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}F_{p,n}(i\lambda)\Lambda(i\lambda)^{2}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\cdot\frac{(1+\lambda)^{4}}{\left(p^{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+\lambda\right)\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)}\left(\frac{\lambda}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\right)^{2}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\left(p^{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}+\lambda\right)\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda).

Then, by the Monotone Convergence Theorem, we get

\displaystyle C_{p}=\lim_{k\to\infty}c_{p,n_{k}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\left(p^{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n_{k}}+\lambda\right)\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{n_{k}}\right)}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\left(p^{2}+\lambda^{2}\right)\cdot\lambda\cdot 1}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda).

This in particular implies that the measure
\widetilde{\nu}
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

d\widetilde{\nu}(\lambda)=\left|F(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)

is finite. We then get a finite measure
\nu
on
[0,\infty]
by

\nu\big|_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}=\widetilde{\nu},\quad\nu(\{0\})=A\quad\text{and}\quad\nu(\{\infty\})=B.

Finally, for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle|F(p)|^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{p}+\frac{1}{\pi}A\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}B

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\,d\nu(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\pi}\nu(\{0\})\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi}\nu(\{\infty\})\cdot 1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\,d\nu(\lambda)=\Psi_{\nu}(p).\qed

Theorem 4.329
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS.

(b)

h=h_{\nu}
for some measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

That (b) implies (a) is precisely the statement of Corollary
4.323
. We now show that also the converse is true. So let
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
be a maximal RPHS. Then, by Corollary
4.39
, there exists an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

h=\frac{F}{RF}.

By Proposition
4.328
this implies that there exists a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that

F=C\cdot F_{\nu}.

We get

h=\frac{F}{RF}=\frac{C\cdot F_{\nu}}{C\cdot RF_{\nu}}=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}=h_{\nu}.\qed

4.4
Uniqueness of the measure

In Theorem
4.329
we have seen that, for every function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS, there exists measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that
h=h_{\nu}
. In this subsection we want to discuss to which extent this measure is unique. For this we first notice that, for a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by Lemma
A.25
, we have

F_{c\cdot\nu}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{c\cdot\nu}}\right)=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{c}\cdot\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)=\sqrt{c}\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)=\sqrt{c}\cdot F_{\nu}

and therefore

h_{c\cdot\nu}=\frac{F_{c\cdot\nu}}{RF_{c\cdot\nu}}=\frac{\sqrt{c}\cdot F_{\nu}}{\sqrt{c}\cdot RF_{\nu}}=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}=h_{\nu}.

This shows that the map

\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\ni\nu\mapsto h_{\nu}

is not injective. On the other hand, defining the equivalence relation

\nu??\nu^{\prime}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad(\exists c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+})\,\nu=c\cdot\nu^{\prime}

and denoting the corresponding equivalence classes by
[\nu]
, it shows that the map

\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,\ni[\nu]\mapsto h_{\nu}

is well-defined. We want to show that this map is injective. For this, we need the following proposition:

Proposition 4.41
.

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
we set

q_{z}:[0,\infty]\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad\lambda\mapsto\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}.

Then

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}\right\}}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right\}}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}})

where the closure is taken with respect to the supremum norm on
[0,\infty]
.

Proof.

We set

A\coloneqq\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}\right\}}\subseteq C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})\quad\text{and}\quad B\coloneqq\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right\}}\subseteq C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}}).

We now show that
A
and
B
are algebras. For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
one has

q_{z}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}=\frac{1-z}{\lambda+z}+1.

Further, for
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
, we get

\displaystyle(1-z)(1-w)\cdot\frac{w-z}{(\lambda+z)(\lambda+w)}

\displaystyle=(1-z)(1-w)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\lambda+z}-\frac{1}{\lambda+w}\right)

\displaystyle=(1-w)\cdot\frac{1-z}{\lambda+z}-(1-z)\cdot\frac{1-w}{\lambda+w}

\displaystyle=(1-w)\cdot(q_{z}(\lambda)-1)-(1-z)\cdot(q_{w}(\lambda)-1).

If
z\neq w
this yields

\displaystyle q_{z}(\lambda)\cdot q_{w}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-z}{\lambda+z}+1\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1-w}{\lambda+w}+1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-z)(1-w)}{(\lambda+z)(\lambda+w)}+\frac{1-z}{\lambda+z}+\frac{1-w}{\lambda+w}+1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w-z}\cdot\left((1-w)\cdot(q_{z}(\lambda)-1)-(1-z)\cdot(q_{w}(\lambda)-1)\right)+q_{z}(\lambda)+q_{w}(\lambda)-1

\displaystyle=\frac{1-z}{w-z}q_{z}(\lambda)+\frac{1-w}{z-w}q_{w}(\lambda).

Therefore, for
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
with
z\neq w
one has
q_{z}\cdot q_{w}\in A
and
q_{z}\cdot q_{w}\in B
if
z,w\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Further, for
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|q_{z}(\lambda)-q_{w}(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}-\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+w}\right|=\frac{|\lambda+1|\cdot|w-z|}{|\lambda+z|\cdot|\lambda+w|}

\displaystyle=|q_{z}(\lambda)|\cdot\frac{|w-z|}{|\lambda+w|}\leq|q_{z}(\lambda)|\cdot\frac{|w-z|}{|w|}=|q_{z}(\lambda)|\cdot\left|1-\frac{z}{w}\right|,

so

\left\lVert q_{z}-q_{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert q_{z}\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\left|1-\frac{z}{w}\right|\xrightarrow{w\to z}0.

This shows that, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
, one has

q_{z}\cdot q_{z}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\to z\\
w\neq z\end{subarray}}q_{z}\cdot q_{w}\in A.

By the same argument we get
q_{z}\cdot q_{z}\in B
for all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.
We therefore have

q_{z}\cdot q_{w}\in A\quad\forall z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad q_{z}\cdot q_{w}\in B\quad\forall z,w\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

This implies that

A\cdot A\subseteq A\quad\text{and}\quad B\cdot B\subseteq B,

so that
A
and
B
are algebras. We have

\textbf{1}=q_{1}\in B\subseteq A.

Further, for
\lambda,y\in[0,\infty]
with
\lambda\neq y
, we have

q_{2}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+2}\neq\frac{y+1}{y+2}=q_{2}(y),

so the algebras
A
and
B
separate points. Since

\overline{q_{z}}=q_{\overline{z}},\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}

the algebra
A
is invariant under complex conjugation. Therefore, by the Stone–Weierstraß Theorem, we have

A=\overline{A}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})\quad\text{and}\quad B=\overline{B}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}}).\qed

Corollary 4.42
.

Let
\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
. Then
\Psi_{\nu}=\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}
, if and only if
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since the other implication is trivial, we assume that
\Psi_{\nu}=\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}
and show that then
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
. Setting

f_{p}:[0,\infty]\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad\lambda\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}},\qquad p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},

by assumption, we have

\int_{[0,\infty]}f_{p}\,d\nu=\Psi_{\nu}(p)=\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}(p)=\int_{[0,\infty]}f_{p}\,d\nu^{\prime}\qquad\forall p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}.

This yields that

\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu=\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu^{\prime}\qquad\forall f\in\overline{{\rm span}\{f_{p}:p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\}}.

Now, for
z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we consider the function

q_{z}:[0,\infty]\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad\lambda\mapsto\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}.

Then, defining the map

\mathcal{W}:C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}})\to C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}}),\quad f\mapsto f\circ\sqrt{\cdot},

for
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, we get

(\mathcal{W}f_{p})(\lambda)=f_{p}\big(\sqrt{\lambda}\big)=\frac{1+\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda}=q_{p^{2}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

so
\mathcal{W}f_{p}=q_{p^{2}}
. Using that
\mathcal{W}
is an isometric isomorphism,
by Proposition
4.41
, we get

\displaystyle C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{-1}C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}})=\mathcal{W}^{-1}\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{-1}\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{q_{p^{2}}:p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\right\}}=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\mathcal{W}^{-1}q_{p^{2}}:p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\right\}}=\overline{{\rm span}\{f_{p}:p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\}}.

Therefore, we have

\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu=\int_{[0,\infty]}f\,d\nu^{\prime}\quad\forall f\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{R}}),

which implies
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
.
∎

Another ingredient we need to prove the injectivity of the map

\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,\ni[\nu]\mapsto h_{\nu}

is a better understanding of the kernel

\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)

appearing in Proposition
4.36
. This will be provided by the following proposition:

Proposition 4.43
.

For every measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)={\mathbb{C}}F_{\nu}.

Proof.

For every
C\in{\mathbb{C}}
we have

\Theta_{h_{\nu}}(C\cdot F_{\nu})=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}R(C\cdot F_{\nu})=C\cdot\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}RF_{\nu}=C\cdot F_{\nu}.

This yields

{\mathbb{C}}F_{\nu}\subseteq\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right).

We now show the other inclusion. For this we take a function
F\in\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
. We now want to show that
F\in{\mathbb{C}}F_{\nu}
. If
F=0
, the statement is trivial, so we can assume that
F\neq 0
. By Corollary
4.39
, we have

F\in\ker(\Theta_{h}-\textbf{1})\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore, by Proposition
4.328
there exists a measure
\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that

F=C\cdot F_{\nu^{\prime}}.

We notice that for the function

K(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{|F_{\nu}(x)|\cdot\sqrt{1+x^{2}}},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},

by Lemma
A.27
and Lemma
A.25
, we have

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{1}{F_{\nu}(z)}\cdot\frac{i}{z+i},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Further, by Lemma
4.217
, one has

K(x)=\frac{1}{|F_{\nu}(x)|\cdot\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}(x)}\cdot\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\leq 1,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},

so, by Theorem
A.22
, we have
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. By Lemma
4.310
, we get

\Lambda F=C\cdot\Lambda F_{\nu^{\prime}}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and therefore, by Proposition
A.113
, we have

g\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)\cdot\Lambda F\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Further

g(z)=\frac{1}{F_{\nu}(z)}\cdot\frac{i}{z+i}\cdot\frac{iz}{(z+i)^{2}}\cdot F(z)=-\frac{z}{(z+i)^{3}}\cdot\frac{F(z)}{F_{\nu}(z)},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Then, using that
F,F_{\nu}\in\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{F(x)}{F_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{h_{\nu}(x)\cdot(RF)(x)}{h_{\nu}(x)\cdot(RF_{\nu})(x)}=\frac{F(-x)}{F_{\nu}(-x)},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

and therefore

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{(x+i)^{3}}\cdot\frac{F(x)}{F_{\nu}(x)}=-\frac{x}{(x+i)^{3}}\cdot\frac{F(-x)}{F_{\nu}(-x)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(-x+i)^{3}}{(x+i)^{3}}\cdot g(-x)=\frac{(x-i)^{3}}{(x+i)^{3}}\cdot(Rg)(x)={\varphi_{i}(x)}^{3}(Rg)(x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then, since
Rg\in RH^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
, we get

g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}^{3}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-}).

Now, we have

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})

\displaystyle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\perp}\right)={\mathbb{C}}Q_{i},

using Lemma
3.38
in the last step. This implies

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}^{3}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})

\displaystyle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}^{3}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\perp}\right)=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{k-1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}^{k}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\perp}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{k-1}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap M_{\varphi_{i}}\left(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\perp}\right)\right)=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{k-1}{\mathbb{C}}Q_{i}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{C}}Q_{i}+{\mathbb{C}}M_{\varphi_{i}}Q_{i}+{\mathbb{C}}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{2}Q_{i}=({\mathbb{C}}M_{\textbf{1}}+{\mathbb{C}}M_{\varphi_{i}}+{\mathbb{C}}M_{\varphi_{i}}^{2})\,Q_{i}.

Therefore, there are
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that

g(z)=(a+b\varphi_{i}(z)+c\varphi_{i}(z)^{2})\cdot Q_{i}(z)=\left(a+b\left(\frac{z-i}{z+i}\right)+c\left(\frac{z-i}{z+i}\right)^{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{z+i},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This is equivalent to

\frac{F(z)}{F_{\nu}(z)}=-\frac{(z+i)^{3}}{z}\cdot g(z)=\frac{1}{z}\cdot\frac{-i}{2\pi}\left(a(z+i)^{2}+b(z+i)(z-i)+c(z-i)^{2}\right),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This shows that there are
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that

\frac{F(z)}{F_{\nu}(z)}=\frac{1}{z}\cdot\left(\alpha z^{2}+\beta z+\gamma\right)=\alpha z+\beta+\gamma\frac{1}{z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Since

\alpha x+\beta+\gamma\frac{1}{x}=\frac{F(x)}{F_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{F(-x)}{F_{\nu}(-x)}=-\alpha x+\beta-\gamma\frac{1}{x},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},

we have
\alpha=\gamma=0
and therefore

\frac{F(z)}{F_{\nu}(z)}=\beta,\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

so
F=\beta\cdot F_{\nu}
.
∎

Corollary 4.44
.

The map

\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,\ni[\nu]\mapsto h_{\nu}

is injective.

Proof.

Let
\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
with
h_{\nu}=h_{\nu^{\prime}}
. Then, by Proposition
4.43
, we have

F_{\nu^{\prime}}\in\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu^{\prime}}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)=\ker\left(\Theta_{h_{\nu}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}_{\Lambda}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)={\mathbb{C}}F_{\nu}.

Since
F_{\nu^{\prime}}\neq 0
this implies that there exists
c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
such that

F_{\nu^{\prime}}=c\cdot F_{\nu}.

This yields

\Psi_{\nu^{\prime}}(p)=|F_{\nu^{\prime}}(p)|^{2}=|c|^{2}\cdot|F_{\nu}(p)|^{2}=|c|^{2}\cdot\Psi_{\nu}(p)=\Psi_{|c|^{2}\cdot\nu}(p),\quad p\in{\mathbb{R}}.

This, by Corollary
4.42
, implies
\nu^{\prime}=|c|^{2}\cdot\nu
and therefore
\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu
, so
[\nu^{\prime}]=[\nu]
.
∎

4.5
The Osterwalder–Schrader transform of the Hardy space

In this section, given a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a RPHS, we want to give an explicit form of the Osterwalder–Schrader transform (OS transform) of this RPHS, which is defined as follows:

Definition 4.51
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Def. 3.1.1]
)
Let
\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta\right)
be a RPHS. We set

??\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{+}:\left\langle\eta\middle|\theta\eta\right\rangle=0\right\},

and consider the quotient map

??:\mathcal{E}_{+}\to\mathcal{E}_{+}/\penalty\,\mathcal{N}_{\theta},\quad\eta\mapsto\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}.

On
\mathcal{E}_{+}/\penalty\,\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
we define a scalar product by

\left\langle\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\middle|\xi+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}\coloneqq\left\langle\eta\middle|\theta\xi\right\rangle,\quad\eta,\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}

and write
??
for the Hilbert space completion of
\mathcal{E}_{+}/\penalty\,\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
with respect to the norm

\left\lVert\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right\rVert_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}\coloneqq\sqrt{\left\langle\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\middle|\eta+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}}=\sqrt{\left\langle\eta\middle|\theta\eta\right\rangle},\quad\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{+}.

We call
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}
the
Osterwalder–Schrader transform of
\mathcal{E}_{+}
.

Proposition 4.52
.

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)\eqqcolon(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)
is a RPHS and let
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
such that

\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Further, for a function
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we consider the function
f^{\tt V}\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
defined by

f^{\tt V}(\lambda)=f(i\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Then the map

\Phi:\mathcal{E}_{+}\big/\penalty\,\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right),\quad f+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mapsto f^{\tt V}

extends to an isometric isomorphism of Hilbert spaces

\widetilde{\Phi}:\widehat{\mathcal{E}}\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right).

Proof.

For
f\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
we have

\left\lVert f^{\tt V}\right\rVert^{2}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}f(i\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)=\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h}f\right\rangle=\left\langle f+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\middle|f+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}},

so the map
\Phi
is isometric and therefore well defined and injective. This also shows that
\Phi
maps every Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{E}_{+}\big/\penalty\,\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
to a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
and therefore we can continue
\Phi
to a map
\widetilde{\Phi}:\widehat{\mathcal{E}}\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
in a continuous way. It remains to show that this map
\widetilde{\Phi}
is surjective. So let
F\in\widetilde{\Phi}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})^{\perp}
. Then, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

0=\langle\Phi(Q_{z}+\mathcal{N}_{\theta})|F\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{z}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda).

Since

Q_{z}(i\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{i}{i\lambda-\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{i}{i\lambda+i}\cdot\frac{i\lambda+i}{i\lambda-\overline{z}}=Q_{i}(i\lambda)\cdot\frac{i\lambda+i}{i\lambda-\overline{z}}=Q_{i}(i\lambda)\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

we have

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{z}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda-iz}\cdot\overline{Q_{i}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Further, since
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
is equivalent to
(-iz)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
, this is equivalent to

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}\cdot\overline{Q_{i}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}.

Then Proposition
4.41
implies that

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\cdot\overline{Q_{i}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}}),

so especially

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\cdot\overline{Q_{i}(i\lambda)}F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}).

Since the function
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ni\lambda\mapsto\overline{Q_{i}(i\lambda)}
is continuous and bounded from below on compact intervals, this yields

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)F(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}),

Therefore, by
[
Ru86
, Thm. 3.14]
, we have
F=0\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
. This shows that
\widetilde{\Phi}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})
is dense in
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
, but since
\widetilde{\Phi}
is isometric, we know that
\widetilde{\Phi}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
and therefore is equal to
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mu\right)
.
∎

We now want to apply this proposition to maximal RPHS. By Theorem
4.329
, we know that the functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS, are precisely the functions of the form
h=h_{\nu}
for some measure
{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}}
. So, we are interested in how the Osterwalder–Schrader transform looks in these cases:

Corollary 4.53
.

Let
{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}}
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{+},\theta)\coloneqq\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h_{\nu}}\right)
. Then

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}\cong L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{T}\nu\right),

where the isometric isomorphism between these Hilbert spaces is given by the map

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{T}\nu\right),\quad f+\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\mapsto f^{\tt V}

with

f^{\tt V}(\lambda)\coloneqq f(i\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Proof.

This follows immediately by Theorem
4.322
and Proposition
4.52
.
∎

5
Application to Hankel operators

In the last chapter, we answered the question for which functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
the triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS. The triple
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
being a RPHS is, by Lemma
4.325
, equivalent to the operator

H_{h}\coloneqq p_{+}\theta_{h}p_{+}^{*}=p_{+}M_{h}R\,p_{+}^{*}

being a positive operator, where by
??:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
we denote the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
onto its subspace
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. We have already seen that this operator
H_{h}
is an example of a Hankel operator (cf. Definition
4.324
). These Hankel operators will be the object of investigation in this chapter. We start with the following definition:

Definition 51
.

(a)

We write
??
for the set of Hankel operators
H\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
and write

??\coloneqq\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}):H\geq 0\}

for the set of positive Hankel operators.

(b)

For a function
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}
