we define

??\coloneqq p_{+}M_{h}R\,p_{+}^{*}\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})).

(c)

A function
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}
is called a
symbol
of an Hankel operator
H\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
if
H=H_{h}
.

We have the following result about symbols of Hankel operators:

Theorem 52
.

(Nehari’s Theorem)
(cf.
[
Pa88
, Cor. 4.7]
)

For every
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}
the operator
H_{h}
is a Hankel operator. Conversely, for every Hankel operator
H
there exists
{h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})}
such that
H=H_{h}
. Further, this symbol
h
can be choosen in a way such that
\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert H\right\rVert
.

Since the map

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\ni h\mapsto H_{h}

is linear, the question of whether a given Hankel operator has a unique symbol comes down to the question, for which functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
one has
H_{h}=0
. This question is answered in the following proposition:

Proposition 53
.

(cf.
[
Pa88
, Cor. 4.8]
)

For a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
one has
H_{h}=0
, if and only if
h\in H^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{-}\right)
.

Proof.

One has
H_{h}=0
, if and only if

\{0\}=p_{+}M_{h}Rp_{+}^{*}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=p_{+}M_{h}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-}),

which is equivalent to

M_{h}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{-}).

This, by Proposition
A.113
, is equivalent to
h\in H^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{-}\right)
.
∎

We already know that, given a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
, for which
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
is a maximal RPHS, the Hankel operator
H_{h}
is positive and satisfies

\left\lVert H_{h}\right\rVert=\left\lVert p_{+}M_{h}Rp_{+}^{*}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert M_{h}R\right\rVert=\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}=1.

By Theorem
4.329
, we know that these functions are exactly the functions of the form
h=h_{\nu}
for some measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. So we get a map

\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\mapsto\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\},\quad\nu\mapsto H_{h_{\nu}}.

The goal of this chapter is to show that this map is surjective or in other words: Not only does every maximal RPHS of the form
\left(L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right),\theta_{h}\right)
give rise to a positive contractive Hankel operator, but in fact every positive contractive Hankel operator can be obtained in this manner. To prove this, we need to link Hankel operators to measures, which we will do in the following section.

5.1
Hankel operators, Carleson measures and the WOT

In this section, we want to see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between positive Hankel operators and a certain class of measures, which are called Carleson measures and defined as follows:

Definition 5.11
.

(a)

A measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
is called
Carleson measure
, if the map

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad(f,g)\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

defines a continuous sesquilinear form on
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

(b)

We write
\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
for the set of Carleson measures on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

(c)

Given a Carleson measure
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
, we write
??\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
for the operator defined by

\left\langle f\middle|H_{\mu}g\right\rangle\coloneqq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

We start by showing that the so obtained operators
H_{\mu}\in B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
with
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
are Hankel operators:

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Then
H_{\mu}\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
.

Proof.

For every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
f,g\in H^{2}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle f\middle|H_{\mu}S_{t}g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}e^{it\cdot i\lambda}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}e^{-t\lambda}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{e^{-t\lambda}f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{e^{it\cdot i\lambda}f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|H_{\mu}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}H_{\mu}g\right\rangle

so
HS_{t}=S_{t}^{*}H
and therefore
H_{\mu}
is a Hankel operator. That
H_{\mu}
is positive follows from the fact that, for
f\in H^{2}(\Omega)
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|H_{\mu}f\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}f\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\left|f\left(i\lambda\right)\right|^{2}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)\geq 0.\qed

The following result shows that, in fact, every positive Hankel operator can be obtained in this manner from a unique measure
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
:

Proposition 5.13
.

The map

\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\to\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+},\quad\mu\mapsto H_{\mu}

is a bijection.

Proof.

The surjectivity follows immediately by Theorem
4.326
. We now show the injectivity. So let
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
with
H_{\mu}=H_{\mu^{\prime}}
. This is equivalent to

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f\left(i\lambda\right)}g\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu^{\prime}\left(\lambda\right)\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

which especially implies

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu^{\prime}\left(\lambda\right)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{i\lambda-\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}},

so

4\pi^{2}\overline{Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{\lambda+1}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}}=\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}.

Therefore, since
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, if and only if
i\overline{z}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}
, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+z}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}\,d\mu^{\prime}\left(\lambda\right)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{r}.

By Proposition
4.41
this implies

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}\,d\mu\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}\,d\mu^{\prime}\left(\lambda\right)\quad\forall f\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})

and therefore
\mu=\mu^{\prime}
.
∎

This theorem shows that there is a one-to-one correspondence between positive Hankel operators and Carleson measures. Hence, we can define:

Definition 5.14
.

Let
H
be a positive Hankel operator. Then we write
??\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
for the unique Carleson measure with
H=H_{\mu_{H}}
.

Since we are interested in positive Hankel operators
H
of the form
H=H_{h_{\nu}}
with measures
{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}}
, we want to know what their corresponding Carleson measure is. The following proposition will answer this question:

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then
H_{h_{\nu}}\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
with
\left\lVert H_{\nu_{n}}\right\rVert\leq 1
. Further,
\mathcal{T}\nu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
(cf. Definition
4.311
) and

H_{\mathcal{T}\nu}=H_{h_{\nu}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.322
, for all
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, one has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}\,g(i\lambda)\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)=\left\langle f\middle|\theta_{h_{\nu}}g\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|H_{h_{\nu}}g\right\rangle,

which immediately implies the statement.
∎

Since later we will work with approximations in the weak operator topology, we now want to see how this topology behaves on the set of positive Hankel operators. For this, we make the following definition:

Definition 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space over a field
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
.

(a)

Given a subset
M\subseteq B(\mathcal{H})
, we write

\overline{M}^{??}

for the closure of
M
with respect to the weak operator topology, i.e. the topology induced by the functions

f_{v,w}:B(\mathcal{H})\to{\mathbb{K}},\quad A\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|Aw\right\rangle,\quad v,w\in\mathcal{H}.

(b)

Given
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
and a sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
B(\mathcal{H})
, we write

A_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}A

if
A_{n}
converges to
A
with respect to the weak operator topology, i.e. if

\left\langle v\middle|A_{n}w\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle v\middle|Aw\right\rangle\quad\forall v,w\in\mathcal{H}.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Let
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
and
(A_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
B(\mathcal{H})
with

A_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}A.

Then

\left\lVert A\right\rVert\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert A_{n}\right\rVert.

Proof.

For
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
we have

|\left\langle v\middle|Aw\right\rangle|=\limsup_{n\to\infty}|\left\langle v\middle|A_{n}w\right\rangle|\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert A_{n}\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert v\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert w\right\rVert,

which immediately implies the statement.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

The set

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}

is closed in the weak operator topology.

Proof.

Let
H\in\overline{\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}}
. Since
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
is separable, the weak operator topology restricted to the closed unit ball is metrizable, and therefore there exists a sequence
(H_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}
with

H_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}W.

By Lemma
5.17
we then get

\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{n}\right\rVert\leq 1.

Further, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|HS_{t}g\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle f\middle|H_{n}S_{t}g\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}H_{n}g\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|H_{n}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|Hg\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|S_{t}^{*}Hg\right\rangle,

so
HS_{t}=S_{t}^{*}H
and therefore
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Also, for every
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, one has

\left\langle f\middle|Hf\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle f\middle|H_{n}f\right\rangle\geq 0,

so
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
.
∎

We now want to see how convergence of positive Hankel operators in the weak operator topology translates to convergence of their corresponding Carleson measures. For this, we need the following lemmata:

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
V\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a dense subspace. Then, for
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
and a bounded sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
B(\mathcal{H})
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

A_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}A
.

(b)

\left\langle v\middle|A_{n}w\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle v\middle|Aw\right\rangle\quad\forall v,w\in V
.

Proof.

That (a) implies (b) is trivial. We now assume that (b) holds and let
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Further, let

M\coloneqq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\lVert A_{n}-A\right\rVert<\infty.

Then there exist
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}\in V
with

\left\lVert w-\widetilde{w}\right\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{3(M\left\lVert v\right\rVert+1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert v-\widetilde{v}\right\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{3(M\left\lVert\widetilde{w}\right\rVert+1)}.

Further, since

\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|A_{n}\widetilde{w}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|A\widetilde{w}\right\rangle,

there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\left|\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\quad\forall n\geq N.

Then, for
n\geq N
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle v\middle|(A_{n}-A)w\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle+\left\langle v-\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle+\left\langle v\middle|(A_{n}-A)(w-\widetilde{w})\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle v-\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle v\middle|(A_{n}-A)(w-\widetilde{w})\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left\langle\widetilde{v}\middle|(A_{n}-A)\widetilde{w}\right\rangle\right|+\left\lVert v-\widetilde{v}\right\rVert\cdot M\cdot\left\lVert\widetilde{w}\right\rVert+\left\lVert v\right\rVert\cdot M\cdot\left\lVert w-\widetilde{w}\right\rVert

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}=\varepsilon.\qed

Lemma 5.110
.

Let
B
be a Banach space and
V\subseteq B
be a dense subspace. Then, for
\varphi\in B^{\prime}
and a bounded sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
B^{\prime}
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\varphi
.

(b)

\varphi_{n}(v)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\varphi(v)\quad\forall v\in V
.

Proof.

That (a) implies (b) is trivial. We now assume that (b) holds and let
v\in B
and
\varepsilon>0
. Further, let

M\coloneqq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\lVert\varphi_{n}\right\rVert<\infty.

Then there exists
\widetilde{v}\in V
with

\left\lVert v-\widetilde{v}\right\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M+1)}.

Further, since

\varphi_{n}(\widetilde{v})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\varphi(\widetilde{v}),

there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(\widetilde{v})\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\quad\forall n\geq N.

Then, for
n\geq N
, one has

\displaystyle\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(v)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(\widetilde{v})+(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(v-\widetilde{v})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(\widetilde{v})\right|+\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(v-\widetilde{v})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|(\varphi_{n}-\varphi)(\widetilde{v})\right|+M\cdot\left\lVert v-\widetilde{v}\right\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon.\qed

Proposition 5.111
.

For
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
and a bounded sequence
(H_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
the following are equivalent:

(a)

H_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H
.

(b)

\left\langle Q_{w}\middle|H_{n}Q_{z}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\left\langle Q_{w}\middle|HQ_{z}\right\rangle\quad\forall w,z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.

(c)

One has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{H_{n}}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)

for every function
f\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})
.

(d)

One has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)

for every function
f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})
and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda).

Proof.

(a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b):

This follows immediately by the sesquilinearity of the scalar product, the fact that, by Lemma
A.16
, the set
{\rm span}\{Q_{z}:z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\}
is dense in
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and Lemma
5.19
.

(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c):

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{i\lambda-\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

For
w,z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
this yields

\overline{Q_{w}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda-iw}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda-iw}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

so setting

f_{w,z}:[0,\infty]\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad\lambda\mapsto\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda-iw}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}},

we have
f_{w,z}\in C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})
and

\overline{Q_{w}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{z}\left(i\lambda\right)=f_{w,z}(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Since

f_{i,z}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda-i\cdot i}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda+i\overline{z}},

by Proposition
4.41
, we have

\overline{{\rm span}\{f_{w,z}:w,z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\}}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}}),

where the closure is taken with respect to the supremum norm. The equivalence then follows by Lemma
5.110
, since we have

\overline{Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)=f_{i,i}(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)^{2}}

and therefore

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\cdot\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)}Q_{i}\left(i\lambda\right)\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\cdot\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\left\langle Q_{i}\middle|H_{n}Q_{i}\right\rangle\leq 4\pi^{2}\cdot\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\lVert H_{n}\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert^{2}<\infty.

(c)
\Leftrightarrow
(d):

By the Stone–Weierstraß Theorem we have

\overline{C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus({\mathbb{C}}\cdot\textbf{1})}=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}}),

where the closure is taken with respect to the supremum norm. This, by Lemma
5.110
, implies that (c) holds, if and only if

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)

and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}).

The latter is equivalent to

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\,d\mu_{n}(\lambda)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f(\lambda)\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}),

since the function

{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ni\lambda\mapsto\frac{1}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}

is continuous and bounded from below on compact sets. ∎

As mentioned before, our goal is to show that the map

\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\mapsto\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\},\quad\nu\mapsto H_{h_{\nu}}

is surjective. We will do this in two steps. In the next section, we will show that

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}=\overline{\left\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}

and in the following section, we then will use this to show that

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}=\left\{H_{h_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

5.2
Hankel operators with fixed points and a density theorem

In this section, we start by considering positive Hankel operators
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
and with the additional assumption that
H
has a fixed point. A characterization of these operators is given by the following proposition:

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\ker(H-\textbf{1})\neq\{0\}
.

(b)

There exists a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
such that
H_{h}=H
and
\ker\left(\theta_{h}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\left\{0\right\}
.

(c)

There exists a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that
H=H_{h_{W(\mu)}}
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):

Let
f\in\ker(H-\textbf{1})\setminus\{0\}
. By Nehari’s theorem (Theorem
52
) there exists a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with
\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
such that
H_{h}=H
. Then, denoting by
P_{+}
the orthogonal projection onto
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert H_{h}f\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert P_{+}hRf\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert hRf\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert(\textbf{1}-P_{+})hRf\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}-\left\lVert(\textbf{1}-P_{+})hRf\right\rVert^{2},

which yields
\left\lVert(\textbf{1}-P_{+})hRf\right\rVert^{2}=0
and therefore
(\textbf{1}-P_{+})hRf=0
, so we get

\theta_{h}f=hRf=P_{+}hRf=H_{h}f=f.

This yields
f\in\ker\left(\theta_{h}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and therefore

\ker\left(\theta_{h}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\left\{0\right\}.

Further, using Theorem
A.14
, we get

h=\frac{f}{Rf}.

This yields

h=\frac{f}{Rf}=R\left(\frac{Rf}{f}\right)=R\left(\frac{1}{h}\right)=\frac{1}{Rh}.

Then, for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we get

1\geq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\left|h(x)\right|=\frac{1}{|h(-x)|}\geq\frac{1}{\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}}\geq 1

and therefore
h(x)\in{\mathbb{T}}
, so we have
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})
. This yields
\frac{1}{h}=\overline{h}
and therefore

h^{\flat}=\overline{Rh}=\overline{\left(\frac{1}{h}\right)}=h.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):

Let
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
and
f\in\left(\ker\left(\theta_{h}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then

Hf=H_{h}f=p_{+}\theta_{h}p_{+}^{*}f=p_{+}\theta_{h}f=p_{+}f=f

and therefore
f\in\ker(H-\textbf{1})\setminus\{0\}
.

(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c):

By Lemma
4.325
, for a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
, one has
H_{h}\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
, if and only if
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS. Further, by Theorem
4.221
, the functions
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{T}})^{\flat}
for which
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),\theta_{h})
is a RPHS and
\ker\left(\theta_{h}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\neq\left\{0\right\}
are precisely the functions of the form
h_{W(\mu)}
for some measure
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. ∎

We now want to find a sufficient condition for a Hankel operator to have a fixed point, that is easy to check. One of these conditions is that the Hankel operator is compact:

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
H
be a compact positive Hankel operator with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert=1
. Then

\ker(H-\textbf{1})\neq\{0\}.

Proof.

By the spectral theorem for compact self-adjoint operators, there is a null sequence
\left(\lambda_{k}\right)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
with

\lambda_{k+1}\leq\lambda_{k}\quad\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}

and a sequence of orthogonal finite-dimensional projections
\left(P_{k}\right)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
B(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}))
such that

H=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}P_{k}.

Since
\left\lVert H\right\rVert=1
, we have
\lambda_{1}=1
and
P_{1}\neq 0
. Therefore we can choose a function
f\in P_{1}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\setminus\{0\}
and get

Hf=\lambda_{1}P_{1}f=f.\qed

A powerful tool for checking if a Hankel operator is compact is given by Hartman’s Theorem:

Theorem 5.23
.

(Hartman’s Theorem for the upper half-plane)

A Hankel operator
H
is compact, if and only if
H
has a continuous symbol
h\in C({\mathbb{R}})
with

\lim_{p\to-\infty}h(p)=\lim_{p\to\infty}h(p).

Proof.

This follows immediately by transforming Hartman’s Theorem (
[
Pa88
, Thm. 3.20]
) from the unit disc to the upper half-plane.
∎

In general, it is quite difficult to find a concrete symbol for a given Hankel operator, even more so to find a continuous one as it is required in Hartman’s Theorem, but for positive Hankel operators, the following theorem provides concrete symbols with good analytic properties:

Theorem 5.24
.

(cf.
[
ANS22
, Thm. 4.1]
)

Let
H
be a positive Hankel operator and set

h\left(p\right)\coloneqq\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu_{H}\left(\lambda\right),\quad p\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then
h\in L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right)
and
H_{h}=H
.

This result, together with Hartman’s Theorem, allows us to provide an easy-to-check criterion for a positive Hankel operator to be compact:

Proposition 5.25
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{BM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
with

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\,d\mu(\lambda)<\infty.

Then
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
and the Hankel operator
H_{\mu}
is compact.

Proof.

For
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by Proposition
A.17
, we have

|f(i\lambda)|=|\left\langle Q_{i\lambda}\middle|f\right\rangle|\leq\left\lVert Q_{i\lambda}\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert f\right\rVert=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\lambda}}\cdot\left\lVert f\right\rVert.

Therefore, for
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we get

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}|f(i\lambda)|\cdot|g(i\lambda)|\,d\mu(\lambda)\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\lambda}}\cdot\left\lVert f\right\rVert\cdot\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\lambda}}\cdot\left\lVert g\right\rVert\,d\mu(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\,d\mu(\lambda)\cdot\left\lVert f\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert g\right\rVert,

which shows that
\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
. Further, by Theorem
5.24
, we know that, for the function

h(p)\coloneqq\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda),

we have
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
H_{h}=H_{\mu}
. We now show that
h\in C({\mathbb{R}})
with

\lim_{p\to-\infty}h(p)=\lim_{p\to\infty}h(p),

since then the statement follows by Theorem
5.23
. For every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\left|\frac{p}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\right|=\frac{\left|p\right|}{\lambda^{2}+\left|p\right|^{2}}\leq\frac{\left|p\right|}{2\lambda\left|p\right|}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}.

Then, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, for every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\lim_{p^{\prime}\to p}h(p^{\prime})=\lim_{p^{\prime}\to p}\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p^{\prime}}{\lambda^{2}+p^{\prime 2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)=\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)=h(p),

so
h\in C({\mathbb{R}})
. Also, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we get

\lim_{p\to\pm\infty}h(p)=\lim_{p\to\pm\infty}\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p}{\lambda^{2}+p^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)=\frac{i}{\pi}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}0\,d\mu(\lambda)=0,

which implies the statement.
∎

Corollary 5.26
.

Let
H
be a positive Hankel operator with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert=1
. Then there exists a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that

H_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H.

Proof.

For
t\in\left[1,\infty\right)
we define the measure
\rho_{t}
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

\rho_{t}(A)\coloneqq\mu_{H}\left(A\cap\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]\right)

for every Borel set
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\,d\rho_{t}(\lambda)=\int_{\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}\frac{1}{\lambda}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)\leq\int_{\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}t\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)=t\cdot\mu_{H}\left(\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]\right)<\infty,

so, by Proposition
5.25
, we have
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)
and the Hankel operator
H_{\rho_{t}}
is compact. Further, for
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
t\in[1,\infty)
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle f\middle|(H-H_{\rho_{t}})g\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}\left|f(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)}\cdot\sqrt{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}\left|g(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)}\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0.

This implies that
H_{\rho_{t}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}}H
. We have

\left\langle f\middle|H_{\rho_{t}}f\right\rangle=\int_{\left[\frac{1}{t},t\right]}\left|f(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\left|f(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\,d\mu_{H}(\lambda)=\left\langle f\middle|Hf\right\rangle,

and therefore
\left\lVert H_{\rho_{t}}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert H\right\rVert
. This, by Lemma
5.17
, yields

1=\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{\rho_{t}}\right\rVert\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\left\lVert H\right\rVert=1.

Therefore, we can choose a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that

t_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert H_{\rho_{t_{n}}}\right\rVert\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1

and

H_{\rho_{t_{n}}}\neq 0\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

We now set

H_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\left\lVert H_{\rho_{t_{n}}}\right\rVert}H_{\rho_{t_{n}}}.

Then

H_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H.

Further, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the operator
H_{n}
is a positive compact Hankel operator with
\left\lVert H_{n}\right\rVert=1
, so, by Lemma
5.22
and Proposition
5.21
, there exists a measure
\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that
H_{n}=H_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}
. We then have

H_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}=H_{n}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H.\qed

Proposition 5.27
.

One has

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}=\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.15
we have

\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}\subseteq\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}

and therefore, using Lemma
5.18
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}

\displaystyle=\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}.

We now show the other inclusion. For every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and every
f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|H_{\delta_{x}}g\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{f(i\lambda)}g(i\lambda)\,d\delta_{x}(\lambda)=\overline{f(ix)}g(ix)=\langle f|Q_{ix}\rangle\left\langle Q_{ix}\middle|g\right\rangle.

Then, by Proposition
A.17
, we have

\left\lVert H_{\delta_{x}}\right\rVert=\left\lVert Q_{ix}\right\rVert^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi x}.

Therefore, for the measure

\rho_{x}\coloneqq 4\pi x\cdot\delta_{x},

we have
\left\lVert H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert=1
. Now, let
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
. Then, for every
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we have

\left\langle f\middle|(H+H_{\rho_{x}})f\right\rangle=\left\langle f\middle|Hf\right\rangle+\left\langle f\middle|H_{\rho_{x}}f\right\rangle\geq\left\langle f\middle|H_{\rho_{x}}f\right\rangle,

so

\left\lVert H+H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert=1.

Therefore, for the function

F_{x}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad t\mapsto\left\lVert H+t\cdot H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert,

we have

F_{x}(0)=\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad F_{x}(1)=\left\lVert H+H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert\geq 1,

so, by the Intermediate Value Theorem, there exists
t_{x}\in[0,1]
such that

1=F_{x}(t_{x})=\left\lVert H+t_{x}\cdot H_{\rho_{x}}\right\rVert.

This, by Corollary
5.26
, implies

H+t_{x}\cdot H_{\rho_{x}}\in\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}},\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Further, for
w,z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{w}\middle|H_{\rho_{x}}Q_{z}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=4\pi x\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\overline{Q_{w}(i\lambda)}Q_{z}(i\lambda)\,d\delta_{x}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=4\pi x\cdot\overline{Q_{w}(ix)}Q_{z}(ix)=\frac{x}{\pi}\cdot\overline{\frac{1}{ix-\overline{w}}}\frac{1}{ix-\overline{z}}\xrightarrow{x\to 0}0,

so, by Proposition
5.111
, we have

H_{\rho_{x}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{x\to 0}}0

and therefore

H+t_{x}\cdot H_{\rho_{x}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{x\to 0}}H,

which shows that

H\in\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}.

We therefore have

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}\subseteq\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}.\qed

5.3
Convergence in the weak-
*
-topology

In this section, we want to better understand the set

\left\{H_{h_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

For this we have to know, given a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and a sequence
(\nu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
with

\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu,

what are the consequences for the measures
\mathcal{T}\nu
and
(\mathcal{T}\nu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
. We start by the following definition:

Definition 5.31
.

For a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
we define the continuous function

??:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\quad t_{\nu}(\lambda)=\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}

with
F_{\nu}
as in Definition
4.219
.

Remark 5.32
.

By definition, for
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
, we have

d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)=t_{\nu}(\lambda)\,d\nu(\lambda)

(cf. Definition
4.311
).

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
\left(\nu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
with

\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu.

Then the functions
(t_{\nu_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converge to
t_{\nu}
uniformly on compact sets.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.25
, we have

F_{\nu}^{2}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)^{2}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(\Psi_{\nu})

and analogously
F_{\nu_{n}}^{2}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(\Psi_{\nu_{n}})
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so, by Lemma
A.26
, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, one has

t_{\nu}(\lambda)=\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)

and

t_{\nu_{n}}(\lambda)=\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)dp\right).

Therefore, it suffices to show that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)dp\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)dp

uniformly on
[a,b]
with
0<a<b<\infty
. Since, for every
\lambda\in[a,b]
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)dp-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)dp\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|dp

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{b}{p^{2}+a^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|dp

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq b\cdot\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right\}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+p^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|dp,

it remains to show that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+p^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|dp\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Since by assumption

\nu_{n}([0,\infty])=\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu=\nu([0,\infty])\neq 0,

we have that

M\coloneqq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\left|\log\left(\nu_{n}([0,\infty])\right)\right|<\infty.

Then, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, by Lemma
4.217
, we have

\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|=\left|\log\left(\nu_{n}([0,\infty])\right)\right|+\left|\log\left(\frac{\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)}{\nu_{n}([0,\infty])}\right)\right|\leq M+\left|\log\left(p^{2}\right)\right|

and analogously

\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)\right|\leq M+\left|\log\left(p^{2}\right)\right|.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
this yields

\frac{1}{1+p^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)\right|+\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}\leq 2\cdot\frac{M+\left|\log\left(p^{2}\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}

Then, since

\int_{\mathbb{R}}2\cdot\frac{M+\left|\log\left(p^{2}\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp<\infty,

and since, by Lemma
4.318
, we have

\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0,\quad\forall p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},

by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+p^{2}}\left|\log\left(\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(\Psi_{\nu_{n}}\left(p\right)\right)\right|dp\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.\qed

Proposition 5.34
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
and
\left(\nu_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
with

\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu.

Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}).

Proof.

Since by assumption

\nu_{n}([0,\infty])=\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu=\nu([0,\infty]),

we have that

M\coloneqq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\nu_{n}([0,\infty])<\infty.

Further, by Lemma
5.33
, the functions
(t_{\nu_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converge uniformly to
t_{\nu}
on compact sets. Therefore, for every function
f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})
one has

\left\lVert f\cdot\left(t_{\nu_{n}}-t_{\nu}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

This yields

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot\left(t_{\nu}-t_{\nu_{n}}\right)\,d\nu_{n}\right|\leq\left\lVert f\cdot\left(t_{\nu}-t_{\nu_{n}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\nu_{n}([0,\infty])=\left\lVert f\cdot\left(t_{\nu}-t_{\nu_{n}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot M\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Also we have
f\cdot t_{\nu}\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})
, so

\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu}\,d\nu-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu}\,d\nu_{n}\right|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

This yields

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu_{n})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu}\,d\nu-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu_{n}}\,d{\nu_{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu}\,d\nu-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot t_{\nu}\,d\nu_{n}\right|+\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\cdot\left(t_{\nu}-t_{\nu_{n}}\right)\,d\nu_{n}\right|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0+0=0.\qed

Theorem 5.35
.

One has

\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}=\left\{H_{h_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.15
we have

\left\{H_{h_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}\subseteq\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\}.

We now show the other inclusion. So let
H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}
with
\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1
. Then, by Proposition
5.27
, we have

H\in\overline{\{H_{h_{W(\mu)}}:\mu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\}}^{\mathrm{WOT}}.

Since
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
is separable and therefore the weak operator topology restricted to the closed unit ball is metrizable, this implies that there exists a sequence of measures
(\mu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
such that

H_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we now set

\nu_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{(W(\mu_{n}))([0,\infty])}W(\mu_{n}).

Since, by Remark
4.312
, we have

\mathcal{T}\left(\nu_{n}\right)=\mathcal{T}\left(W(\mu_{n})\right),

by Proposition
5.15
, we get

H_{\mathcal{T}\nu_{n}}=H_{\mathcal{T}W(\mu_{n})}=H_{h_{W(\mu_{n})}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H.

Further, by construction, we have

\nu_{n}([0,\infty])=1\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

The set

\{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right):\nu([0,\infty])\leq 1\}

is a closed subset of

B_{1}(0)=\{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathbb{C}}\left([0,\infty]\right):|\nu|([0,\infty])\leq 1\}

with respect to the weak-
*
-topology on

\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{\mathbb{C}}\left([0,\infty]\right)=C([0,\infty],{\mathbb{C}})^{\prime}

and therefore sequentially compact by the Banach–Alaoglu Theorem. Therefore, there exists a weakly convergent subsequence of
(\nu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
. By replacing
(\nu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with that subsequence, we can assume that

\nu_{n}\underset{w}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}\nu

for some measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)
. Then

1=\nu_{n}([0,\infty])=\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,\infty]}\textbf{1}\,d\nu=\nu([0,\infty]),

so
\nu([0,\infty])=1
and therefore
\nu\neq 0
.
Then, by Proposition
5.34
, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu)\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}).

But, since

H_{\mathcal{T}\nu_{n}}\underset{\mathrm{WOT}}{\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}}H,

we also have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d(\mathcal{T}\nu_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}f\,d\mu_{H}\quad\forall f\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})

by Proposition
5.111
. This implies
\mu_{H}=\mathcal{T}\nu
and therefore, by Proposition
5.15
, we have

H=H_{\mathcal{T}\nu}=H_{h_{\nu}}.\qed

Corollary 5.36
.

One has

\{\mu\in\mathcal{CM}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right):\left\lVert H_{\mu}\right\rVert\leq 1\}=\left\{\mathcal{T}\nu:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.15
we have

\left\{H_{h_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}=\left\{H_{\mathcal{T}\nu}:\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right\}

The statement then follows by Theorem
5.35
and Proposition
5.13
.
∎

Example 5.37
.

For the measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right)
defined by

d\nu(\lambda)=\frac{2}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda

we get

\displaystyle\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\cdot\frac{2}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=\frac{2}{\pi|p|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{1+\big(\frac{\lambda}{|p|}\big)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{|p|}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi|p|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\frac{2}{\pi|p|}\cdot[\arctan(x)]_{0}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{\left|p\right|}.

Then the outer function
F_{\nu}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{\nu}}\right)
, by Lemma
A.27
, is given by

F_{\nu}(z)=\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2z}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

where, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, by
\sqrt{z}
we denote the square root of
z
with
\mathrm{Re}(\sqrt{z})>0
.
For
x>0
this yields

h_{\nu}(x)=\frac{F_{\nu}(x)}{RF_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2x}}}{\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{-2x}}}=\frac{\sqrt{-x}}{\sqrt{x}}=i

and analogously we get
h_{\nu}(x)=-i
for every
x<0
, so we have

h_{\nu}=i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits.

Further, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

F_{\nu}(i\lambda)^{2}=\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2i\lambda}}\right)^{2}=\frac{(1+i)^{2}}{2i\lambda}=\frac{1}{\lambda},

so

d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)=\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda)=\lambda\cdot\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{2}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=2\,d\lambda

and therefore

\mathcal{T}\nu=2\lambda_{1}.

5.4
Uniqueness of the measure

In Section
4.4
we have seen, that, defining on
\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
the equivalence relation

\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad(\exists c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+})\,\nu=c\cdot\nu^{\prime},

one has

h_{\nu}=h_{\nu^{\prime}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}.

This, of course, implies that

\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\quad\Rightarrow\quad H_{h_{\nu}}=H_{h_{\nu^{\prime}}}

and therefore, we can define the map

\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,\to\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\},\quad[\nu]\mapsto H_{h_{\nu}}.

The goal of this section is to answer the question of whether it is possible to prove an analogue of Corollary
4.44
, i.e. to answer the question of whether the above map is injective. The following lemma shows that, in this generality, this is not true:

Lemma 5.41
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{T}\nu=0
.

(b)

\nu=s\cdot\delta_{0}+t\cdot\delta_{\infty}
for some
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

That
\mathcal{T}\nu=0
is equivalent to

0=d(\mathcal{T}\nu)(\lambda)=\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\,d\nu(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

and therefore to

\nu({\mathbb{R}}_{+})=0,

since

\left|F_{\nu}(i\lambda)\right|^{-2}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}>0\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

This shows that for a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left(\left[0,\infty\right]\right)\setminus\{0\}
one has

\mathcal{T}\nu=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nu({\mathbb{R}}_{+})=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\exists s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}:\nu=s\cdot\delta_{0}+t\cdot\delta_{\infty}).\qed

Example 5.42
.

(a)

For the measure
\nu=\pi\cdot\delta_{0}
, one gets

\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)=\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\cdot\,d\delta_{0}(\lambda)=\frac{1+0^{2}}{p^{2}+0^{2}}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}.

Then, by Lemma
A.27
, one has

F_{\nu}(z)=\frac{i}{z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and therefore

h_{\nu}(x)=\frac{F_{\nu}(x)}{RF_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{\frac{i}{x}}{\frac{i}{-x}}=-1,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

and indeed, one has
H_{-\textbf{1}}=-p_{+}Rp_{+}^{*}=0
.

(b)

For the measure
\nu=\pi\cdot\delta_{\infty}
, one gets

\Psi_{\nu}\left(p\right)=\int_{[0,\infty]}\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\cdot\,d\delta_{\infty}(\lambda)=1.

Then, one has
F_{\nu}\equiv 1
and therefore

h_{\nu}=\frac{F_{\nu}}{RF_{\nu}}=\frac{\textbf{1}}{R\textbf{1}}=\textbf{1}

and indeed, one has
H_{\textbf{1}}=p_{+}Rp_{+}^{*}=0
.

This example shows that the map

\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left([0,\infty]\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,\to\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1\},\quad[\nu]\mapsto H_{h_{\nu}}

is not injective, since we have
\pi\cdot\delta_{0}\not\sim\pi\cdot\delta_{\infty}
but

H_{h_{\pi\cdot\delta_{0}}}=0=H_{h_{\pi\cdot\delta_{\infty}}}.

However, we will now see that, when one restricts it to positive Hankel operators with a non-zero fixed point, it is, in fact, a bijection:

Proposition 5.43
.

The map

\displaystyle W\left(\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}\right)/\penalty\sim\,

\displaystyle\to\{H\in\mathrm{Han}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{+}:\left\lVert H\right\rVert\leq 1,\,\ker(H-\textbf{1})\neq\{0\}\}

\displaystyle[W(\mu)]

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{h_{W(\mu)}}

is a bijection.

Proof.

The surjectivity follows immediately by Proposition
5.21
. We now show the injectivity. So let
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\right)\setminus\{0\}
with

H_{h_{W(\mu)}}=H_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}.

We set

f\coloneqq F_{W(\mu)}\in\ker\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

and

g\coloneqq F_{W(\mu^{\prime})}\in\ker\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}-{\bf 1}\right)\cap\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

(cf. Definition
4.219
). Then, for
t\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}-{\bf 1}\right)g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|(H_{h_{W(\mu)}}-\textbf{1})g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|(H_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}-\textbf{1})g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}-{\bf 1}\right)g\right\rangle=0

and for
t\leq 0
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}S_{t}f\middle|g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{-t}\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}f\middle|g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{-t}f\middle|g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{-t}f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{-t}f\middle|H_{h_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{-t}f\middle|H_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{-t}f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle=\left\langle\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}S_{-t}f\middle|g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}f\middle|g\right\rangle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|g\right\rangle

and therefore

\displaystyle\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}-{\bf 1}\right)g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g\right\rangle-\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|g\right\rangle=0.

This yields

\left\langle S_{t}f\middle|\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}-{\bf 1}\right)g\right\rangle=0\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Since, by Corollary
A.24
, we have
\overline{{\rm span}S({\mathbb{R}})f}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, we get
\left(\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}-{\bf 1}\right)g=0
. This implies
\theta_{h_{W(\mu)}}g=g
and therefore

h_{W(\mu)}=\frac{g}{Rg}=\frac{F_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}{RF_{W(\mu^{\prime})}}=h_{W(\mu^{\prime})},

so, by Corollary
4.44
, we get
[W(\mu)]=[W(\mu^{\prime})]
.
∎

6
Application to standard subspaces

In this chapter, we will investigate so-called standard subspaces and certain unitary one-parameter groups that are compatible with a standard subspace. We will relate these unitary one-parameter groups to real (reflection positive) ROPGs using Borchers’ Theorem. To understand Borchers’ Theorem, we need some basic theory of standard subspaces as well as basics of the representation theory of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
.

6.1
Standard subspaces

We start with the definition and basic theory of standard subspaces:

Definition 6.11
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Def. 5.1.3]
)
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space.

(a)

A closed real subspace
??\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is called a
standard subspace
, if

{\tt V}\cap i{\tt V}=\{0\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{{\tt V}+i{\tt V}}=\mathcal{H}.

(b)

Given a standard subspace
{\tt V}
we consider the densely defined
Tomita operator
given by

??:{\tt V}+i{\tt V}\to\mathcal{H},\quad x+iy\mapsto x-iy.

(c)

We write
??
for the anti-unitary involution and
??
for the strictly positive, densely defined operator one gets from the polar decomposition

T_{\tt V}=J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}

of the Tomita operator. We call
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
the pair of
modular objects
of
{\tt V}
.

Proposition 6.12
.

(cf.
[
NÓ18
, Rem. 5.1.4]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a standard subspace. Then the pair
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
of modular objects of
{\tt V}
satisfies the relation

J_{V}\Delta_{V}=\Delta_{V}^{-1}J_{V}

and one has

{\tt V}=\mathrm{Fix}\big(J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)

Conversely, for every pair of a strictly positive selfadjoint operator
\Delta
and an anti-unitary involution
J
satisfying
J\Delta=\Delta^{-1}J
, the space
{\tt V}\coloneqq\mathrm{Fix}\big(J\Delta^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)
is a standard subspace.

This proposition shows that, starting with a standard subspace
{\tt V}
and considering its modular pair
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
, one gets another modular pair by
(\Delta_{\tt V}^{-1},J_{\tt V})
, which gives rise to another standard subspace. To get a more explicit form of that standard subspace, we make the following definition:

Definition 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a standard subspace. Then we set

??\coloneqq(i{\tt V})^{\perp_{\mathbb{R}}},

where, by
\perp_{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote the orthogonal complement with respect to the real scalar product

\left\langle\cdot\middle|\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad(v,w)\mapsto\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits\,\left\langle v\middle|w\right\rangle.

Lemma 6.14
.

(cf.
[
NÓ17
, Lem. 3.7]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
a standard subspace. Then
{\tt V}^{\prime}
is also a standard subspace and

J_{{\tt V}^{\prime}}=J_{{\tt V}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{{\tt V}^{\prime}}=\Delta_{{\tt V}}^{-1}.

6.2
Positive energy representations of the affine group

In this section, we provide some basic results of the representation theory of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
. The affine group is an example of a so-called graded group, defined as follows:

Definition 6.21
.

A
graded group
is a pair
\left(G,\varepsilon_{G}\right)
of a group
G
and a group homomorphism
\varepsilon_{G}:G\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. We set
G_{\pm}\coloneqq\varepsilon_{G}^{-1}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right)
.

Example 6.22
.

(a)

The affine group
??={\mathbb{R}}\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
becomes a graded group with the grading

??={\mathbb{R}}\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{-}={\mathbb{R}}\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}_{-}.

(b)

Given a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
we consider the group
??
of unitary and anti-unitary operators on
\mathcal{H}
. This becomes a graded group with the grading

\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)_{+}=\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)_{-}=\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)\setminus\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}).

Now that we have the concept of a graded group, we want to define what a representation of a graded group is:

Definition 6.23
.

An
anti-unitary representation
of a graded group
(G,\varepsilon_{G})
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a morphism of graded groups
{\rho:G\to\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}
.

We are especially interested in so-called positive energy representations of the affine group, which are defined as follows:

Definition 6.24
.

(a)

Given a unitary representation
{\rho:\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}\to\mathrm{U}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}
of the
ax+b
-group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}
, we say that
\rho
is a
positive energy representation
, if, for the one-parameter group

U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}),\quad t\mapsto\rho(t,1),

one has
\partial U\geq 0
. We call the representation
\rho
a
strictly positive energy representation
if additionally
{\ker(\partial U)=\{0\}}
.

(b)

An anti-unitary representation
{\rho:\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})\to\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}
of the affine group is a (strictly) positive energy representation if the restriction
{\rho\big|_{\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}}:\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}\to\mathrm{AU}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}
is a (strictly) positive energy representation.

Example 6.25
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a real Hilbert space. We set

??\coloneqq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}.

Then
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
becomes a complex Hilbert space with the complex structure

I:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},\quad f\mapsto i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits\cdot f.

Then the map
??:\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})\to\mathrm{AU}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}})
with

(\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(t,1)f)(x)\coloneqq e^{itx}f(x),\quad(\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,e^{s})f)(x)\coloneqq e^{\frac{s}{2}}f(e^{s}x)\qquad s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

and

(\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,-1)f)(x)\coloneqq i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits(x)\cdot f(-x)

defines a strictly positive energy representation of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
.

The following proposition shows that, in fact, every strictly positive energy representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
is of this form:

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
be a strictly positive energy representation of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
is equivalent to the anti-unitary representation
(\rho_{\mathcal{K}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}})
. Further, the representation
(\rho_{\mathcal{K}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}})
is irreducible, if and only if
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

By
[
Lo08
, Thm. 1.6.1]
there exists – up to equivalence – exactly one irreducible unitary representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}
with strictly positive energy. By
[
NÓ17
, Sec. 2.4.1]
this representation
U
can be realized on the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}})
by

(U(t,1)f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x)\quad\text{and}\quad(U(0,e^{s})f)(x)\coloneqq e^{\frac{s}{2}}f(e^{s}x),\qquad f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}),s,t,x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Via the map

\Psi:L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{C}}\right)\to L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right)^{\sharp},\quad(\Psi f)(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}f(x)&\text{if }x>0\\[3.22916pt]
\overline{f(-x)}&\text{if }x<0,\end{cases}

this representation is equivalent to the representation
\rho_{\mathbb{R}}\big|_{\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}}
.

Further, by
[
Lo08
, Thm. 1.6.1]
, every unitary representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}
with strictly positive energy is a multiple of this unique irreducible representation, so every strictly positive energy representation
(\rho,\mathcal{H})
of the group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}
is equivalent to

\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq\rho_{\mathcal{K}}\big|_{\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}}:\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})

for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. Now, by
[
Lo08
, Thm. 1.6.3]
, for every unitary representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})_{+}
, there exists an – up to equivalence – unique extension to an anti-unitary representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
on the same Hilbert space. Since
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}
is such an extension of
\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathcal{K}}
, every anti-unitary representation of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
is equivalent to
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. Finally, by
[
Lo08
, Thm. 1.6.3]
the representation
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}
is irreducible, if and only if
\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathcal{K}}
is irreducible, which is the case, if and only if
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

6.3
Extending Borchers’ Theorem beyond standard pairs

To state Borchers’ Theorem, which links strictly positive energy representations of the affine group to standard subspaces, we need one more definition:

Definition 6.31
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space,
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
a standard subspace and
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
a strongly continuous one-parameter group. We call the pair
({\tt V},U)
a
standard pair
on
\mathcal{H}
, if

U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial U\geq 0.

We say that a standard pair
({\tt V},U)
is non-degenerate if
\ker(\partial U)=\{0\}
.

Theorem 6.32
.

(Borchers’ Theorem (one-particle))
(
[
Bo92
, Thm. II.9]
,
[
Lo08
, Thm. 2.2.1]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and let
({\tt V},U)
be a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the following commutation relations hold:

\Delta_{\tt V}^{is}U_{t}\Delta_{\tt V}^{-is}=U_{e^{-2\pi s}t},\qquad J_{\tt V}U_{t}J_{\tt V}=U_{-t}\qquad\forall s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Therefore

\rho(t,1)\coloneqq U_{t},\quad\rho(0,e^{s})\coloneqq\Delta_{\tt V}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho(0,-1)\coloneqq J_{\tt V}\qquad s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

defines a strictly positive energy representation
V
of
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
on
\mathcal{H}
.

Borchers’ Theorem shows us that for every non-degenerate standard pair, we get a strictly positive energy representation of the affine group
\mathrm{Aff}({\mathbb{R}})
. The following proposition shows that conversely, starting with a strictly positive energy representation of the affine group, we also obtain a non-degenerate standard pair:

Proposition 6.33
.

For every real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
, the space
??\coloneqq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
is a standard subspace in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
with

J_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}=\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,-1)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}=\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,e^{s}),\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Further, defining the one-parameter group

U_{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}),\quad t\mapsto\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(t,1),

the pair
({\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},U_{\mathcal{K}})
is a non-degenerate standard pair.

Proof.

We consider the set

\mathcal{S}_{\pi}\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:0<\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(z)<\pi\}

and the corresponding Hardy space

H^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\pi})\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S}_{\pi}):\sup_{y\in(0,\pi)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x+iy)|^{2}\,dx<\infty\right\}.

By
[
LL15
, Sec. 4]
for every function
f\in H^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\pi})
the lower and upper boundary values

f(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}f(x+i\varepsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad f(x+i\pi)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}f(x+i(\pi-\varepsilon))

exist for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and the lower boundary values define a function in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Now, by
[
LL15
, Lem. 4.1]
, the space

H\coloneqq\{f\in H^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\pi}):\overline{f(x+i\pi)}=f(x)\text{ for almost every }x\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

is a standard subspace in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

J_{H}f=\overline{f}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left(\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}f\right)(x)=f(x+s),\quad f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),s,x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We now consider the unitary map

\Psi:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}},\quad\begin{cases}\frac{(1+i)}{2\sqrt{x}}f(\log(x))&\text{if }x>0\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{(1+i)}{2\sqrt{-x}}\overline{f(\log(-x))}&\text{if }x<0.\end{cases}

Then

\Psi(H)=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\sharp}={\tt V}_{\mathbb{R}}.

This shows that
{\tt V}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a standard subspace in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
with

J_{{\tt V}_{\mathbb{R}}}=\Psi\circ J_{H}\circ\Psi^{-1}=\rho_{\mathbb{R}}(0,-1)

and

\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\mathbb{R}}}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}=\Psi\circ\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}\circ\Psi^{-1}=\rho_{\mathbb{R}}(0,e^{s}),\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The first statement then follows by taking direct sums. The second statement follows immediately by the fact that

\left(U_{\mathcal{K}}\right)_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=S_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

(cf. Theorem
2.17
) and that
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}
is a strictly positive energy representation.
∎

Corollary 6.34
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and let
({\tt V},U)
be a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a unitary operator
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
such that

\psi({\tt V})={\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},\qquad\psi\circ J_{\tt V}\circ\psi^{-1}=\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi\circ U_{t}\circ\psi^{-1}=S_{t}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(cf. Definition
4.14
, Theorem
2.17
).

Proof.

By Proposition
6.26
there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a unitary operator
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
such that

\psi\circ J_{\tt V}\circ\psi^{-1}=\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,-1)=\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi\circ U_{t}\circ\psi^{-1}=\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(t,1)=S_{t}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

and

\psi\circ\Delta_{\tt V}^{-\frac{is}{2\pi}}\circ\psi^{-1}=\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(0,e^{s})\quad\forall s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

This, by Proposition
6.33
, implies that
\psi({\tt V})
and
{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}
are both standard subspaces in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
with

J_{\psi({\tt V})}=J_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{\psi({\tt V})}=\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}

and therefore, we have

\psi({\tt V})=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits\left(J_{\psi({\tt V})}\Delta_{\psi({\tt V})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits\left(J_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)={\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}.\qed

This corollary provides a normal form for non-degenerate standard pairs. The goal of this section is to provide similar normal form theorems for pairs
(V,U)
of a standard subspace
{\tt V}
and a unitary one-parameter group
U
on
\mathcal{H}
with
U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, but without the additional assumption that the infinitesimal generator of
U
is positive. We want to replace this assumption by the weaker assumption that – as it was the case for non-degenerate standard pairs by Corollary
6.34
– there exists an isomorphism between
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. This condition, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
), is equivalent to
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
being a real ROPG. This motivates the following definition:

Definition 6.35
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space,
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
a standard subspace, and
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
a strongly continuous one-parameter group.

(a)

We call
({\tt V},U)
a regular pair, if
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is a real ROPG.

(b)

We call
({\tt V},U)
a reflection positive regular pair, if
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
is a real reflection positive ROPG.

Remark 6.36
.

It follows directly from the definition that every reflection positive regular pair
({\tt V},U)
is, in particular, a regular pair.

First, we prove that both of these conditions are fulfilled if
({\tt V},U)
is a non-degenerate standard pair to show that we are, in fact, dealing with a generalization of non-degenerate standard pairs. For this, we need the following proposition:

Proposition 6.37
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a standard subspace. Then
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is a maximal real RPHS.

Proof.

Given a standard subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, by Proposition
6.12
, for every
v\in{\tt V}\setminus\{0\}
, one has

J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}v=v

and therefore
\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}v\neq 0
, so

\left\langle v\middle|J_{\tt V}v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|J_{\tt V}\big(J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|\Delta_{\tt V}^{\frac{1}{2}}v\right\rangle>0,

hence the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is a RPHS. By the same argument, using that
J_{\tt V}=J_{{\tt V}^{\prime}}
by Lemma
6.14
, we have that

\left\langle v\middle|J_{\tt V}v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|J_{{\tt V}^{\prime}}v\right\rangle>0

for every
v\in{\tt V}^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
. This yields

\left\langle iv\middle|J_{\tt V}iv\right\rangle=\left\langle iv\middle|-iJ_{\tt V}v\right\rangle=-\left\langle v\middle|J_{\tt V}v\right\rangle<0

for every
v\in{\tt V}^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
. Since

i{\tt V}^{\prime}={\tt V}^{\perp_{\mathbb{R}}},

this yields that

\left\langle v\middle|J_{\tt V}v\right\rangle<0\quad\forall v\in{\tt V}^{\perp_{\mathbb{R}}}.

We now assume that
w\in\mathcal{H}
such that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V}+{\mathbb{R}}w,J_{\tt V})
is a real RPHS. Then
{\tt V}+{\mathbb{R}}w={\tt V}+{\mathbb{R}}P_{{\tt V}^{\perp_{\mathbb{R}}}}w
, so we can assume that
w\in{\tt V}^{\perp_{\mathbb{R}}}
, which by the above implies that
w=0
, so
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is a maximal RPHS.
∎

Corollary 6.38
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space,
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
a standard subspace, and
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
a strongly continuous one-parameter group. If
({\tt V},U)
is a non-degenerate standard pair, then
({\tt V},U)
is a reflection positive regular pair.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.37
the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is a RPHS and by Borchers’ Theorem (Theorem
6.32
) we have
J_{\tt V}\circ U_{t}=U_{-t}\circ J_{\tt V}
. Together with the assumption

U_{t}{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V},\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

this implies that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
is a reflection positive unitary one-parameter group. Further, by Corollary
6.34
there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that the triple
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
, which is a real ROPG by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
). This implies that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
is a reflection positive ROPG.
∎

By the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
) we know that, for every regular pair
({\tt V},U)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, the ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is equivalent to
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. We now want to see which ROPGs
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
correspond to the multiplicity-free case
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
. For this, we make the following definition:

Definition 6.39
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and let
U:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
be a strongly continuous one-parameter group. We say that
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic, if there exists
v\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}U({\mathbb{R}})v}=\mathcal{H}.

Proposition 6.310
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a regular pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the multiplicity space of
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic.

Proof.

If the multiplicity space of
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}
, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
), we can without loss of generality assume that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)=(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\sharp},S)
. Then, for the function

F(z)\coloneqq\frac{iz}{(z+i)^{2}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

by Lemma
A.27
, Lemma
A.26
and Theorem
A.22
, we have
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\sharp}
. This, by Corollary
A.24
, yields

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})F}

and therefore
S
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic.

Now, for the converse, we assume that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is a real ROPG such that
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic. Then, by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
), we can without loss of generality assume that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)=(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
for some real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
. The
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclicity then implies that there exists
F\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
such that

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})F}.

This in particular implies that, for
v\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{0\}
, setting
F_{v}\coloneqq\left\langle v\middle|F(\cdot)\right\rangle\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
, we have

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})F_{v}}\otimes v=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})P_{{\mathbb{C}}v}F}=P_{{\mathbb{C}}v}\,\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})F}=P_{{\mathbb{C}}v}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\otimes v

and therefore

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})F_{v}}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},

so, by Wiener’s Tauberian Theorem (
[
Wi32
, Thm. I]
), we have

0\neq F_{v}(x)=\left\langle v\middle|F(x)\right\rangle

for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We now define the function
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
by

f(x)=\left\lVert F(x)\right\rVert,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then also
f(x)\neq 0
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, so, by Wiener’s Tauberian Theorem (
[
Wi32
, Thm. I]
), we have

\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})f}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}.

We now consider the following densely defined linear operator

\varphi:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\supseteq L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\cdot F\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},\quad g\cdot F\mapsto g\cdot f.

For
g\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}
we have

\left\lVert\varphi(g\cdot F)\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert g\cdot f\right\rVert^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}\cdot|f(x)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}\cdot\left\lVert F(x)\right\rVert^{2}\,dx=\left\lVert g\cdot F\right\rVert^{2},

so
\varphi
is isometric, and therefore we can extend it to a unitary operator

\widetilde{\varphi}:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}\to\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}S({\mathbb{R}})f}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}.

By construction, we have

\varphi\circ S_{t}=S_{t}\circ\varphi\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

and therefore also

\widetilde{\varphi}\circ S_{t}=S_{t}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

So, setting

\mathcal{E}_{+}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp}),

the operator
\widetilde{\varphi}
is an isomorphism between the ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)=(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},S)
and the ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},\mathcal{E}_{+},S)
. By Theorem
2.36
the latter is equivalent to the real ROPG
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})^{\sharp},H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})^{\sharp},S)
. Since, by Theorem
2.26
, the multiplicity space is unique up to isomorphism, this yields that
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

We now summarize our results in the following three theorems that give a normal form for regular pairs, reflection positive regular pairs, and non-degenerate standard pairs respectively:

Theorem 6.311
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a regular pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the following hold:

(a)

There exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that the real ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
is equivalent to
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
.

(b)

Denoting by
\varphi
the isomorphism between
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
and setting

\theta\coloneqq\varphi\circ J_{\tt V}\circ\varphi^{-1}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}),

the triple
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},\theta)
is a maximal RPHS.

(c)

One has
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic.

Proof.

Statement (a) follows immediately by the Lax–Phillips Theorem (Theorem
2.17
), (b) follows by Proposition
6.37
and (c) by Proposition
6.310
.
∎

Theorem 6.312
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a reflection positive regular pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the following hold:

(a)

There exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\flat}
with
h=h^{\sharp}
such that the real reflection positive ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J)
is equivalent to
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S,\theta_{h})
.

(b)

The triple
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},\theta_{h})
is a maximal RPHS.

(c)

One has
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic. In this case there exists an – up to multiplication with a positive constant – unique measure
{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}([0,\infty])\setminus\{0\}}
such that
h=h_{\nu}
.

Proof.

Statement (a) follows immediately by Theorem
4.111
and (b) follows by Proposition
6.37
. We now prove (c). By Proposition
6.310
we have that
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic. By (b) we have that
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},\theta_{h})
is a maximal real RPHS, which by Corollary
4.114
is equivalent to
(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),S)
being a maximal complex RPHS. By Theorem
4.329
, this, in turn, is equivalent to the existence of a measure
{\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}([0,\infty])\setminus\{0\}}
such that
h=h_{\nu}
. The uniqueness assertion about this measure then follows by Corollary
4.44
.
∎

Theorem 6.313
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the following hold:

(a)

There exists a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
such that the real reflection positive ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J)
is equivalent to
