(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S,\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits})
.

(b)

The triple
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits})
is a maximal RPHS.

(c)

One has
\mathcal{K}={\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
U
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-cyclic. In this case one has
\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits}=\theta_{\nu}
for the measure
\nu\in\mathcal{BM}^{\mathrm{fin}}([0,\infty])\setminus\{0\}
given by

d\nu(\lambda)=\frac{2}{1+\lambda^{2}},\quad\lambda\in[0,\infty].

Proof.

Statement (a) follows immediately by Corollary
6.34
, (b) follows by Proposition
6.37
and (c) by Proposition
6.310
and Example
5.37
.
∎

The results of these three theorems are presented in the following table:

M
({\tt V},U)
M

regular pair

\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize\bf reflection positive}\\

\text{\normalsize Example~\ref{ex:Lebesgue})}\end{subarray}

6.4
Extending the Longo–Witten Theorem beyond standard pairs

In
[
LW11
]
, given a non-degenerate standard pair
({\tt V},U)
on a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, the authors consider the semigroup

\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)\coloneqq\{A\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}):A{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V},\,(\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}})\,U_{t}A=AU_{t}\}.

The authors give the following theorem characterizing this semigroup as the semigroup of symmetric inner functions (cf. Definition
A.31
):

Theorem 6.41
.

(Longo-Witten)
(
[
LW11
, Thm. 2.6]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a non-degenerate standard pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Further, let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of the real reflection positive ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
and let
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
be an isomorphism between
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U,J_{\tt V})
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S,\theta_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits})
. Then

\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)=\psi^{-1}\circ M(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp})\circ\psi.

The goal of this section is to provide a generalization of this theorem from non-degenerate standard pairs to regular pairs. So, we make the following definition:

Definition 6.42
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a regular pair. We set

??\coloneqq\{A\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}):A{\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V},\,(\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}})\,U_{t}A=AU_{t}\}.

Theorem 6.43
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and
({\tt V},U)
be a regular pair on
\mathcal{H}
. Further, let
\mathcal{K}
be the multiplicity space of the real ROPG
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
and let
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
be an isomorphism between
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
. Then, considering the complex structure
I
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
defined by

If=\psi(i\psi^{-1}f),

there exists a function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp}
such that

I=M_{h}

and one has

\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)=\psi^{-1}\circ\left[M(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp})\cap\{M_{h}\}^{\prime}\right]\circ\psi.

Further, in the case
\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{K}<\infty
, let
\left(W_{t}\right)_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)
be a strongly continuous one-parameter semigroup. Then, setting

F\coloneqq\psi\circ\partial W\circ\psi^{-1}\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}

(cf. Theorem
A.44
) and extending
W
to a unitary one-parameter group
\widetilde{W}:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
, we have that
({\tt V},\widetilde{W})
is a regular pair, if and only if one of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(a)

{\rm Spec}(F(z))\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.

(b)

\ker(x\textbf{1}-M_{F_{*}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=\{0\}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(c)

{\mathbb{R}}\setminus D(F,x)
has non-zero Lebesgue measure for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(d)

D(F,x)
is a Lebesgue zero-set for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(e)

D(F,M)
is a Lebesgue zero-set for all Lebesgue zero-sets
M\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
.

(cf. Definition
3.16
for the definition of
D(F,x)
and
D(F,M)
). In this case, the multiplicity space
\mathcal{M}
of
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},\widetilde{W})
is given by

\mathcal{M}\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{R}}^{\deg F}&\text{if }F\in\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})\\
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{R}})&\text{if }F\notin\mathrm{Rat}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}).\end{cases}

(cf. Definition
3.322
).

Proof.

By Theorem
6.311
, we can, without loss of generality, assume that
(\mathcal{H},{\tt V},U)=(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}},S)
. Since we assumed
U
to be unitary, i.e. complex linear, we have
S_{t}I=IS_{t}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This, by Proposition
2.19
, implies that
I=M_{h}
for some function
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))
. That
h\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp}
then follows by

M_{h}^{*}=I^{*}=I^{-1}=M_{h}^{-1},

using that
I
is a complex structure and the fact that

M_{h}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}=I\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}.

To calculate the semigroup
\mathcal{E}({\tt V},U)
, we notice that, by Proposition
2.19
, the commutation relation
S_{t}A=AS_{t}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, is equivalent to
A\in M(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}})))
. Then the condition
A{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}\subseteq{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}
, by Proposition
A.113
, is equivalent to
A\in M(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp})
and then finally
A\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
is equivalent to
A\in M(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp})\cap\{M_{h}\}^{\prime}
.

The statement about the regularity of the pair
({\tt V},\widetilde{W})
follows by Theorem
3.17
and Theorem
3.110
. In this case, we get the formula for the multiplicity space
\mathcal{M}
by Theorem
3.328
.
∎

Remark 6.44
.

If
({\tt V},U)
is a non-degenerate standard pair, by Example
6.25
and Corollary
6.34
, we have
I=M_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits\cdot\textbf{1}}
and therefore

M(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp})\cap\{M_{i\cdot\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits\cdot\textbf{1}}\}^{\prime}=M(\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}}))^{\sharp}).

So, in this case, Theorem
6.43
specializes to Theorem
6.41
.

Appendix A
Holomorphic functions on the upper half-plane

A.1
Hardy spaces

In this section, we will introduce the concept of Hardy spaces and collect some basic facts about them. We will restrict ourselves mostly to Hardy spaces on the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, but everything can analogously also be done for the lower half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
. We start with the definition of a Hardy space:

Definition A.11
.

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{p}}\coloneqq\sup_{y>0}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|f(x+iy)\right|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{for }1\leq p<\infty

and

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}\coloneqq\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left|f(z)\right|.

Now, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we define the
Hardy space

??
by

H^{p}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{p}}<\infty\}.

Theorem A.12
.

(cf.
[
RR94
, Cor. 5.24, Sec. 5.29]
)

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the Hardy space
H^{p}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
is a Banach space. Further,
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
becomes a Hilbert space when endowed with the scalar product

\left\langle f\middle|g\right\rangle_{H^{2}}\coloneqq\lim_{y\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{f(x+iy)}g(x+iy)dx,\quad f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Theorem A.13
.

(cf.
[
RR94
, Cor. 5.17, Thm. 5.19, Thm. 5.23, Sec. 5.29]
)

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, for every
f\in H^{p}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, the non-tangential limit

f_{*}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}f(x+i\varepsilon)

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and defines a function
f_{*}\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with
\left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{p}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{p}}
. In particular, for the scalar product on
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, one has

\left\langle f\middle|g\right\rangle_{H^{2}}=\left\langle f_{*}\middle|g_{*}\right\rangle\quad\forall f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

This theorem shows that we have an isometric embedding

\iota_{p}:H^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)\to L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right),\quad f\mapsto f_{*}.

From now on, we will identify
H^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
with
\iota_{p}\left(H^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)\right)\subseteq L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}\right)
and we will write
f(x)
instead of
f_{*}(x)
for
f\in H^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorem A.14
.

(cf.
[
RR94
, Cor. 5.17]
,
[
RR94
, Sec. 5.14(v)]
)

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Then, for every
{f\in H^{p}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\setminus\{0\}}
, one has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{|\log|f(x)||}{1+x^{2}}\,dx<\infty,

so especially

f(x)\neq 0

for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

After considering Hardy spaces in general, we will now focus on the Hardy space
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
as a Hilbert space is an example of a so-called reproducing kernel Hilbert space, defined as follows:

Definition A.15
.

Let
X
be an arbitrary set and
H
be a Hilbert space that is a subset of the complex-valued functions on
X
. We say that
H
is a
reproducing kernel Hilbert space
if the linear functionals

\mathop{{\rm ev}}\nolimits_{x}:H\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad f\mapsto f\left(x\right)

are continuous for every
x\in X
. Then, by the Riesz Representation Theorem, for every
x\in X
, there exists a function
Q_{x}\in H
such that

f\left(x\right)=\mathop{{\rm ev}}\nolimits_{x}\left(f\right)=\left\langle Q_{x}\middle|f\right\rangle.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
H
be a reproducing kernel Hilbert space. Then

\overline{\mathrm{span}\left\{Q_{x}:x\in X\right\}}=H.

Proof.

Let
f\in\left\{Q_{x}:x\in X\right\}^{\perp}
. Then for every
x\in X
we have

0=\left\langle Q_{x}\middle|f\right\rangle=f\left(x\right)

and therefore
f=0
.
∎

Proposition A.17
.

(cf.
[
ANS22
, Ex. C.2]
)

The Hilbert space
H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, and its reproducing kernel functions are given by the Szegö kernel

??:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad z\mapsto\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{i}{z-\overline{w}},\qquad w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Further, for
w\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\left\lVert Q_{w}\right\rVert_{2}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\mathrm{Im}(w)}}.

Proof.

Obviously,
Q_{w}
is holomorphic and we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert Q_{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|Q_{w}(x+iy)\right|^{2}dx=\sup_{y>0}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{(x-\mathrm{Re}(w))^{2}+(y+\mathrm{Im}(w))^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\sup_{y>0}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\pi}{y+\mathrm{Im}(w)}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\pi}{\mathrm{Im}(w)}=\frac{1}{4\pi\mathrm{Im}(w)}<\infty,

so
Q_{w}\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
with

\left\lVert Q_{w}\right\rVert_{2}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\mathrm{Im}(w)}}.

Further, by Cauchy’s Integral Formula, we have

\left\langle Q_{w}\middle|f\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{1}{p-w}f\left(p\right)dp=f\left(w\right),

which shows, that
Q_{w}
is the reproducing kernel function.
∎

Corollary A.18
.

Let
f\in H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
and
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq H^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\right)
such that
\left\lVert f_{n}-f\right\rVert_{2}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. Then, for every
\beta>0
, the sequence
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges uniformly to
f
on the set

I_{\beta}\coloneqq\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Im}\left(z\right)\geq\beta\right\}.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.17
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in I_{\beta}}\left|f_{n}\left(z\right)-f\left(z\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in I_{\beta}}\left|\left\langle Q_{z}\middle|f_{n}-f\right\rangle\right|\leq\sup_{z\in I_{\beta}}\left\lVert Q_{z}\right\rVert_{2}\cdot\left\lVert f_{n}-f\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in I_{\beta}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\mathrm{Im}(z)}}\cdot\left\lVert f_{n}-f\right\rVert_{2}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\beta}}\cdot\left\lVert f_{n}-f\right\rVert_{2}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0

and therefore the convergence is uniformly on
I_{\beta}
.
∎

After considering Hardy spaces of scalar-valued functions, we now want to introduce Hardy spaces with values in a Hilbert space or in the bounded operators of some Hilbert space respectively:

Definition A.19
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space.

(a)

For an open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{C}}
we write
\mathcal{O}(U,\mathcal{H})
for the set of holomorphic functions on
U
with values in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}(U,B(\mathcal{H}))
for the set of holomorphic functions on
U
with values in
B(\mathcal{H})
.

(b)

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{2}}\coloneqq\sup_{y>0}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert f(x+iy)\right\rVert^{2}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and define

??\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{2}}<\infty\}.

(c)

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}\coloneqq\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left\lVert f(z)\right\rVert

and define

??\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H})):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}<\infty\}.

Remark A.110
.

As before in the scalar-valued case,
H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
is a Banach space, and
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
becomes a Hilbert space when endowed with the scalar product

\left\langle f\middle|g\right\rangle_{H^{2}}\coloneqq\lim_{y\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\langle f(x+iy)\middle|g(x+iy)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,dx,\quad f,g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})

denoting by
\left\langle\cdot\middle|\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
the scalar product on
\mathcal{H}
.

By Theorem
A.13
, for every
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
and
v\in\mathcal{H}
, the limit

f_{v}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left\langle v\middle|f(x+i\varepsilon)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Analogously, for every
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
and every trace-class operator
T\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, the limit

f_{T}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathrm{Tr}(Tf(x+i\varepsilon))

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This shows that, by taking weak limits, we can identify
H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
with a subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{H})
and
H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
with a subspace of
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
respectively.

Proposition A.111
.

Let
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
with
f(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
f
is constant.

Proof.

Since
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
\exp:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
maps bounded sets to bounded sets, we have

g_{+}\coloneqq e^{if}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\quad\text{and}\quad g_{-}\coloneqq e^{-if}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})

with

|g_{+}(x)|=|g_{-}(x)|=1

for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This implies that

|g_{+}(z)|\leq\left\lVert g_{+}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1\quad\text{and}\quad|g_{-}(z)|\leq\left\lVert g_{-}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1\qquad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This yields

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(z))\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(z))\leq 0\qquad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and therefore
f(z)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for every
z\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This, by the Cauchy–Riemann equations, implies that
f
is constant.
∎

Corollary A.112
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space. Then

H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{H}))=B(\mathcal{H})\cdot\textbf{1}.

Proof.

We first consider the scalar-valued case, so let
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
. Then
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})
, where the latter implies that
\overline{f}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. This yields

\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits(f),\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f)\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

which, by Proposition
A.111
implies that
\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits(f)
and
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f)
are constant and therefore also
f
is constant, so
f=c\cdot\textbf{1}
for some
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

We now consider the operator-valued case, so let
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Then, for every
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\left\langle v\middle|f(\cdot)w\right\rangle\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})={\mathbb{C}}\cdot\textbf{1}.

This implies that
f\in B(\mathcal{H})\cdot\textbf{1}
.
∎

Proposition A.113
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and let
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Then

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),

if and only if
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
.

Proof.

Let
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
and
g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. Then obviously
M_{f}g=f\cdot g\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. Further

\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{f}g\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert f\cdot g\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert f(x+iy)g(x+iy)\right\rVert^{2}\,dx\leq\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert f(x+iy)\right\rVert^{2}\cdot\left\lVert g(x+iy)\right\rVert^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{y>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\left\lVert g(x+iy)\right\rVert^{2}\,dx=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{2}^{2},

so
M_{f}g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
. This implies that

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Now, for the converse, we first consider the scalar-valued case. So let
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

This, in particular, implies

f\cdot Q_{i}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Therefore, by
[
RR94
, Thm. 5.19(i)]
, we have

0=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(x)\cdot Q_{i}(x)}{x-\overline{z}}\,dx=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(x)}{(x-\overline{z})(x+i)}\,dx\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

which, by
[
Ma09
, Sec. 3.1]
implies that
f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
.

We now consider the operator-valued case, so let
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
with

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

Then, for every
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
, defining

f_{v,w}\coloneqq\left\langle v\middle|f(\cdot)w\right\rangle\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

we get

M_{f_{v,w}}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\left\langle v\middle|M_{f}(H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\otimes w)\right\rangle\subseteq\left\langle v\middle|M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right\rangle\subseteq\left\langle v\middle|H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\right\rangle=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

so

f_{v,w}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

This means that, for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we can define the map

F_{z}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad(v,w)\mapsto f_{v,w}(z),

which is sesquilinear by construction. Further, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

|F_{z}(v,w)|=|f_{v,w}(z)|\leq\left\lVert f_{v,w}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\left\langle v\middle|f(\cdot)w\right\rangle\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\left\lVert v\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert w\right\rVert,\quad\forall v,w\in\mathcal{H},

so there exists
A(z)\in B(\mathcal{H})
with
\left\lVert A(z)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}
such that

F_{z}(v,w)=\left\langle v\middle|A(z)w\right\rangle,\quad\forall v,w\in\mathcal{H}.

Then

A:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to B(\mathcal{H}),\quad z\mapsto A(z)

is a bounded function for which all the functions

\left\langle v\middle|A(\cdot)w\right\rangle=f_{v,w},\quad v,w\in\mathcal{H}

are holomorphic. This, by
[
Ne00
, Cor. A.III.5]
, implies that
A
is holomorphic and therefore
A\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
. Now, by construction, for every
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
and almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\left\langle v\middle|A(x)w\right\rangle=f_{v,w}(x)=\left\langle v\middle|f(x)w\right\rangle,

which implies that
f=A\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
.
∎

Corollary A.114
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space and let
f,g\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},B(\mathcal{H}))
such that
f(x),g(x)\in U(\mathcal{H})
for almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, one has

M_{f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=M_{g}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}),

if and only if there exists
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
such that

f=g\cdot u.

Proof.

By multiplying with
M_{g^{-1}}
and
M_{f^{-1}}
respectively from the left, we get

M_{g^{-1}f}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})\quad\text{and}\quad M_{f^{-1}g}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})=H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H}).

By Proposition
A.113
this implies
g^{-1}f,f^{-1}g\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
. On the other hand

g^{-1}f=(f^{-1}g)^{-1}=(f^{-1}g)^{*}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{H})),

so, by Corollary
A.112
, we get

g^{-1}f\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))\cap H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-},B(\mathcal{H}))=B(\mathcal{H})\cdot\textbf{1}

and therefore, there exists
u\in B(\mathcal{H})
such that

f=g\cdot u.

That
u\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
follows from the fact that
f
and
g
have unitary values almost everywhere.
∎

We finish this section with some density theorems:

Proposition A.115
.

For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we consider the function
e_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

e_{t}(x)\coloneqq e^{itx},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then one has

\overline{{\rm span}\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{{\rm span}\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\}}=H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

where the closures are taken with respect to the weak-
*
-topology on
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e_{t}(x)\cdot f(x)\,dx=\widehat{f}(-t)\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then
\widehat{f}=0
and therefore
f=0
, since the Fourier transform is injective. This shows that

\overline{{\rm span}\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

The second statement follows by
[
ANS22
, Lem. B.6(b)]
.
∎

We need a similar statement to the one of Proposition
A.115
also for the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})
and its corresponding Hardy space on the unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
. For this, we first have to introduce Hardy spaces on the disc:

Definition A.116
.

(a)

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{D}})
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{p}}\coloneqq\sup_{0<r<1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|f(rz)\right|^{p}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{for }1\leq p<\infty

and

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}\coloneqq\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{D}}}\left|f(z)\right|.

Now, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we define the
Hardy space

H^{p}({\mathbb{D}})
by

??\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{D}}):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{p}}<\infty\}.

(b)

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\mathcal{H})
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{2}}\coloneqq\sup_{0<r<1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert f(rz)\right\rVert^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and define

H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{H})\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{H}):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{2}}<\infty\}.

(c)

For a function
f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{D}},B(\mathcal{H}))
, we set

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}\coloneqq\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{D}}}\left\lVert f(z)\right\rVert

and define

H^{\infty}({\mathbb{D}},B(\mathcal{H}))\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{D}},B(\mathcal{H})):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{\infty}}<\infty\}.

Remark A.117
.

Similar to the Hardy spaces on the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, by taking boundary values

f(z)\coloneqq\lim_{r\uparrow 1}f(rz),\quad z\in{\mathbb{T}},

one gets an isometric embedding of
H^{p}({\mathbb{D}})
into the Banach space
{L^{p}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})\coloneqq L^{p}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}},\mu)}
(cf.
[
RR94
, Cor. 4.26]
), where by
\mu
we denote the unique Haar measure on the group
{\mathbb{T}}
normalized by
\mu({\mathbb{T}})=1
. In the same fashion one also has embeddings of
H^{2}({\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{H})
into
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{H})
and of
H^{\infty}({\mathbb{D}},B(\mathcal{H}))
into
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},B(\mathcal{H}))
.

Proposition A.118
.

One has

\overline{{\rm span}\{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}^{n}:n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{{\rm span}\{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}^{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\}}=H^{\infty}({\mathbb{D}}),

where the closure is taken with respect to the weak-
*
-topology on
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}})
with

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{n}\cdot f(x)\,dx=\widehat{f}(-n)\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Then
\widehat{f}=0
and therefore
f=0
, since the Fourier transform is injective. This shows that

\overline{{\rm span}\{\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{T}}^{n}:n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}}).

The second statement follows by
[
ANS22
, Lem. B.6(a)]
.
∎

A.2
Outer functions

In this section, we introduce the concept of outer functions and present some basic facts about them, as well as about their interplay with Hardy spaces. We start with the definition of outer functions:

Definition A.21
.

(cf.
[
RR94
, Thm. 5.13]
)

(a)

For
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
and an almost everywhere defined function
K:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
with

??\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left|\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)\right|}{1+p^{2}}\,dp<\infty

we define the function
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\left(z\right)=C\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p-z}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

and call such a function an
outer function
.

(b)

We set
??\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(1,K)
.

(c)

We write
??
for the set of outer functions on
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and set

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}).

Theorem A.22
.

(cf.
[
RR94
, Thm. 5.13]
,
[
Ru86
, Thm. 17.16]
)

Let
C\in{\mathbb{T}}
and
K:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with
I(K)<\infty
. Then the limit

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)(x+i\varepsilon)

exists for almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and satisfies

|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)(x)|=K(x).

Further, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(C,K)\in H^{p}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, if and only if
K\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Theorem A.23
.

(
[
Ru86
, Thm. 17.23]
)

Let
F\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and consider, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the unitary operator
S_{t}\coloneqq M_{e_{t}}
with
e_{t}(x)\coloneqq e^{itx}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, if and only if

H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})=\overline{\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F}.

Corollary A.24
.

For every outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
one has

\overline{{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}})F}=\overline{\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\right)F}=\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})F}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Proof.

By Theorem
A.23
, we have

\overline{{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}})F}\supseteq\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\overline{{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}}_{>t})F}}=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}\overline{{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}}_{+})F}}=\overline{\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

using Theorem
2.16
in the last step.
Since

{\rm span}\,\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}={\rm span}\,\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\}+{\rm span}\,\{e_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\}\subseteq H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\subseteq L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

we get

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\subseteq\overline{{\rm span}\,S({\mathbb{R}})F}\subseteq\overline{\left(H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})+H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{-})\right)F}\subseteq\overline{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})F}\subseteq L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),

which implies the statement.
∎

We now show some basic properties of outer functions:

Lemma A.25
.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and let
f,g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
with
I(f)<\infty
and
I(g)<\infty
. Then

I(c\cdot f)<\infty,\quad I(f\cdot g)<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad I\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)<\infty

and

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(c\cdot f)=c\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(f),\quad\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(f\cdot g)=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(f)\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(g)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)=\frac{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(f)}{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(g)}.

Proof.

For
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
one has

\left|\log\left((c\cdot f)\left(p\right)\right)\right|=\left|\log\left(c\right)+\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)\right|\leq\left|\log\left(c\right)\right|+\left|\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)\right|

and

\left|\log\left((f\cdot g)\left(p\right)\right)\right|=\left|\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)+\log\left(g\left(p\right)\right)\right|\leq\left|\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)\right|+\left|\log\left(g\left(p\right)\right)\right|

and

\left|\log\left(\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)\left(p\right)\right)\right|=\left|\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)-\log\left(g\left(p\right)\right)\right|\leq\left|\log\left(f\left(p\right)\right)\right|+\left|\log\left(g\left(p\right)\right)\right|,

which immediately implies

I(c\cdot f)<\infty,\quad I(f\cdot g)<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad I\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)<\infty.

The second statement then follows immediately from the definition of outer functions.
∎

Lemma A.26
.

Let
K:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
be a symmetric function with
I(K)<\infty
.
Then

\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(-\overline{z})}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Further

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Proof.

For every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(-\overline{z})}

\displaystyle=\overline{\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p+\overline{z}}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{-1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p+z}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{-1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{-p+z}-\frac{-p}{1+(-p)^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(-p\right)\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p-z}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)=F_{K}(z).

Further, since
K
is symmetric, also
\sqrt{K}
is symmetric and therefore also

\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(-\overline{z})}=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(z)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This in particular yields

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)=\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(-\overline{i\lambda})}=\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)}\quad\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Then, by Lemma
A.25
, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)\cdot\overline{\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)}=\left|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits\big(\sqrt{K}\big)(i\lambda)\right|^{2}\geq 0,

so
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Further, for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)\in\exp({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, so

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Therefore, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)\right|=\left|\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p-i\lambda}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\mathrm{Re}\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p-i\lambda}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\mathrm{Re}\left(\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{p+i\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}-\frac{p}{1+p^{2}}\right]\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(K\left(p\right)\right)dp\right).\qed

Finally, we provide some concrete examples of outer functions:

Lemma A.27
.

Let
\sqrt{\cdot}:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}
denote the square root on
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
with
\sqrt{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, given the function
K:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
with

(a)

K(p)=|p|
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=-iz
.

(b)

K(p)=\frac{1}{|p|}
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{i}{z}
.

(c)

K(p)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|p|}}
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{i+1}{\sqrt{2z}}
.

(d)

K(p)=1+p^{2}
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=-(z+i)^{2}
.

(e)

K(p)=\frac{\left|p\right|}{1+p^{2}}
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{iz}{(z+i)^{2}}
.

(f)

K(p)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+p^{2}}}
, one has
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{i}{z+i}
.

Proof.

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\log\left(p\right)}{p^{2}+1}\,dp=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\log\left(e^{x}\right)}{e^{2x}+1}\cdot e^{x}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{e^{x}+e^{-x}}\,dx=0,

where the last equality follows from the fact that the integrand is skew-symmetric.

Then, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by Lemma
A.26
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left|p\right|dp\right)=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(p\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda p)^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(\lambda p\right)dp\right)=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\log\left(\lambda p\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\log\left(\lambda\right)dp+\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\log\left(p\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\log\left(\lambda\right)+0\right)=\lambda=-i(i\lambda).

This shows that the holomorphic functions
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)
and
z\mapsto-iz
coincide on
i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and are therefore equal.

(b)

By Lemma
A.25
and (a) we have

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{1}{-iz}=\frac{i}{z},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(c)

By Lemma
A.25
and (b), for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{i}{z},

so

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\pm\frac{\frac{i+1}{\sqrt{2}}}{\sqrt{z}}=\pm\frac{i+1}{\sqrt{2z}}.

The statement then follows by Lemma
A.26
since

+\frac{i+1}{\sqrt{2i}}=1\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

(d)

We define the function

g:(-1,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}},\quad\lambda\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\left[\log\left(1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}\right)-2\log\left(\lambda+1\right)\right]dp.

For
\lambda\neq 1
, we have

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\left[\frac{2\lambda p^{2}}{1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}}-\frac{2}{\lambda+1}\right]dp

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda^{2}-1}\left[\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}-\frac{1}{1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}}\right]-\frac{2}{\lambda+1}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\,dp

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda^{2}-1}-\frac{2}{\lambda+1}\right]\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\,dp-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}}\,dp

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\,dp-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{1+p^{2}}\,dp=0.

Further

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(1)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\left[\frac{2p^{2}}{1+p^{2}}-\frac{2}{1+1}\right]dp=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{p^{2}-1}{(p^{2}+1)^{2}}\,dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{2x}-1}{(e^{2x}+1)^{2}}\cdot e^{x}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{x}-e^{-x}}{(e^{x}+e^{-x})^{2}}\,dx=0,

where the last equality follows from the fact that the integrand is skew-symmetric. This shows that
g^{\prime}\equiv 0
and therefore
g
is constant. Since

\displaystyle g(0)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\left[\log\left(1+0\right)-2\log\left(0+1\right)\right]dp=0

this implies that
g\equiv 0
. This yields that, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we have

0=g(\lambda)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\left[\log\left(1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}\right)-2\log\left(\lambda+1\right)\right]dp,

so

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\log\left(1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}\right)dp=2\log\left(\lambda+1\right)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}dp=\pi\log\left(\lambda+1\right)

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(1+p^{2}\right)dp

\displaystyle=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(1+p^{2}\right)dp

\displaystyle=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda p)^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(1+(\lambda p)^{2}\right)dp

\displaystyle=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\frac{1}{p^{2}+1}\log\left(1+\lambda^{2}p^{2}\right)dp=2\pi\log\left(\lambda+1\right).

This, by Lemma
A.26
, yields

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lambda}{p^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\log\left(1+p^{2}\right)dp\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(2\log\left(\lambda+1\right)\right)=(\lambda+1)^{2}=-(i\lambda+i)^{2},\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

This shows that the holomorphic functions
\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)
and
z\mapsto-(z+i)^{2}
coincide on
i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and are therefore equal.

(e)

By (a), (d) and Lemma
A.25
we have

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{-iz}{-(z+i)^{2}}=\frac{iz}{(z+i)^{2}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(f)

By Lemma
A.25
and (d), for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\frac{1}{-(z+i)^{2}},

so

\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits(K)(z)=\pm\frac{i}{z+i}.

The statement then follows by Lemma
A.26
since

+\frac{i}{i+i}=\frac{1}{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.\qed

A.3
Inner functions

In this section, we will introduce the concept of inner functions and present some of their basic properties:

Definition A.31
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space.

(a)

We call a function
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
an
inner fuction
, if
\varphi(x)\in\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(b)

We write
??
for the set of inner functions.

(c)

We set
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\coloneqq\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B({\mathbb{C}}))
, i.e.
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
is the set of functions
\varphi\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
with
\left|\varphi(x)\right|=1
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

The following theorem links Hardy spaces, outer functions, and inner functions:

Theorem A.32
.

(
[
RR94
, Sec. 5.14(iv), Cor. 5.17, Lem. 5.21]
)

Let
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\setminus\{0\}
. Then there exists an inner function
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and an outer function
F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

f=\varphi\cdot F.

We now consider a special class of inner functions, namely the so-called Blaschke products:

Definition A.33
.

For
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we define the
Blaschke factor

??\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by

\varphi_{\omega}(z)\coloneqq\frac{z-\omega}{z-\overline{\omega}},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

A
finite Blaschke product
is a function
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
of the form

\varphi=c\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{N}\varphi_{\omega_{n}}

with
c\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{N}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.

Remark A.34
.

By construction the zeros of a finite Blaschke product
\varphi=c\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{N}\varphi_{\omega_{n}}
are precisely the numbers
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{N}
counted with multiplicity.

Proposition A.35
.

Let
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
with
f(\omega)=0
. Then there exists
g\in H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that

f=\varphi_{\omega}\cdot g.

Proof.

If
f=0
the statement follows by choosing
g=0
. Therefore, we can assume that
{f\neq 0}
. Then, by Theorem
A.32
, there exists an inner function
\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
and an outer function
{F\in\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})}
such that

f=\varphi\cdot F.

Since
F({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq\exp({\mathbb{C}})={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, we have
F(\omega)\neq 0
and therefore
\varphi(\omega)=0
. This implies that there exists a function
m\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
such that
\varphi(z)=(z-\omega)\cdot m(z)
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Then, defining
h\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
by
h(z)\coloneqq(z-\overline{\omega})\cdot m(z)
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, we have

\varphi=\varphi_{\omega}\cdot h.

Since
h
is holomorphic and therefore continuous, for
\varepsilon\in(0,\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\omega))
, we have

\sup_{z\in U_{\leq\varepsilon}(\omega)}|h(z)|<\infty.

Further, for every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus U_{\leq\varepsilon}(\omega)
, one has

\displaystyle|h(z)|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{z-\overline{\omega}}{z-\omega}\cdot\varphi(z)\right|\leq\left|\frac{z-\overline{\omega}}{z-\omega}\right|\cdot\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left|1+\frac{\omega-\overline{\omega}}{z-\omega}\right|\cdot 1\leq 1+\frac{|\omega-\overline{\omega}|}{|z-\omega|}\leq 1+\frac{2\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\omega)}{\varepsilon},

so also

\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus U_{\leq\varepsilon}(\omega)}|h(z)|<\infty.

This shows that
h\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Then, setting

g\coloneqq h\cdot F\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\cdot\mathop{{\rm Out}}\nolimits^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\subseteq H^{2}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}),

we get

f=\varphi\cdot F=\varphi_{\omega}\cdot h\cdot F=\varphi_{\omega}\cdot g.\qed

A.4
Pick functions

After considering inner functions, in this section, we want to take a closer look at the infinitesimal generators of one-parameter semigroups

\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H})).

We will see that these infinitesimal generators are precisely operator-valued Pick functions, defined as follows:

Definition A.41
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space.

(a)

We call a function
{F\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))}
an
operator-valued Pick function
if

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))\coloneqq\frac{1}{2i}(F(z)-F(z)^{*})\geq 0\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

(b)

We write
??
for the set of operator-valued Pick functions.

(c)

We write
??
for the set of Pick functions
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
, for which the limit

??(x)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F(x+i\varepsilon)

exists for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and is self-adjoint. We say such a Pick function has self-adjoint boundary values.

(d)

We set

\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})\coloneqq\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B({\mathbb{C}}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B({\mathbb{C}}))_{\mathbb{R}}

and, in the latter case, we say these functions have real boundary values.

To better understand this definition of Pick functions, we first want to see some consequences of the condition
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))\geq 0
and what happens if
0
is contained in the spectrum of
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(z))
:

Proposition A.42
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and let
A\in B(\mathcal{H})
such that
{\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(A)\geq 0}
. Then the following hold:

(a)

{\rm Spec}(A)\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
.

(b)

If
x\in{\rm Spec}(A)\cap{\mathbb{R}}
and
v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
with
Av=xv
, then
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(A)v=0
and
A^{*}v=xv
.

Proof.

(a)

For every eigenvalue
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
of
F(z)
and every normalized eigenvector
v\in\mathcal{H}
to this eigenvalue
\lambda
, we have

\lambda=\left\langle v\middle|\lambda v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|Av\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|\frac{1}{2}(A+A^{*})v\right\rangle+i\left\langle v\middle|\frac{1}{2i}(A-A^{*})v\right\rangle\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}},

so
{\rm Spec}(A)\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}
.

(b)

Since
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

0=\left\langle v\middle|xv\right\rangle-\left\langle xv\middle|v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|Av\right\rangle-\left\langle Av\middle|v\right\rangle=\left\langle v\middle|Av\right\rangle-\left\langle v\middle|A^{*}v\right\rangle=2i\left\langle v\middle|\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(A)v\right\rangle.

Since
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(A)\geq 0
, this implies
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(A)v=0
and therefore

A^{*}v=Av=xv.\qed

Lemma A.43
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. If there exists
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
such that
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(f(z_{0}))=0
, then
f
is constant.

Proof.

Using the biholomorphic Cayley transform

h(z)\coloneqq\frac{z-i}{z+i}

with

h({\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{T}}\setminus\{1\}\quad\text{and}\quad h({\mathbb{C}}_{+})={\mathbb{D}},

we get

h(f(z_{0}))\in h({\mathbb{R}})\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}

and

h(f(z))\in h(\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}})\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Therefore

|h(f(z))|\leq 1=h(f(z_{0}))\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

This, by the Maximum modulus principle, implies that
h\circ f
and therefore also
f
is constant.
∎

We now present some results about Pick functions with self-adjoint/real boundary values:

Theorem A.44
.

(cf.
[
Sc20
, Prop. 3.4.6]
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))
be a strongly continuous one-parameter semigroup of inner functions. Then there exists a Pick function
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
such that

\varphi_{t}(z)=e^{itF(z)},\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Lemma A.45
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
{F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+},B(\mathcal{H}))_{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then, the function
F_{*}
is measurable.

Proof.

Let
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define

g_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}},\quad x\mapsto\left\langle v\middle|F\left(x+\frac{i}{n}\right)w\right\rangle.

Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the function
g_{n}
is continuous and therefore measurable. This implies that also the function

\left\langle v\middle|F_{*}(\cdot)w\right\rangle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}g_{n}

is measurable. Since
v,w\in\mathcal{H}
were chosen arbitrarily, this implies that
F_{*}
is measurable.
∎

Theorem A.46
.

(Nevanlinna Representation)
(cf.
[
RR94
, Thm. 5.3, Thm. 5.13(iii)]
,
[
Ge17
, Thm. 7]
)

Let
F\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
. Then
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})
, if and only if there exist
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
D\geq 0
and some Borel measure
\mu
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu(\lambda)}{1+\lambda^{2}}<\infty

such that

F(z)=C+Dz+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\lambda-z}-\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}}\,d\mu(\lambda)\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

In this case, one has
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
, if and only if
\mu
is singular with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\mu
is singular, a minimal support for it is given by

??\coloneqq\left\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(F(x+i\varepsilon))=+\infty\right\}.

Remark A.47
.

If one takes the measure
\mu
from this theorem and defines a measure
\widetilde{\nu}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
by

d\widetilde{\nu}(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{d\mu(\lambda)}{1+\lambda^{2}},

one gets that

F(z)=C+Dz+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1+\lambda z}{\lambda-z}\,d\widetilde{\nu}(\lambda)

for a finite measure
\widetilde{\nu}
on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Notice that

\lim_{\lambda\to\pm\infty}\frac{1+\lambda z}{\lambda-z}=z,

so, if we extend our measure
\widetilde{\nu}
to a measure
\nu
on
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\coloneqq{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
by
\nu(\{\infty\})\coloneqq D
, we get

F(z)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda z}{\lambda-z}\,d\nu(\lambda).

Then, setting

??\coloneqq\begin{cases}S_{\mu}&\text{if }D=0\\
S_{\mu}\cup\{\infty\}&\text{if }D>0,\end{cases}

in the case that
\mu
was singular, the set
S_{F}
is a minimal support for the measure
\nu
.

Given a Pick function
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
, we want to understand how the function
F_{*}
behaves on
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus S_{F}
. For this, we need the following definition:

Definition A.48
.

Let
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\coloneqq{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
be the one-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
. For
a,b\in\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
, we set

(a,b)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:a<x<b\}&\text{if }a,b\in{\mathbb{R}},a\leq b\\
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:b<x\}\cup\{\infty\}\cup\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:x<a\}&\text{if }a,b\in{\mathbb{R}},a>b\\
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:a<x\}&\text{if }a\in{\mathbb{R}},b=\infty\\
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:x<b\}&\text{if }b\in{\mathbb{R}},a=\infty\\
\emptyset&\text{if }a,b=\infty.\end{cases}

Lemma A.49
.

Let
A\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
be a closed subset. Then there exists a countable family
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)_{j\in J}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\times\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
such that
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus A=\bigsqcup_{j\in J}\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)
.

Proof.

The maximally connected subsets of
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus A
are of the form
(a,b)
for some
a,b\in\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Therefore

\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus A=\bigsqcup_{j\in J}\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)

for some family
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)_{j\in J}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\times\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
with
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
j\in J
. Since
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)\neq\emptyset
implies that
\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)\cap{\mathbb{Q}}\neq\emptyset
, for every
j\in J
, there exists
q_{j}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
q_{j}\in\left(a_{j},b_{j}\right)
. From this follows the existence of a surjective function
f:{\mathbb{Q}}\to J
, and therefore
J
is countable.
∎

Proposition A.410
.

Let
F\in\mathrm{Pick}({\mathbb{C}}_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Further, let
a,b\in\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
with
a\neq b
such that
(a,b)\cap S_{F}=\emptyset
and
a,b\in S_{F}
. Then the map

F_{*}\big|_{\left(a,b\right)}:(a,b)\to{\mathbb{R}}

is monotonically increasing, bijective, and continuously differentiable.

Proof.

By replacing
F
with
F\circ M
, where
M:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
is a suitable Möbius transformation, we can, without loss of generality, assume that
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
with

a<0<b.

Now, since
(a,b)\cap S_{F}=\emptyset
, by
[
Do74
, Lem. II.2]
, there exists a function
\widetilde{F}\in\mathcal{O}({\mathbb{C}}_{+}\cup(a,b)\cup{\mathbb{C}}_{-})
with

\widetilde{F}(z)=\overline{\widetilde{F}(\overline{z})}\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\cup(a,b)\cup{\mathbb{C}}_{-}

such that

\widetilde{F}\big|_{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}=F.

Since
\widetilde{F}
is holomorphic, we have that
F_{*}\big|_{(a,b)}=\widetilde{F}\big|_{a,b}
is continuously differentiable. Further, we have

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits\widetilde{F}(z)=\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits F(z)\geq 0\quad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

which implies that
\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits\widetilde{F}(z)\leq 0
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{-}
and therefore

\frac{d}{dy}\bigg|_{y=0}\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits\widetilde{F}(x+iy)\geq 0\quad\forall x\in(a,b).

This, by the Cauchy–Riemann equations, implies that

0\leq\frac{d}{dx}\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits\widetilde{F}(x)=\frac{d}{dx}F_{*}(x)\quad\forall x\in(a,b),

so
F_{*}\big|_{(a,b)}
is monotonically increasing.

Now we assume that
F_{*}\big|_{(a,b)}=\widetilde{F}\big|_{(a,b)}
is not injective, i.e. that there exist
c,d\in(a,b)
with
c<d
such that

\widetilde{F}(c)=\widetilde{F}(d).

Then by the monotony of
F_{*}\big|_{(a,b)}=\widetilde{F}\big|_{(a,b)}
, the function
\widetilde{F}
would be constant on
[c,d]
and therefore constant in contradiction to the assumption that
a,b\in S_{F}
. Therefore
F_{*}\big|_{\left(a,b\right)}
is injective.

To show that the function
F_{*}\big|_{\left(a,b\right)}
is surjective, due to its monotony, it suffices to show that

\lim_{x\downarrow a}F_{*}(x)=-\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{x\uparrow b}F_{*}(x)=+\infty.

For
x\in(a,b)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\lambda\in\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b)
, setting
m\coloneqq\max\{|a|,|b|\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1+\lambda(x+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(x+i\varepsilon)+\frac{1+(x+i\varepsilon)^{2}}{\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)}\right|\leq|(x+i\varepsilon)|+\frac{1+\left|x+i\varepsilon\right|^{2}}{|\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)|}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1+m^{2}}+\frac{2+m^{2}}{|\lambda-x|}\leq\sqrt{1+m^{2}}+\frac{2+m^{2}}{\min\{|x-a|,|x-b|\}}.

Therefore, writing

F(z)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda z}{\lambda-z}\,d\nu(\lambda),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},

with
\nu
defined as in Remark
A.47
, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we get

\displaystyle F_{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F(x+i\varepsilon)=C+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda(x+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b)}\frac{1+\lambda(x+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)}\,d\nu(\lambda)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b)}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\lambda(x+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(x+i\varepsilon)}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda).

For
x\in\left(0,b\right)
and
\lambda\in\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b+1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|x+\frac{1+x^{2}}{\lambda-x}\right|\leq|x|+\frac{1+x^{2}}{|\lambda-x|}\leq b+\frac{1+b^{2}}{\min\left\{|a|,1\right\}},

so, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)=\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}.\,d\nu(\lambda)

Since

\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\geq 0\quad\forall x\in(0,b),\lambda\geq b

and since for every
\lambda\geq b
the function

(0,b)\ni x\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}=\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda-x}-\lambda

is monotonically increasing, by the Monotone Convergence Theorem, we get

\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{[b,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)=\int_{[b,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\,d\nu(\lambda).

This yields

\displaystyle\lim_{x\uparrow b}F_{*}(x)

\displaystyle=C+\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)=C+\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)+\lim_{x\uparrow b}\int_{[b,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda x}{\lambda-x}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus(a,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\,d\nu(\lambda)+\int_{[b,b+1)}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\,d\nu(\lambda)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\,d\nu(\lambda).

If
\lim_{x\uparrow b}F_{*}(x)<\infty
, this would imply that
\left(\lambda\mapsto\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\right)\in L^{1}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},{\mathbb{C}};\nu)
and therefore also

\left(\lambda\mapsto\frac{1}{1+b^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}-b\right)=\frac{1}{\lambda-b}\right)\in L^{1}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},{\mathbb{C}};\nu).

Then, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1+\lambda(b+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(b+i\varepsilon)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(b+i\varepsilon)+\frac{1+\left(b+i\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{\lambda-(b+i\varepsilon)}\right|\leq\left|b+i\varepsilon\right|+\frac{1+\left|b+i\varepsilon\right|^{2}}{\left|\lambda-(b+i\varepsilon)\right|}\leq\sqrt{1+b^{2}}+\frac{2+b^{2}}{\left|\lambda-b\right|},

so, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we would get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}F(b+i\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=C+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda(b+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(b+i\varepsilon)}\,d\nu(\lambda)

\displaystyle=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\lambda(b+i\varepsilon)}{\lambda-(b+i\varepsilon)}\,d\nu(\lambda)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda b}{\lambda-b}\,d\nu(\lambda)=\lim_{x\uparrow b}F_{*}(x)<\infty

in contradiction to the assumption
b\in S_{F}
. Therefore

\lim_{x\uparrow b}F_{*}(x)=\infty.

Analogously one shows that

\displaystyle\lim_{x\downarrow a}F_{*}(x)=C+\int_{\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{1+\lambda a}{\lambda-a}\,d\nu(\lambda)

and sees that
\lim_{x\downarrow a}F_{*}(x)>-\infty
is in contradiction to the assumption
a\in S_{F}
, which shows that

\lim_{x\downarrow a}F_{*}(x)=-\infty.\qed

Appendix B
An approximation theorem

In this chapter, we will prove the existence of a limit appearing in the proof of Proposition
4.328
and calculate its value. Since this proof is very technical and lengthy, we decided to remove it from the proof of Proposition
4.328
and include it here as an appendix. We start with a definition:

Definition B1
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We define the following three functions:

\displaystyle??(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{x^{2}}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle??(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle??(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\cdot\left(\chi_{\left(-1,1\right)}(x)\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\chi_{\left(-\infty,-1\right)\cup\left(1,\infty\right)}(x)\right),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

Remark B2
.

(a)

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, one has

g_{n}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}\leq\frac{x^{2}}{n\cdot x^{2}\cdot 1}=\frac{1}{n},

so

\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(x)=0.

(b)

For every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
with
|x|\neq p
one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{p,n}(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(x)\cdot\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}\right)^{2}}=0.

The goal of this section is to show that for any measurable function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\left|\varphi(x)\right|\,dx<\infty

one has

\frac{\varphi(p)+\varphi(-p)}{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{p,n}(x)\,\varphi(x)\,dx

for almost every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We start by the following lemma:

Lemma B3
.

For any measurable function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\left|\varphi(x)\right|\,dx<\infty

and every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}\left(d_{p,n}(x)+\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\,dx=0.

Proof.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}<p
and
x\in\left(-p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
one has

\displaystyle\left|d_{p,n}(x)+\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\right|=\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\cdot p^{2}+1\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)p^{2}+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)p^{2}+\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{x^{4}\left(1-p^{2}\right)+x^{2}\left(p^{2}+\frac{3}{n^{2}}p^{2}+p^{4}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{n^{4}}+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left|x^{2}\left(1-p^{2}\right)+\left(p^{2}+\frac{3}{n^{2}}p^{2}+p^{4}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\right)\right|+\left(\frac{1}{n^{4}}+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}{\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}-x^{2}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(p^{2}\left(1+p^{2}\right)+\left(4p^{2}+p^{4}+2\right)\right)+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)}{\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}-\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}-\frac{1}{n}+2p\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{x^{2}}{p^{2}n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{\left(0-\frac{p}{\sqrt{n}}+2p\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{x^{2}}{p^{2}n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{\frac{p^{2}}{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{x^{2}}{p^{2}n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)}{\frac{p^{2}}{n}}+\frac{x^{2}}{p^{2}n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{\frac{p^{2}}{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{x^{2}}{\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)}{p^{4}}+\frac{x^{2}}{1+n^{2}x^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{p^{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 1\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(2p^{4}+5p^{2}+2\right)}{p^{4}}+\frac{x^{2}}{1}\cdot\frac{\left(1+p^{2}\right)}{p^{4}}=x^{2}\cdot\frac{2p^{4}+6p^{2}+3}{p^{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(2p^{4}+6p^{2}+3\right)\left(1+p^{2}\right)^{2}}{p^{4}}.

By assumption, we have

\int_{(-p,p)}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(2p^{4}+6p^{2}+3\right)\left(1+p^{2}\right)^{2}}{p^{4}}\left|\varphi(x)\right|\,dx<\infty

and therefore, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}\left(d_{p,n}(x)+\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-p,p\right)}\chi_{\left(-p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}(x)\left(d_{p,n}(x)+\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\left(-p,p\right)}\lim_{n\to\infty}\chi_{\left(-p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}(x)\left(d_{p,n}(x)+\frac{g_{n}(x)}{p^{2}}\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\left(-p,p\right)}0\cdot\varphi(x)\,dx=0.\qed

Lemma B4
.

For any measurable function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\left|\varphi(x)\right|\,dx<\infty

and every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-\infty,-p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cup\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\infty\right)}\left(d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\right)\varphi(x)\,dx=0.

Proof.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}<p
and
x\in\left(-\infty,-p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cup\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\infty\right)
, one has

\displaystyle\left|d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\right|=g_{n}(p)\cdot\left|\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}+1\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\left|\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)+\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\left|\frac{x^{2}\left(\frac{5}{n^{2}}+3-p^{2}\right)-\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)+\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(\frac{5}{n^{2}}+3+p^{2}\right)+\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{x^{2}\left(8+p^{2}\right)+x^{2}\left(p^{2}+2\right)}{\left(x^{2}\cdot\frac{p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\left(1-\frac{p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2x^{2}\left(p^{2}+5\right)}{\left(x^{2}\left(1-\frac{p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2x^{2}\left(p^{2}+5\right)}{\left(x^{2}\cdot\frac{2p\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{nx^{2}}\cdot\frac{2\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}{\left(2p\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{nx^{2}}\cdot\frac{2\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{\left(2p\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{2p^{2}}=\frac{x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{2p^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}+1\right)^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{2p^{2}}=\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(1+p^{2}\right)^{2}\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{2p^{6}}.

By assumption, we have

\int_{(-\infty,-p)\cup(p,\infty)}\frac{x^{2}}{\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left(1+p^{2}\right)^{2}\left(p^{2}+5\right)\left(p+1\right)^{2}}{2p^{6}}\left|\varphi(x)\right|\,dx<\infty

and therefore, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-\infty,-p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cup\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\infty\right)}\left(d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(-\infty,-p\right)\cup\left(p,\infty\right)}\chi_{\left(-\infty,-p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cup\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\infty\right)}(x)\left(d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\left(-p,p\right)}\lim_{n\to\infty}\chi_{\left(-\infty,-p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cup\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\infty\right)}(x)\left(d_{p,n}(x)+g_{n}(x)\right)\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\left(-p,p\right)}0\cdot\varphi(x)\,dx=0.\qed

Definition B5
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We define the following functions:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)\coloneqq\frac{px}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle??(x)\coloneqq\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)\cdot\frac{2p^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}

Lemma B6
.

For every
p\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there exist sequences
\left(b_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
\left(B_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
with

\lim_{n\to\infty}b_{p,n}=1=\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{p,n}

such that for sufficiently large
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, one has

b_{p,n}\cdot\widetilde{d}_{p,n}(x)\leq d_{p,n}(x)\leq B_{p,n}\cdot\widetilde{d}_{p,n}(x)

for every
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
.

Proof.

We have

\frac{g_{n}(x)}{\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)}=\frac{\frac{x^{2}}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}}{\frac{px}{n\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}}=\frac{x\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}{p\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+x^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{x^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)},

so for
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
one has

\frac{g_{n}(x)}{\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)}\leq\frac{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}{p\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}\eqqcolon A_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1

and

\frac{g_{n}(x)}{\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)}\geq\frac{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+p^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}{p\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)}\eqqcolon a_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1.

Further, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d_{p,n}(x)}{\widetilde{d}_{p,n}(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{n}(x)}{\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)}\cdot\frac{\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}}{\frac{2p^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{n}(x)}{\widetilde{g}_{p,n}(x)}\cdot\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}{2p^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)}\cdot\left(\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\right)^{-1}.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\leq p
and
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
one has

\displaystyle\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)=-x^{4}+x^{2}\left(\frac{3}{n^{2}}+3+p^{2}\right)-\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{4}+\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{3}{n^{2}}+3+p^{2}\right)-\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\eqqcolon v_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}-p^{4}+p^{2}\left(3+p^{2}\right)-p^{2}=2p^{2}>0

and

\displaystyle\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\left(1-x^{2}\right)+2x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)=-x^{4}+x^{2}\left(\frac{3}{n^{2}}+3+p^{2}\right)-\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq-\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{4}+\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{3}{n^{2}}+3+p^{2}\right)-\left(p^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\eqqcolon V_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}-p^{4}+p^{2}\left(3+p^{2}\right)-p^{2}=2p^{2}>0

and

\displaystyle 2p^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}+1\right)\eqqcolon w_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}2p^{2}>0

and

\displaystyle\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(x^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\geq 1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq 1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}=1+\frac{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}}{p^{2}}\eqqcolon u_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1

and

\displaystyle\frac{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4x^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(x^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}\leq 1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}-p^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}+4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}-p^{2}\right)}{4p^{2}\frac{1}{n^{2}}}=1+\frac{\frac{1}{n}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}}{p^{2}}\eqqcolon U_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1.

Therefore, for sufficiently large
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
, one has

\frac{d_{p,n}(x)}{\widetilde{d}_{p,n}(x)}\leq A_{p,n}\cdot\frac{V_{p,n}}{w_{p,n}}\cdot u_{p,n}^{-1}\eqqcolon B_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1\cdot\frac{2p^{2}}{2p^{2}}\cdot 1=1

and

\frac{d_{p,n}(x)}{\widetilde{d}_{p,n}(x)}\geq a_{p,n}\cdot\frac{v_{p,n}}{w_{p,n}}\cdot U_{p,n}^{-1}\eqqcolon b_{p,n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1\cdot\frac{2p^{2}}{2p^{2}}\cdot 1=1.\qed

Definition B7
.

Let
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Then
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
is called a
Lebesgue point of
f
, if

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{(p-\varepsilon,p+\varepsilon)}|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx=0.

Lemma B8
.

Let
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}}
be a Lebesgue point of
f
. Further, let
\left(k_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence of non-negative functions with

\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)\,dx=1,

such that there exist constants
r,R>0
such that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, one has

\max\left\{k_{n}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),k_{n}\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right\}\leq R\sqrt{n}

and
k_{n}\in C^{1}\left(\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)
with

\displaystyle k_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p-\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\left|k_{n}^{\prime}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq rn^{2}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{n},p+\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle k_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 0

\displaystyle\forall x\in\left(p+\frac{1}{n},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Then

f(p)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)f(x)\,dx.

Proof.

For
\varepsilon>0
we set

c_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{(p-\varepsilon,p+\varepsilon)}|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx.

Then, since
p
is a Lebesgue point, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}c_{\varepsilon}=0
. We set

C_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in(0,\varepsilon]}c_{t}.

Then also
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}C_{\varepsilon}=0
. We now define the function

m_{n}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}rn-rn^{2}|x-p|&\text{if }x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{n},p+\frac{1}{n}\right)\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Then, for every
x\in\left(p,p+\frac{1}{n}\right)
, we have

k_{n}^{\prime}(x)+m_{n}^{\prime}(x)=k_{n}^{\prime}(x)-rn^{2}\leq rn^{2}-rn^{2}=0

and for every
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{n},p\right)
, we have

k_{n}^{\prime}(x)+m_{n}^{\prime}(x)=k_{n}^{\prime}(x)+rn^{2}\geq-rn^{2}+rn^{2}=0,

so
k_{n}^{\prime}(x)+m_{n}^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
for
x\in\left(p,p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
and
k_{n}^{\prime}(x)+m_{n}^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
for
x\in\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p\right)
.
This yields

\displaystyle\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(k_{n}(x)+m_{n}(x)\right)|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(k_{n}\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)-\int_{x}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\,dt\right)|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=k_{n}\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cdot\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\int_{x}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}-\left(k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\right)|f(x)-f(p)|\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\leq R\sqrt{n}\cdot 2\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\int_{p}^{t}-\left(k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\right)|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad=2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}-\left(k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\right)\int_{p}^{t}|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}-\left(k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\right)2\left(t-p\right)c_{t-p}\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+2C_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}-\left(k_{n}^{\prime}(t)+m_{n}^{\prime}(t)\right)\left(t-p\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad=2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+2C_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(\left[-\left(k_{n}(t)+m_{n}(t)\right)\left(t-p\right)\right]_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(k_{n}(t)+m_{n}(t)\right)\,dt\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+2C_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(-k_{n}\left(p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\int_{p}^{p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}k_{n}(t)\,dt+\frac{r}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2R\cdot c_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}+2C_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}\left(0+1+\frac{r}{2}\right)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0

and analogously

\int_{p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}^{p}\left(k_{n}(x)+m_{n}(x)\right)|f(x)-f(p)|\,dx\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)f(x)\,dx-f(p)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)\left(f(x)-f(p)\right)\,dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)\left|f(x)-f(p)\right|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}\left(k_{n}(x)+m_{n}(x)\right)\left|f(x)-f(p)\right|\,dx\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0,

so

f(p)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)}k_{n}(x)f(x)\,dx.\qed

Lemma B9
.

For every
